On the Direct Problem in Differential Galois Theory for the Classical Groups
Daniel Robertz, Matthias Seiß

Abstract
. Let G G be a classical group of Lie rank l l and let C C be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. For l l differential indeterminates 𝒗 = ( v 1 , … , v l ) \boldsymbol{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{l}) over C C we constructed in [ Seia ] a general Picard-Vessiot extension ℰ \mathcal{E} of the differential field C ​ ⟨ 𝒔 ⁡ ( 𝒗 ) ⟩ C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle having differential Galois group G ⁡ ( C ) G(C) . Here 𝒔 ⁡ ( 𝒗 ) = ( s 1 ​ ( 𝒗 ) , … , s l ​ ( 𝒗 ) ) \boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})=(s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}),\dots,s_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})) are certain differential polynomials in C ​ { 𝒗 } C\{\boldsymbol{v}\} which are differentially algebraically independent over C C . The linear differential equation defining ℰ \mathcal{E} is defined by the normal form matrix A G ​ ( 𝒔 ​ ( 𝒗 ) ) A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})) lying in the Lie algebra of G G . In the first part of this paper we analyze the structure of ℰ \mathcal{E} induced by the action of the standard parabolic subgroups of G ⁡ ( C ) G(C) on ℰ \mathcal{E} . In the second part we consider specializations A G ​ ( 𝒔 ⁡ ( 𝒗 ) ) → A G ​ ( 𝒔 ¯ ) A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))\to A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}) with 𝒔 ¯ ∈ C ​ ( z ) l \overline{\boldsymbol{s}}\in C(z)^{l} of the normal form matrix for G G of type A l A_{l} , B l B_{l} , C l C_{l} or G 2 \mathrm{G}_{2} (here l = 2 l=2 ). We show how one can combine the results of the first part with known algorithms for the computation of the differential Galois group and its Lie algebra to determine the differential Galois group of certain specialized equations ∂ ( 𝒚 ) = A G ​ ( 𝒔 ¯ ) ​ 𝒚 \partial(\boldsymbol{y})=A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})\boldsymbol{y} over C ⁡ ( z ) C(z) with C C a computable algebraically closed field of characteristic zero.



1.
Introduction

Differential Galois Theory

Differential Galois theory is a generalization of the well-known classical Galois theory for polynomial equations to linear ordinary differential equations.
We recall briefly that in the classical theory one considers a polynomial equation

p(x)\,:=\,x^{n}+a_{n-1}x^{n-1}+\dots+a_{1}x+a_{0}\,=\,0

with coefficients in some field
k
and that one wants to study the symmetries of the roots of this equation.
These symmetries are described by the group of all
k
-automorphisms of a splitting field for
p(x)=0
, i.e. a smallest
extension field of
k
such that
p(x)
splits into linear factors. This group of
k
-automorphism is called the Galois group.
In differential Galois theory we study instead the solutions of a linear differential equation

(1)

L(y)\,:=\,y^{(n)}+a_{n-1}y^{(n-1)}+\dots+a_{1}y^{\prime}+a_{0}y\,=\,0

with coefficients in some differential field
F
with algebraically closed field of constants
C
.
Here, the analogous object to the splitting field is the so-called Picard-Vessiot field, which is a certain differential field extension
E
of
F
containing a full set of solutions for the equation
L(y)=0
.
The differential algebraic relations of the solutions are now described by the properties of the group of all differential
F
-automorphisms of
E
, meaning the
F
-automorphisms of
E
which commute with the derivation of
F
.
While the classical Galois group has a representation as a permutation group, the differential Galois group acts by
C
-linear transformations on the full set of solutions and so has a representation as a linear algebraic group defined over
C
.
The direct problem is concerned with the computation of the differential Galois group for a given linear differential equation (
1
).
Even though some progress has been made in some particular cases, the general case is still very challenging, in particular effective computations. This paper makes a contribution to this problem based on recent progress for the case of generic equations.

State of the Art of the Direct Problem

The first algorithmic contribution to the direct problem in differential Galois theory (for a general introduction to the topic see
[
vdPS03
,
Mag94
]
) was given by J. Kovacic in 1986 in
[
Kov86
]
. Kovacic uses the classification of the algebraic subgroups of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(C)
to design an effective algorithm to compute the Liouvillian solutions of an order two homogeneous linear differential equation of type

L(y)\,:=\,y^{\prime\prime}+a_{0}y\,=\,0\qquad\text{with}\ a_{0}\in C(z)\,,

where
C
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero and
F:=C(z)
is the rational function field in
z
endowed with the usual derivation
\partial=\frac{d}{dz}
.
Knowing the Liouvillian solutions of the equation
L(y)=0
, one can deduce its differential Galois group
H\leq\mathrm{SL}_{2}(C)
and vice versa. M. F. Singer and F. Ulmer generalized this idea to order three differential equations in
[
SU93b
,
SU93a
,
SU97
]
.

For a homogeneous linear differential equation

L(y)\,:=\,y^{(n)}+a_{n-1}y^{(n-1)}+\dots+a_{1}y^{\prime}+a_{0}y\,=\,0\qquad\text{with}\ a_{i}\in C(z),

of order
n

whose corresponding first order system is completely reducible, i.e. a direct sum of irreducible systems,
E. Compoint and M. F. Singer showed in
[
CS99
]
how to compute the differential Galois group
H\leq\mathrm{GL}_{n}(C)
.
Their approach is based on E. Compoint’s result that if the differential Galois group is unimodular and reductive, then the ideal defining the Picard-Vessiot extension is generated by the invariants of the differential Galois group it contains.
Degree bounds for generating invariants of reductive groups give a degree bound for the generating polynomials of the ideal defining the Picard-Vessiot extension.
These polynomials can be computed from the differential equation without knowing the differential Galois group using the methods of
[
vHW97
]
.

An algorithm for the general case was given by E. Hrushovski in
[
Hru02
]
using model theory. His approach was elaborated on and improved by R. Feng in
[
Fen15
]
, by M. Sun in
[
Sun19
]
and by D. Rettstadt in his PhD thesis
[
Ret14
]
. An improvement for the degree bounds of the defining equations of the proto-Galois group was given by E. Amzallag,
A. Minchenko and G. Pogudin in
[
AMP22
]
.
But it is still very hard to actually compute the Galois group using E. Hrushovski’s algorithm.

Instead of computing the differential Galois group
H
of an order
n
equation one can focus on the computation of the Lie algebra
\Lie(H)
of
H
. This approach is based on the Kolchin-Kovacic reduction theorems appearing in
[
Kov69
,
Kov71
]
and
[
Kol73
,
Kol99
]
.
Roughly speaking they state that over an algebraic extension of the base field
C(z)
one can gauge transform the defining matrix
A
of the corresponding first order system into the Lie algebra of the differential Galois group.
Such a matrix is called a
reduced form
of
A
.
Computing the smallest Lie algebra containing a Wei-Norman decomposition (cf.
[
WN63
]
) of a reduced form determines
\Lie(H)
.
However, this approach only yields a full solution if the differential Galois group is connected.
An algorithm to compute a reduced form of a first order system is presented in the paper
[
DW22
]
by T. Dreyfus and J.-A. Weil. It is based on the works
[
CW04
,
Bar07
,
vdH07
]
and
[
BCWDV16
,
AMCW13
]
by
A. Aparicio-Monforte, E. Compoint, M. Barkatou, T. Cluzeau, L. Di Vizio, J.-A. Weil and J. van der Hoeven.

General Extension Fields for the Classical Groups

In this paper we consider the direct problem in differential Galois theory of a special family of linear differential equations with differential Galois group a subgroup
H
of a classical group
G
of type
A_{l}
,
B_{l}
,
C_{l}
or
\mathrm{G}_{2}
(here
l=2
).
We make essential use of the general differential equations and their general Picard-Vessiot extensions constructed in
[
Seia
,
Seib
]
, meaning that the general extension field
\mathcal{E}
has generators which depend on
l
differential indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{l})
over
C
, where
l
is the Lie rank of
G
. Since in this paper we consider specializations into the differential field
F
, we here perform our construction of
\mathcal{E}
over
F\supset C
as in
[
RS23
]
, meaning that
\boldsymbol{v}
are differential indeterminates over
F
.
The construction relies on the geometric structure of
G
expressed by a Chevalley basis of the Lie algebra
\Lie(G)
and the Bruhat decomposition of
G
.
The first one facilitates the construction of the
normal form matrix

A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
defining the differential equation

(2)

\partial(\boldsymbol{y})\,=\,A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))\,\boldsymbol{y}

for
\mathcal{E}
. The matrix
A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
depends on certain
l
differential polynomials

\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\,=\,(s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}),\dots,s_{l}(\boldsymbol{v}))\quad\text{with}\quad s_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})\in C\{\boldsymbol{v}\},

which are differentially algebraically independent over
F
.
In this framework we can construct a fundamental matrix
\mathcal{Y}
for (
2
) as a parametrization of the double coset in the Bruhat decomposition corresponding to the longest Weyl group element
\overline{w}
.
In other words, with a representative
n(\overline{w})
of
\overline{w}
in the normalizer of a maximal torus we let

(3)

\mathcal{Y}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})\,,

where

•

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})
is a parametrization of the product of all negative root groups by
\boldsymbol{v}
and
m-l
differential polynomials
\boldsymbol{f}=(f_{l+1},\dots,f_{m})
in
C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
with
m=|\Phi^{-}|
where
\Phi
denotes the root system of
G
,

•

\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})
is the product of torus elements parametrized by exponentials
\boldsymbol{\exp}=(\exp_{1},\dots,\exp_{l})
satisfying
\frac{\exp_{i}^{\prime}}{\exp_{i}}=g_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})
with
g_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathbb{Z}[\boldsymbol{v}]
homogeneous of degree one,

•

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})
is a parametrization of the product of all negative root groups by certain iterated integrals
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}=(\mathrm{int}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{int}_{m})
.

It is shown in
[
RS23
, Section 7]
that the field

\mathcal{E}\,=\,F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})

is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
with differential Galois group
G
.
Note that the whole construction depends on the differential indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}
. A summary of our construction and the main notation are presented in Part
I
.

The Contribution of the Paper

The goal of this paper is to present an algorithm to compute the differential Galois groups
H(C)\leq G(C)
of certain well-behaving specializations

\partial(\boldsymbol{y})\,=\,A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})\,\boldsymbol{y}

of the normal form matrix
A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
with

\sigma_{0}\colon F\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\}\to F,\quad\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\mapsto\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}\in F^{l},\quad F\,=\,C(z)\,.

This is achieved by combining the above two mentioned algorithms of E. Compoint and M. F. Singer respectively T. Dreyfus and J.-A. Weil with our construction of a general extension and in particular of our fundamental matrix
\mathcal{Y}
. We are going to elaborate on our approach.

The General Extension and Parabolic Subgroups

Before developing our algorithm for the specialized equation we analyze in Part
II
intermediate extensions of our general extension field
\mathcal{E}
over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
corresponding, according to the Fundamental Theorem of Differential Galois Theory, to parabolic subgroups of
G
.
A key ingredient here is the parametrized Bruhat decomposition of the general fundamental matrix
\mathcal{Y}
in (
3
),
which allows us to connect explicitly standard parabolic subgroups
P_{J}\leq G
for
J\subseteq\{1,\dots,l\}
, where
\Delta=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{l}\}
is a basis of the root system
\Phi
of
G
, and their Levi decompositions

P_{J}\,=\,L_{J}\ltimes R_{u}(P_{J})

with intermediate extensions. Here
R_{u}(P_{J})
denotes the unipotent radical and
L_{J}
the standard Levi group of
P_{J}
(cf. (
16
) on page
16
). The Galois action of
g\in G(C)
on the elements
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{f}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}
is induced by recomputing the Bruhat decomposition of

\mathcal{Y}\,g\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})\,g\,.

We establish a bijection between standard parabolic subgroups
P_{J}
and partitions
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext}\cup\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
of the differential indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}
, where
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
consists of those indeterminates which are fixed under the action of
P_{J}
.
Correspondingly we obtain partitions of the set of indices
I=\{1,\dots,l\}
of
\boldsymbol{v}
into
I=I^{\prime}\cup I^{\prime\prime}
, where the indices in
I^{\prime}
correspond to the indeterminates in
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext}
and the ones in
I^{\prime\prime}
to the indeterminates in
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
.
We show that the fixed field
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
for a parabolic subgroup
P_{J}
is generated as differential field by
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
over
F
, that is

\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}\,=\,F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\,.

We denote the negative roots of
\Phi^{-}
by
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{m}
. Let
\Psi
be the root subsystem of
\Phi
generated by all simple roots
\alpha_{i}
with
i\in J
.
Then
\Psi
is the root system of
L_{J}
and
\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
is the set of roots
\beta
whose corresponding root groups
U_{\beta}\leq U^{-}
generate the unipotent radical
R_{u}(P_{J})
.
Combining the structure of the root system with the induced action of
R_{u}(P_{J})
on the parameters we prove that the fixed field
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
of the unipotent radical is generated over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
as a differential field by

(4)

\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext},\,\boldsymbol{\exp},\,\mathrm{int}_{i}\quad\text{with}\ 1\leq i\leq m\ \text{such that}\ \beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}.

Moreover, the Fundamental Theorem of Differential Galois Theory implies that the differential Galois group of

\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
is isomorphic to the Levi group
L_{J}
.
Levi groups of
P_{J}
are maximal reductive subgroups justifying to call the elements in (
4
) the
parameters of the
reductive part
of
P_{J}
. In turn we call the integrals
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
the
parameters of the unipotent radical part
.

The intermediate extensions
\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
and

\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}/\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
and the decompositions
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\cup\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext}
are linked to
solutions of certain differential operators.
Denote by

L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\partial]

the
normal form operator
corresponding to the normal form matrix
A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
.
It turns out that for each indeterminate
v_{i}
there is an associated equation

(5)

L^{\det(i)}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),y)\,=\,0\,,\qquad i=1,\ldots,l\,,

with solution
\exp(\int b_{i}v_{i})
with
b_{i}\in C^{\times}
. Thus,
b_{i}v_{i}
is a solution of the Riccati equation
\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),y)=0
corresponding to (
5
).
The Riccati equations will help us later to compute for the specialized equation the specialized elements
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}
in
F
and so to determine
P_{J}
containing the Galois group. (This is a generalization of
Case 1
in Kovacic’s Algorithm.)
Moreover, each partition
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\cup\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext}
, and thus each standard parabolic subgroup, induces an irreducible factorization of the normal form operator

(6)

L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,=\,L_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\cdots L_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)

over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
.
We prove that the extension
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}/\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
with differential Galois group isomorphic to
L_{J}
is a Picard-Vessiot extension for the least common left multiple

(7)

\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\,:=\,\LCLM(L_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial),\dots,L_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial))

of these irreducible factors.
On the one hand, the basis elements

y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n^{I^{\prime\prime}}}\in\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}\quad\text{(fixed basis)}

of the solution space of the
least common left multiple in
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
are differential rational functions in the parameters of the reductive part.
On the other hand, since
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
is generated as a differential field by this basis over
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
, we can express the parameters of the reductive part as differential rational functions in these basis elements. We can determine differential rational functions

\mathrm{EXP}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z})
,
V^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
\mathrm{INT}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})
in
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}\langle\boldsymbol{Z}\rangle
for
i=1,\dots,l
and
j=1,\dots,m
with
\beta_{j}\in\Psi^{-}
such that

(8)

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{EXP}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}(y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n^{I^{\prime\prime}}})\,=\,\exp_{i},\quad V^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}(y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n^{I^{\prime\prime}}})\,=\,v_{i}\quad\text{and}\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{INT}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{j}(y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n^{I^{\prime\prime}}})\,=\,\mathrm{int}_{j}\,,\end{array}

where
\boldsymbol{Z}=(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n^{I^{\prime\prime}}})
are differential indeterminates over
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
.
The functions
\mathrm{EXP}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z})
,
V^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
\mathrm{INT}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})
are important, since they will allow us later to determine a specialization of the parameters of the reductive part from a basis of a solution space of the specialized least common left multiple.
In the last section of Part
II
we show that the fundamental matrix
\mathcal{Y}
factors into
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{\rm red}\,\mathcal{Y}_{\rm rad}
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\rm red}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\prod_{\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}}u_{\beta_{i}}(\mathrm{int}_{i})\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\rm rad}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}}u_{\beta_{i}}(y_{\beta_{i}})\,\in\,R_{u}(P_{J})(\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})})\,,

where
y_{\beta_{i}}
are polynomials in
C[\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]\setminus C[\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}]
.
Moreover, we prove that we can determine a reduction matrix
g_{1}\in G(\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}})
such that
g_{1}\mathcal{Y}
lies in the parabolic subgroup
P_{J}(\mathcal{E})
and
g_{1}\mathcal{Y}_{\rm red}
is contained in the Levi group
L_{J}(\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})})
.
In other words,
g_{1}\mathcal{Y}
admits the factorization

g_{1}\mathcal{Y}\,=\,(g_{1}\mathcal{Y}_{\rm red})\cdot\mathcal{Y}_{\rm rad}\,\in\,L_{J}(\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})})\cdot R_{u}(P_{J})(\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})})\,,

splitting
g_{1}\mathcal{Y}
into its reductive part and unipotent radical part.

The Three Extensions of
\sigma_{0}

The main part of our paper, that is Part
III
, employs the above results to compute the differential Galois group of the specialized normal form matrix
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
with

\sigma_{0}\colon F\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\}\to F,\quad\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\mapsto\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}\in F^{l},\quad F\,=\,C(z)\,.

We are going to extend
\sigma_{0}
in three steps to a specialization

(9)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma_{\rm PV}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]&\to&\overline{\mathcal{E}},\\[1.99997pt]
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})&\mapsto&(\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})\end{array}

for a Picard-Vessiot extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
of
F
for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
, where
D
is a certain multiplicatively closed subset. In each step
we add new algebraic relations between the parameters
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}
until we obtain the Picard-Vessiot extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
with fundamental matrix
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
being a specialization of
\mathcal{Y}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\rm red}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\rm PV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\rm red}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\rm inter}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{0}}

.
Overview of successive extensions of the specialization
\sigma_{0}

A Parabolic Bound for a Specialization

The first step of the extension of
\sigma_{0}
is supposed to map as many
v_{i}
to rational functions in
F
as possible, provided that a construction of a Picard-Vessiot extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
of
F
is still possible based on that choice.
More precisely, we compute with the known algorithms the rational solutions in
F
of the specialized Riccati equations
\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{i})=0
corresponding to the specialized associated equations
L^{\det(i)}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},y)=0
for
i=1,\dots,l
. Here we make the assumption on
\sigma_{0}
that these specializations are defined.
Using the differential Thomas decomposition (cf.
[
Rob14
]
) we construct a longest tuple
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}
of rational solutions of the various Riccati equations such that the differential ideal

S_{\mathrm{inter}}\,=\,\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})-\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}-\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}\rangle\lhd F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}

is proper.
Being a proper ideal guarantees that we are able to continue from here the construction of a Picard-Vessiot extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}/F
and the extended specialization (
9
).
We obtain the first extended specialization

\sigma_{\rm inter}\colon F\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\to F,\ (\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base})\mapsto(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\,.

We denote by
H
the differential Galois group of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}/F
, whose representation depends on the fixed choice of
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
as well as the subsequent choices made to complete the construction of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
.
The choice of
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}
induces a partition of the indices
I=I^{\prime}\cup I^{\prime\prime}
or equivalently a partition of the indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext}\cup\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
.
According to the bijection established in Part
II
, this partition
determines a standard parabolic subgroup
P_{J}
of
G
.
We will prove that
H
is contained in
P_{J}
.
Since the tuple
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}
has as many entries as possible, we also prove
that
P_{J}
is minimal among the standard parabolic subgroups containing
H
with respect to inclusion.
This implies that for every Levi group
L
of
H
, there is a Levi group

\widetilde{L}
of
P_{J}
with
L\leq\widetilde{L}
such that
L
is
\widetilde{L}
-irreducible,
i.e.,
L
is not contained in any proper parabolic subgroup of
\widetilde{L}
. We also prove that the unipotent radical
R_{u}(H)
of
H
is contained in the unipotent radical
R_{u}(P_{J})
of
P_{J}
.

The Reductive Part

Next we perform an extension of the specialization
\sigma_{\rm inter}
to the parameters of the reductive part.
To this end, we show that under certain natural assumptions on
\sigma_{\rm inter}
the generic irreducible factorization (
6
) corresponding to
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
specializes to an irreducible factorization

L_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\partial)\,=\,L_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\cdots L_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)

of the specialized normal form operator.
We denote by
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)
the
specialization of the generic least common left multiple.
With a further assumption on
\sigma_{\rm inter}
we prove that

\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,=\,\LCLM(L_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial),\dots,L_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial))\,.

We apply now the algorithm of E. Compoint and M. F. Singer developed in
[
CS99
]
to compute the generators of a maximal differential ideal

Q\lhd F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]\,=\,F[X_{i,j},\det(X_{i,j})^{-1}]

for the specialized least common left multiple
and obtain a Picard-Vessiot extension

E_{\rm red}\,:=\,\Frac(F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]/Q),

whose differential Galois group is the stabilizer
\Stab(Q)\leq\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}(C)
of
Q
, which is isomorphic to any Levi group
L
of
H
.

We continue the construction of our Picard-Vessiot extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
of
F
and our extended specialization (
9
) by matching the parameters of the reductive part with their respective counterparts in
E_{\rm red}
.
More precisely, the idea is to find for the parameters
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
rational functions

\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}
,

\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
in
\Frac(F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}])
such that
\sigma_{\rm inter}
extends to a differential homomorphism

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma_{\rm red}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}]&\to&E_{\rm red}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\boldsymbol{v}&\mapsto&\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}+Q\,=:\,\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\boldsymbol{\exp}&\mapsto&\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}}+Q\,=:\,\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\mathrm{int}_{i}&\mapsto&\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}+Q\,=:\,\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\,.\end{array}

Fixing a basis
\overline{X}_{1,1}:=X_{1,1}+Q,\dots,\overline{X}_{1,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}:=X_{1,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}+Q
of the solution space in
E_{\rm red}
of
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0

we develop a necessary and sufficient condition such that the map

\displaystyle\eta\colon D^{-1}C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]/Q\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\,,

\displaystyle y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\overline{c}_{j,i}\overline{X}_{1,j}

sending the generic basis
y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
to
C
-linear combinations of
\overline{X}_{1,1},\dots,\overline{X}_{1,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
is a differential ring homomorphism.
We use the differential Thomas decomposition to determine
\overline{c}_{i,j}\in C
satisfying this condition.
The rational functions

\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}}
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
are then obtained by applying this homomorphism to

\mathrm{EXP}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}),\ V^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{INT}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{j}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})\quad\text{(cf.\ \eqref{eqn:introduction1})}.

Having computed the
matching

\sigma_{\rm red}
, we extend the field
E_{\rm red}
allowing us to specialize also the remaining parameters
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
of the unipotent radical part.
More precisely, we
adjoin for each
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
an element
\underline{\mathrm{intrad}}_{i}
to
E_{\rm red}
which has the appropriate derivative and all these elements are algebraically independent over
E_{\rm red}
.
We obtain the differential ring

\underline{R}\,:=\,E_{\rm red}[\underline{\mathrm{intrad}}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}]

such that
\sigma_{\rm red}
extends to a differential homomorphism

(10)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\underline{\sigma}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]&\to&\Frac(\underline{R})=:\underline{E}\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{int}_{i}&\mapsto&\underline{\mathrm{intrad}}_{i}\quad\text{for}\ \beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}\,.\end{array}

Note that
\underline{E}
is not necessarily a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F
for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
.
We construct our final Picard-Vessiot extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
of
F
as the field of fractions of
\underline{R}/I_{\mathrm{max}}
for a choice of a maximal differential ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}\lhd\underline{R}
.

Applying
\underline{\sigma}
to the general fundamental matrix
\mathcal{Y}
and its decomposition
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{\rm red}\,\mathcal{Y}_{\rm rad}
we obtain

\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,=\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}\in G(\underline{R})

with
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\in G(E_{\rm red})
and
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}\in R_{u}(P_{J})(\underline{R})
.
Note that the subsequent steps of constructing
\overline{\mathcal{E}}

will not affect
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
, whose construction is completed at this point.
We prove that the logarithmic derivative

A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}\,:=\,\ell\delta(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\in\Lie(G)(F)

has entries in
F
and that
E_{\rm red}
is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F
for
A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}
.
The differential Galois group
L_{\rm red}
of
E_{\rm red}/F
,
in its representation induced by the fundamental matrix
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
,
is contained in the standard Levi group of
P_{J}
and will be used later.

The group of differential
F
-automorphisms of
\underline{E}

has a linear representation
\underline{H}
induced by
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
.
We prove that
H\leq\underline{H}\leq P_{J}
and that
\underline{H}
has Levi decomposition
\underline{H}=\underline{L}\ltimes R_{u}(P_{J})
, that is, its unipotent radical coincides with the unipotent radical of
P_{J}
.
It turns out that
L_{\rm red}
and any Levi group
L
of
H
are Levi groups of
\underline{H}
, implying that
\underline{L}
,
L
and
L_{\rm red}
are conjugate by elements in
R_{u}(P_{J})
.
Moreover, we will show that different choices of
I_{\mathrm{max}}\lhd\underline{R}
lead to differential
F
-isomorphic extension fields
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
.
We prove that these isomorphisms are induced by elements of
R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
, as are the automorphisms of
\underline{R}
corresponding to
transitions between different
I_{\mathrm{max}}
.
Moreover, for every Levi group
\underline{L}
of
\underline{H}
, there exists
I_{\mathrm{max}}
such that
\underline{L}
is a Levi group of
H
, in particular, there exists
I_{\mathrm{max}}
realizing
L_{\rm red}
as a Levi group of
H
.
We present an algorithm which computes a generating set of the defining ideals
I_{L_{\rm red}}
and
I_{\underline{H}}
of the respective groups
L_{\rm red}
and
\underline{H}
.

As a conclusion, note that all algebraic relations between the parameters of the reductive part are given by the ideal
Q
. It is left to determine the algebraic relations over
E_{\rm red}
among the indeterminates
\underline{\mathrm{intrad}}_{i}
.

The Unipotent Radical Part

The third and last extension takes care of the specialization of the parameters
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
. The basic idea is to compute the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical
R_{u}(H)=R_{u}(H^{\circ})
of the connected component of a potential differential Galois group
H
by reduction of
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
.
Knowing the Lie algebra we can compute generators of the defining ideal of
R_{u}(H)
using the exponential map.
Applying the same reduction to
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
, the unipotent radical part of the resulting Levi decomposition is required to lie in
R_{u}(H)(\underline{R})
.
Evaluating the above generators in order to express this condition, gives
a generating set for a maximal differential ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}
in
\underline{R}
. We are going to explain this in more detail.

In order to determine the Lie algebra of a potential differential Galois group we compute a reduced form of
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
using the algorithm of
T. Dreyfus and J.-A. Weil presented in
[
DW22
]
. Their algorithm can be divided into three main steps: transforming
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
into block triangular form, reducing the diagonal part and finally reducing the off-diagonal part.
Since we already made choices for the construction of our fundamental matrix
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
, we replace the first two steps by a reduction procedure specific to our choices. We apply the third reduction step of their algorithm to our intermediate result to reduce the part belonging to the Lie algebra of
R_{u}(P_{J})
.

For our first reduction step recall from Part
II
the matrix
g_{1}
and the Levi decomposition of
g_{1}\mathcal{Y}
.
Applying
\underline{\sigma}
defined in (
10
) to them we obtain a matrix
\overline{g}_{1}\in G(F)
and the Levi decomposition

\overline{g}_{1}\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,=\,(\overline{g}_{1}\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\cdot\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}\in P_{J}(\underline{R})

with
\overline{g}_{1}\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\in L_{J}(E_{\rm red})
and
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}\in R_{u}(P_{J})(\underline{R})
.

For our second reduction we possibly need to algebraically extend the base field
F
depending on the connectedness of the differential Galois group of the reductive part.
By the fundamental theorem, the fixed field in
E_{\rm red}
of the connected component
\Stab(Q)^{\circ}
is an algebraic extension
F_{\mathrm{alg}}
of
F
and the differential Galois group of
E_{\rm red}
over
F_{\mathrm{alg}}
is
\Stab(Q)^{\circ}
.
This implies that the differential Galois group of
E_{\rm red}
over
F_{\mathrm{alg}}
for
A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}
is the connected component
L_{\rm red}^{\circ}
of
L_{\rm red}
. Connectedness and the fact that with
F
also
F_{\mathrm{alg}}
is a
C_{1}
-field guarantee that there exists a matrix
\overline{g}_{2}\in L_{J}(F_{\mathrm{alg}})
gauge transforming
A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}
into the Lie algebra of
L_{\rm red}^{\circ}(F_{\mathrm{alg}})
.
In order to compute
\overline{g}_{2}
we use invariant theory for reductive groups to determine first a primitive element in
E_{\rm red}
for the algebraic extension
F_{\mathrm{alg}}
of
F
.
Using the ideal
Q
and the fundamental matrix
\overline{g}_{1}\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
we can compute with Gröbner basis methods a generating set of a maximal differential ideal in
F[\mathrm{GL}_{n}]
defining a Picard-Vessiot extension for
A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}
. Based on the existence of
\overline{g}_{2}
and on the primitive element for
F_{\mathrm{alg}}
we develop an algorithm which computes an
F_{\mathrm{alg}}
-rational point
\overline{g}_{2}\in L_{J}(F_{\mathrm{alg}})
of this maximal differential ideal.
The matrix
\overline{g}_{2}
achieves the reduction of the reductive part, meaning that it satisfies
\overline{g}_{2}\overline{g}_{1}\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\in L_{\rm red}^{\circ}(E_{\rm red})
and
A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}
is reduced into the Lie algebra:

\overline{g}_{2}\overline{g}_{1}.A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}\,=:\,A_{\rm red}\in\Lie(L_{\rm red}^{\circ})(F_{\mathrm{alg}})\,.

We prove that the effect of gauge transforming
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
by
\overline{g}_{2}\overline{g}_{1}
is the direct sum decomposition

(11)

\overline{g}_{2}\overline{g}_{1}.A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})\,=\,A_{\rm red}+A_{\rm rad}^{\rm pre}\in\Lie(L_{\rm red}^{\circ})(F_{\mathrm{alg}})\oplus\Lie(R_{u}(P_{J}))(F_{\mathrm{alg}})

with reduced reductive part
A_{\rm red}
.

For the computation of our third reduction matrix
\overline{g}_{3}\in R_{u}(P_{J})(F_{\mathrm{alg}})
we intend to use the third step of the algorithm of T. Dreyfus and J.-A. Weil.
Note that their algorithm at this step requires a triangular block structure with reduced diagonal part. In case
G=\mathrm{SL}_{l+1}
or
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{2l}
the decomposition (
11
) fulfills this condition.
For the remaining groups the authors wonder if an adaptation of this part of their algorithm using the structure of the Lie algebra instead of the block triangularity is possible (cf. Proposition
12.9
).
Neglecting effectivity at this point, we show that such a reduction using the Lie algebra exists.
Having computed
\overline{g}_{3}
with their algorithm, we achieve a complete reduction

\overline{g}_{3}\overline{g}_{2}\overline{g}_{1}.A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})\,=\,A_{\rm red}+A_{\rm rad}

into the Lie algebra of a connected group
H_{\rm con}
. Theoretically the defining ideal of
H_{\rm con}
would allow us to construct a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F_{\mathrm{alg}}
for
A_{\rm red}+A_{\rm rad}
with connected differential Galois group
H_{\rm con}
.
We prove that
L_{\rm red}^{\circ}
is a Levi group of
H_{\rm con}
and that the unipotent radical
R_{1}
of
H_{\rm con}
is a subgroup of
R_{u}(P_{J})
so that
H_{\rm con}
has the Levi decomposition
H_{\rm con}=L_{\rm red}^{\circ}\ltimes R_{1}
.

Using the Wei-Norman decomposition and the exponential map we can compute from
A_{\rm rad}
generators
f_{1},\dots,f_{a}
of the defining ideal
I_{R_{1}}
of the unipotent radical
R_{1}
. Moreover, we explain how one can compute the Levi decomposition of the reduced fundamental matrix

\overline{g}_{3}\overline{g}_{2}\overline{g}_{1}\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,=\,\widehat{\underline{\mathcal{Y}}}_{\rm red}\widehat{\underline{\mathcal{Y}}}_{\rm rad}

with
\widehat{\underline{\mathcal{Y}}}_{\rm red}\in L_{\rm red}^{\circ}(E_{\rm red})
and
\widehat{\underline{\mathcal{Y}}}_{\rm rad}\in R_{u}(P_{J})(\underline{R})
.
As a crucial step, we show that from evaluating the generators
f_{1},\dots,f_{a}
of
I_{R_{1}}
at the matrix
\widehat{\underline{\mathcal{Y}}}_{\rm rad}
we obtain generators

f_{1}(\widehat{\underline{\mathcal{Y}}}_{\rm rad}),\quad\dots,\quad f_{a}(\widehat{\underline{\mathcal{Y}}}_{\rm rad})\in\underline{R}

of a maximal differential ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}
in
\underline{R}
. We construct with respect to this
I_{\mathrm{max}}
our final Picard-Vessiot extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
of
F
with Galois group
H
and fundamental matrix
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
.
The factors of the Levi decomposition of the reduced fundamental matrix

\overline{g}_{3}\overline{g}_{2}\overline{g}_{1}\mathcal{\overline{Y}}\,=\,\widehat{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}}_{\rm red}\widehat{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}}_{\rm rad}

then satisfy
\widehat{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}}_{\rm red}\in L_{\rm red}^{\circ}(E_{\rm red})
and more importantly
\widehat{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}}_{\rm rad}\in R_{1}(\overline{\mathcal{E}})

with entries in
\overline{\mathcal{E}}\setminus E_{\rm red}
.
We prove that the differential Galois group
H
has Levi decomposition
H=L\ltimes R_{1}
with a Levi group
L
such that
L_{\rm red}^{\circ}=L^{\circ}
.
In other words, we only know that the connected component of
L_{\rm red}
is a Levi group of
H^{\circ}
. We do not know if
L_{\rm red}
is always automatically a Levi group of
H
. Thus, we cannot determine
I_{H}
by computing the defining ideal of the product variety of
L_{\rm red}
and
R_{1}
from
I_{L_{\rm red}}
and
I_{R_{1}}
. Instead, we actually need to compute the generators of the defining ideal
I_{H}
of
H
from the known algebraic relations between the parameters of
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
.
More precisely, choosing elements
\widetilde{f}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{f}_{a}
in

F(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})[\underline{\mathrm{intrad}}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}]

which are equal to
f_{1}(\widehat{\underline{\mathcal{Y}}}_{\rm rad}),\dots,f_{a}(\widehat{\underline{\mathcal{Y}}}_{\rm rad})
modulo
Q
, we present an algorithm which computes from the algebraic relations defined by the ideal
Q\lhd F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]
and by the ideal

(\widetilde{f}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{f}_{a})\lhd F(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})[\underline{\mathrm{intrad}}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}]

generators of the defining ideal
I_{H}
of the differential Galois group
H
.

The Structure of the Paper and Conclusions

The paper is divided into three main parts
I
,
II
,
III
each of which consists of several sections.
Part
I
deals with the introduction of the classical groups and the normal form equations defining the general extension field.
In Part
II
we analyze the action of the standard parabolic subgroups on
\mathcal{E}
and we derive the consequences of the Galois correspondence in the generic case. The main part is
III
in which we develop the algorithm for the computation of the differential Galois group of a specialization of the normal form matrix. Throughout the paper we use statements whose proofs can be found in the appendix. Due to the length of the paper we included at the end a table of notation.

The algorithm in its present form has certainly room for improvement. For example, the computation of the second reduction matrix
\overline{g}_{2}
in Section
12
is very costly and one might use an adapted version of the algorithm of T. Dreyfus and J.-A. Weil.
Moreover, we did not perform a detailed complexity analysis of our algorithm, which uses Gröbner basis and Thomas decomposition computations and the algorithms of M. F. Singer and E. Compoint as well as of T. Dreyfus and J.-A. Weil. Furthermore, we did not investigate yet the consequences of the assumptions on the specializations of our normal form matrices. This and the above mentioned questions are topics for future investigation.

F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
F
\mathcal{E}
\mathcal{\overline{E}}
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
E_{\rm red}^{L_{\rm red}^{\circ}}=F_{\mathrm{alg}}
\mathcal{\overline{E}}^{R_{u}(H)}=E_{\rm red}
L_{J}\cong
R_{u}(P_{J})
\cong L\cong L_{\rm red}
R_{u}(H)=R_{1}
\begin{array}[]{c}P_{J}\cong\\
L_{J}\ltimes R_{u}(P_{J})\end{array}
\begin{array}[]{c}H\cong\\
L\ltimes R_{u}(H)\end{array}

L/L^{\circ}\cong

.
Decomposition of Picard-Vessiot extension as tower of fixed fields for the generic case and its specialization.

Part I
The Classical Groups and Their Normal Forms

2.
The Classical Groups

Let
C
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero and let

G(C)\leq\mathrm{GL}_{n}(C)

be one of the classical groups of Lie rank
l
of type
A_{l}
,
B_{l}
,
C_{l}
,
D_{l}
or
\mathrm{G}_{2}
(here
l=2
).
We denote by
\Phi
the root system of the type corresponding to
G
and we write

\Delta=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{l}\}

for a basis of
\Phi
with simple roots
\alpha_{i}
. Each root in
\Phi
can be written uniquely as a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of simple roots with all coefficients either positive or negative leading to a disjoint decomposition

\Phi\,=\,\Phi^{+}\cup\Phi^{-}

of
\Phi
into a set of positive roots
\Phi^{+}
and a set of negative roots
\Phi^{-}
. The cardinalities of the positive and negative roots are equal and we denote them by

m\,=\,|\Phi^{+}|\,=\,|\Phi^{-}|.

The height
0pt(\alpha)
of a root
\alpha\in\Phi
is defined as the sum of all coefficients in the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of
\alpha
with respect to the basis
\Delta
. We write

\Phi^{+}\,=\,\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}\},

where we enumerate the positive roots
in such a way that
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{l}
are the simple roots from above and
0pt(\alpha_{r})\leq 0pt(\alpha_{s})
for all
r\leq s
. We write

\Phi^{-}\,=\,\{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{m}\}

for the negative roots, where the enumeration is chosen such that
\beta_{i}=-\alpha_{i}
.
Then
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{l}
are the negative simple roots and for
i\leq j
we have
|0pt(\beta_{i})|\leq|0pt(\beta_{j})|
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
be the Weyl group of
\Phi
. It is well known that
\mathcal{W}
is generated by the
l
simple reflections
w_{\alpha_{i}}
for the simple roots
\alpha_{i}\in\Delta
. Each element
w
of
\mathcal{W}
can be written as a finite product of simple reflections.
The minimal number of simple reflections needed to express
w\in\mathcal{W}
as such a product is called the length
l(w)
of
w
.
The length of
w
is equal to the number of roots
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
such that
w(\alpha)\in\Phi^{-}
. There is a unique Weyl group element of maximal length which we denote by
\overline{w}
. It induces a bijection between
\Phi^{+}
and
\Phi^{-}
and it maps the simple roots
\Delta
bijectively to the negative simple roots
-\Delta
.

We denote the Lie algebra of
G
by
\mathfrak{g}\subset\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(C)
and we fix a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
of
\mathfrak{g}
consisting of diagonal matrices. With respect to the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
we consider the Cartan decomposition

(12)

\mathfrak{g}\,=\,\mathfrak{h}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}

of
\mathfrak{g}
with one-dimensional root spaces
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subset\mathfrak{g}
for the roots
\alpha\in\Phi
. We choose a Chevalley basis

(13)

\{H_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq l\}\cup\{X_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Phi\}

according to this decomposition, where
H_{1},\dots,H_{l}
span the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
and each
X_{\alpha}
the root space
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
. We write

\mathfrak{u}^{+}\,=\,\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{i}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{u}^{-}\,=\,\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathfrak{g}_{\beta_{i}}

for the maximal nilpotent Lie subalgebras being the direct sums of all positive and all negative root spaces, respectively. We denote by

\mathfrak{b}^{+}\,=\,\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{u}^{+}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{b}^{-}\,=\,\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{u}^{-}

the maximal solvable Lie subalgebras containing the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
.

For a root
\alpha\in\Phi
we denote by
U_{\alpha}
the one-dimensional root group whose Lie algebra is the root space
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
. For
x\in C
we write
u_{\alpha}(x)\in U_{\alpha}
for the image of
xX_{\alpha}
under the exponential map

\exp\colon\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha},\ X_{\alpha}\mapsto\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{1}{j!}X_{\alpha}^{j}

and we call
u_{\alpha}(x)
a
parametrization of the root group

U_{\alpha}
.
For
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in C^{m}
we denote by

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,=\,u_{\beta_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(x_{m})

the product of all parametrized root group elements for the negative roots in this fixed order.
Let
U^{+}
and
U^{-}
be the maximal unipotent subgroups generated by all root groups corresponding to the positive and negative roots, respectively. The Lie algebra of
U^{+}
(resp.
U^{-}
) is then
\mathfrak{u}^{+}
(resp.
\mathfrak{u}^{-}
).

We denote by
T
the maximal torus whose Lie algebra is
\mathfrak{h}
. Moreover, for
i=1,\dots,l
consider the one-dimensional subtorus
T_{i}
of
T
with Lie algebra spanned by
H_{i}
.
For
x\in C^{\times}
let
t_{i}(x)
be the image of

\begin{pmatrix}x&0\\
0&x^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

under the isomorphism

\mathrm{SL}_{2}\to\langle U_{\alpha_{i}},U_{\beta_{i}}\rangle,\ \begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\exp(xX_{\alpha_{i}}),\ \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
x&1\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\exp(xX_{\beta_{i}})\,.

Then
t_{i}(x)
parametrizes
T_{i}
. For
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{l})\in(C^{\times})^{l}
we write

\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{x})\,=\,t_{1}(x_{1})\cdots t_{l}(x_{l})

for the product of all
t_{i}(x_{i})
, and
\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{x})
parametrizes the full torus
T
. We denote the normalizer of
T
in
G
by
N_{G}(T)
. The Weyl group
\mathcal{W}
is isomorphic to the quotient
N_{G}(T)/T
and for each
w\in\mathcal{W}
we fix a representative
n(w)
of
w
in
N_{G}(T)
.

We denote by
B^{+}
and
B^{-}
the Borel subgroups containing
T
and the maximal unipotent subgroups
U^{+}
and
U^{-}
, respectively.
Clearly, we have
B^{+}=TU^{+}
and
B^{-}=TU^{-}
and their Lie algebras are
\mathfrak{b}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{b}^{-}
, respectively. We denote a parabolic subgroup of
G
, that is a subgroup which contains a Borel subgroup, by
P
.
In this paper the standard parabolic subgroups are those which contain the Borel subgroup
B^{-}
. Each parabolic subgroup of
G
is conjugate to one and only one standard parabolic subgroup. For a subset

J\subset\{1,\dots,l\}
let
\mathcal{W}_{J}
be the subgroup of
\mathcal{W}
generated by the simple reflections
w_{\alpha_{j}}
with
j\in J
. The groups

(14)

P_{J}\,:=\,\bigcup_{w\in\mathcal{W}_{J}}B^{-}\,n(w)\,B^{-}

are the standard parabolic subgroups of
G
, i.e., the map
J\mapsto P_{J}
defines a bijection between the subsets of
\{1,\dots,l\}
and the standard parabolic subgroups of
G
. The roots of
P_{J}
relative to
T
are the roots in
\Phi^{-}
and
\Psi^{+}:=\Psi\cap\Phi^{+}
, where

(15)

\Psi\,:=\,\Phi\cap\langle\alpha_{j}\mid j\in J\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{-span}}\,.

To shorten notation we will omit in the following the plus sign in the notation of Lie subalgebras and subgroups, i.e., we will write
\mathfrak{u}
for
\mathfrak{u}^{+}
and so on.

Our results (including the previous ones in
[
Seib
,
Seia
,
RS23
]
) are based on geometric structure theorems of algebraic groups (cf. Theorems
2.1
–
2.3
). They are used to establish a connection between the defining matrix for the differential equation, the fundamental solution matrix and the differential Galois group.

For the reductive group
G
the following two theorems provide a normal form for elements in
G
parametrized by
B
and
\mathcal{W}
. For their proofs we refer to
[
Hum75
, 28.3 Theorem and 28.4 Theorem]
.

Theorem 2.1

(Bruhat decomposition)
.

We have

G\,=\,\biguplus_{w\in\mathcal{W}}B\,n(w)\,B\qquad\text{(disjoint union)}

with
Bn(w)B=Bn(\widetilde{w})B
if and only if
w=\widetilde{w}
in
\mathcal{W}
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Each element
g\in G
can be written in the form

g\,=\,u^{\prime}\,n(w)\,t\,u\,,

where
w\in\mathcal{W}
,
t\in T
,
u\in U
and
u^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}_{w}:=U\cap n(w)\,U^{-}\,n(w)^{-1}
are all determined uniquely by
g
.

The next two theorems provide us with a decomposition of a subgroup of
G
into a reductive and a unipotent subgroup. For details and the proofs of the theorems we refer to
[
OV90
, Chapter 6,
4^{\circ}
]
.
The radical
R(H)
of a subgroup
H
of
G
is the largest connected normal solvable subgroup of
H
. The subgroup
R_{u}(H)\leq R(H)
of unipotent elements of
R(H)
is normal in
H
and is called the unipotent radical of
H
.

Theorem 2.3

(Levi decomposition)
.

There is a reductive subgroup
L
of
H
, called a
Levi group
of
H
, such that

H\,=\,L\ltimes R_{u}(H)\qquad\text{(semidirect product).}

Each element
g\in H
can be written in the form
g=g^{\prime}\,u
with
g^{\prime}\in L
and
u\in R_{u}(H)
uniquely determined by
g
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
H=L\ltimes R_{u}(H)
be a Levi decomposition of
H
and let
\widetilde{H}
be a reductive subgroup of
H
. Then there exists
u\in R_{u}(H)
such that
u\widetilde{H}u^{-1}\leq L
. In particular, if
\widetilde{L}
is another Levi group of
H
, then
L
and
\widetilde{L}
are conjugate by an element of
R_{u}(H)
.

For a standard parabolic subgroup
P_{J}
the unipotent radical
R_{u}(P_{J})
is generated by those root groups
U_{\beta}
for which
\beta\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
with
\Psi^{-}:=\Phi^{-}\cap\Psi
.
Its Lie algebra is the direct sum of root spaces

\Lie(R_{u}(P_{J}))\,=\,\bigoplus_{\beta\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}}\mathfrak{g}_{\beta}\,.

The group
P_{J}
has many Levi groups and by Theorem
2.4
any two Levi groups of
P_{J}
are conjugate by an element of
R_{u}(P_{J})
.
We denote by
L_{J}
the Levi group of
P_{J}
whose Lie algebra is

\Lie(L_{J})\,=\,\mathfrak{h}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Psi}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\,.

We call
L_{J}
the
standard Levi group of

P_{J}
and

(16)

P_{J}\,=\,L_{J}\ltimes R_{u}(P_{J})

the
standard Levi decomposition of

P_{J}
.

The following definition is taken from
[
Ser05
, Section 3.2.1]
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
H
be a connected reductive linear algebraic group. A closed subgroup
\widetilde{H}
of
H
is called
H
-irreducible, if
\widetilde{H}
is not contained in any proper parabolic subgroup of
H
.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
H
be a closed reductive subgroup of
G
.
Then there exists a parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
which is minimal with respect to containing
H
, such that
H
is
L
-irreducible for a Levi group
L
of
P
. Every parabolic subgroup which is minimal with respect to containing
H
has this property.

Proof.

In characteristic zero for a closed subgroup
H
of
G
the notion of being
G
-completely reducible and reductive coincide (cf.
[
BMR05
, Section 2.2]
). Thus the statement follows from
[
BMR05
, Corollary 3.5]
.
∎

3.
A Normal Form for the Classical Groups

Let
F
be the rational function field
C(z)
with standard derivation
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{t}}=(\mathrm{t}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{t}_{l})
be differential indeterminates over
F
. Let
G
be one of the classical groups of Section
2
.
In
[
Seib
]
we constructed a matrix
A_{G}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{t}})
in
\mathfrak{g}(C\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{t}}\rangle)
such that

\partial(\boldsymbol{y})=A_{G}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{t}})\,\boldsymbol{y}
defines a Picard-Vessiot extension of
C\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{t}}\rangle
with differential Galois group
G(C)
.
For the construction of
A_{G}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{t}})
we considered the structure of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
More precisely, there are
l
positive roots
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{l}\in\Phi^{+}
such that we obtain a direct sum decomposition

\mathfrak{b}^{+}\,=\,\ad(A_{0}^{-})(\mathfrak{u}^{+})+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma_{i}}\,,

where

A_{0}^{-}\,=\,\sum_{i=1}^{l}X_{\beta_{i}}

is the sum of all basis elements of the root spaces corresponding to the simple negative roots. The roots
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{l}
are called the
complementary roots
and their heights
0pt(\gamma_{i})
are equal to the exponents of
\mathfrak{g}
.
Interchanging the roles of the negative with the positive roots, we define

A_{0}^{+}\,=\,\sum_{i=1}^{l}X_{\alpha_{i}}\,.

Definition 3.1
.

We call the matrix

A_{G}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{t}})\,:=\,A_{0}^{+}+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\mathrm{t}_{i}X_{-\gamma_{i}}

the
normal form matrix
for the classical group
G
.

For the construction of our general extension field we consider further differential indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{l})
over
F
.
Recall that
n(\overline{w})\in N_{G}(T)
denotes a representative of the longest Weyl group element
\overline{w}\in\mathcal{W}
.

Theorem 3.2

(
[
RS23
]
)
.

There are

(i)

non-zero constants
c_{1},\dots,c_{l}
in
C
,

(ii)

polynomials
g_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}),\dots,g_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathbb{Z}[\boldsymbol{v}]
that are
C
-linearly independent and homogeneous of degree one,

(iii)

differential polynomials
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})=(s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}),\dots,s_{l}(\boldsymbol{v}))
with
s_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})\in C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
which are differentially algebraically independent over
F
and

(iv)

differential polynomials
\boldsymbol{f}=(f_{l+1},\dots,f_{m})
with
f_{i}\in C\langle\boldsymbol{v}\rangle

having the following properties:

(a)

The matrix

A_{\rm Liou}(\boldsymbol{v})\,:=\,\sum_{i=1}^{l}g_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})H_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{l}c_{i}X_{\beta_{i}}\in\mathfrak{b}^{-}(C\langle\boldsymbol{v}\rangle),

where
\beta_{i}=-\alpha_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,l
, defines a Picard-Vessiot extension
\mathcal{E}
of
F\langle\boldsymbol{v}\rangle
with differential Galois group
B^{-}(C)
and has fundamental solution matrix

\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{Liou}}\,:=\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})\in B^{-}(\mathcal{E})\,.

The parameters
\boldsymbol{\exp}=(\exp_{1},\dots,\exp_{l})\in\mathcal{E}^{l}
for the diagonal torus in
B^{-}(\mathcal{E})
satisfy

\partial(\exp_{i})\exp_{i}^{-1}\,=\,g_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})

and the parameters
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}=(\mathrm{int}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{int}_{m})\in\mathcal{E}^{m}
for the maximal unipotent subgroup of
B^{-}(\mathcal{E})
are successive integrals, meaning that the integral
\mathrm{int}_{i}
corresponds to the root
\beta_{i}
and depends on
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and on those integrals
\mathrm{int}_{j}
with
|0pt(\beta_{j})|<|0pt(\beta_{i})|
.

(b)

The logarithmic derivative of

\mathcal{Y}\,:=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{Liou}}

is the normal form matrix
A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
.
The differential field
\mathcal{E}=F\langle\boldsymbol{v}\rangle(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{Liou}})
is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
for
A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
and the differential Galois group of
\mathcal{E}
over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
is
G(C)
.

The action of
G(C)
on the parameters
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{f}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}
, which generate
\mathcal{E}
over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
as a field, is induced by the Bruhat decomposition, namely each
g\in G(C)
induces a differential
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
-automorphism

\gamma_{g}\in{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(\mathcal{E}/F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle)

of
\mathcal{E}
by multiplying
\mathcal{Y}
from the right with
g
.
Thus we can determine the effect of
\gamma_{g}
on the parameters
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{f}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}
by means of the Bruhat decomposition of the product

\mathcal{Y}\,g\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})\,g\,.

More precisely, if
\boldsymbol{v}^{g}=(v^{g}_{1},\dots,v^{g}_{l})
,
\boldsymbol{f}^{g}=(f^{g}_{l+1},\dots,f^{g}_{m})
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}^{g}=(\exp^{g}_{1},\dots,\exp^{g}_{l})
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}^{g}=(\mathrm{int}^{g}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{int}^{g}_{m})
are the parameters of the Bruhat decomposition

\mathcal{Y}\,g\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v}^{g},\boldsymbol{f}^{g})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp}^{g})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}^{g})\,,

then the images of
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{f}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}
under
\gamma_{g}
are

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\gamma_{g}(\boldsymbol{v})&\!=&\!\boldsymbol{v}^{g}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\gamma_{g}(\boldsymbol{f})&\!=&\!\boldsymbol{f}^{g}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\gamma_{g}(\boldsymbol{\exp})&\!=&\!\boldsymbol{\exp}^{g}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\gamma_{g}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})&\!=&\!\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}^{g}\,.\end{array}

Recall from Theorem
3.2
that the differential polynomials
s_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})\in F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}\subset\mathcal{E}
are invariant under the action of the differential Galois group
G(C)
.

Definition 3.3
.

The matrix differential equation defined by the normal form matrix
A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
corresponds to the scalar linear differential equation

L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y\,=\,0

introduced in
[
Seib
]
with suitable operator

L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\in C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\}[\partial]\,.

We call
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
the
normal form (scalar) equation
for
G
and the linear operator

L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)

the
normal form operator
for
G
.

Given the normal form equation, we consider its associated equations (cf. Appendix
C
for their definition and construction) and their corresponding Riccati equations in case
G
is of type
A_{l}
,
B_{l}
,
C_{l}
or
\mathrm{G}_{2}
(here
l=2
).

Definition 3.4
.

For
i=1,\dots,l
we denote the

i
-th associated equation
for the normal form equation
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
by

L^{\det(i)}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y\,=\,0\,.

Moreover, we denote by

\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),y)\,=\,0

the Riccati equation for the
i
-th associated equation.

Proposition 3.5
.

The
i
-th associated equation has the exponential

\exp^{\rm ass}_{i}\,:=\,e^{\int b_{i}v_{i}}\in\mathcal{E}

for some
b_{i}\in\{\pm 1,-2\}

as a solution and so
\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),y)=0
has the solution
b_{i}v_{i}\in C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
. Moreover,
b_{1}v_{1},\dots,b_{l}v_{l}
and
g_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}),\dots,g_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})
generate the same
\mathbb{Z}
-module.

Proof.

See Proposition
C.2
and Corollary
C.3
in Appendix
C
.
∎

Over
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
the normal form operator is irreducible.
In the subsequent sections we will consider irreducible factorizations of the normal form operator over intermediate differential fields
of
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle\subset C\langle\boldsymbol{v}\rangle
obtained by adjoining subsets of the indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}
to
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
.
In the other extreme case when the base field is
C\langle\boldsymbol{v}\rangle
we have the following factorization.

Proposition 3.6
.

The normal form operator

L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\partial]

of order
n
factors over
C\langle\boldsymbol{v}\rangle
into a product of first order operators

L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}(\partial-a_{i})\,,

where
a_{i}\in C[\boldsymbol{v}]
is homogeneous of degree one for all
i=1,\dots,n
.

Proof.

See Propositions
B.2
and
B.3
in Appendix
B
.
∎

4.
The Gauge Transformation

Let
K
be a differential field with field of constants
C
and derivation
\partial_{K}
.
Two matrices
A_{1}
and
A_{2}\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(K)
are called
gauge equivalent
over
K
if there exists
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
such that

(17)

g.A_{1}\,:=\,gA_{1}g^{-1}+\partial_{K}(g)g^{-1}\,=\,A_{2}\,.

Consider now the adjoint action
\Ad
of
G
on
\mathfrak{g}
, that is for
g\in G
the automorphism

\Ad(g)\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g},\quad X\mapsto gXg^{-1}\,.

Moreover, let

\ell\delta\colon\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)\to\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(K),\quad g\mapsto\partial_{k}(g)g^{-1}

be the logarithmic derivative.
Then we have

(18)

g.A_{1}\,=\,\Ad(g)(A_{1})+\ell\delta(g)\,=\,A_{2}\,,

and so the following two remarks enable us to describe the gauge transformation of an element in
\mathfrak{g}(K)
by a root group element in terms of the root system of
G
.

Remark 4.1
.

For linearly independent
\alpha
,
\beta\in\Phi
and
r,q\in\mathbb{N}
let

\alpha-r\beta,\dots,\alpha+q\beta
be the
\beta
-string through
\alpha
and let

\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
be the Cartan integer. Then, with respect to the Chevalley basis defined in (
13
), we have
[
Car89
, Section 4.3]

\displaystyle\Ad(u_{\beta}(x))(X_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{i=0}^{q}c_{\beta,\alpha,i}x^{i}X_{\alpha+i\beta}\,,

\displaystyle\Ad(u_{\beta}(x))(H_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{\alpha}-\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle xX_{\beta}\,,

\displaystyle\Ad(u_{\beta}(x))(X_{-\beta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{-\beta}+xH_{\beta}-x^{2}X_{\beta}\,,

where
c_{\beta,\alpha,0}=1
and
c_{\beta,\alpha,i}=\pm\binom{r+i}{i}
.

The next remark is taken from
[
Kov69
]
.

Remark 4.2
.

Let
G\leq\mathrm{GL}_{n}
be a linear algebraic group. Then the restriction of
\ell\delta
to
G
maps

G(K)
to its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}(K)
, i.e., we have

\ell\delta|_{G}\colon G(K)\to\mathfrak{g}(K).

Part II
The General Extension Field and the Standard Parabolic Subgroups

5.
The Fixed Field of a Standard Parabolic Subgroup

Since up to conjugation every reductive subgroup of
G
is contained
L_{J}
-irreducibly in the standard Levi group
L_{J}
of a standard parabolic subgroup
P_{J}
, we investigate in this section the fixed field
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
of the general extension field under
P_{J}
.
It will turn out that it is differentially generated over
F
by
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})
and by the differential indeterminates of a uniquely determined subset of
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{l}\}
.
We introduce the following notation which will be used throughout the paper.

Definition 5.1
.

We denote by

I^{\prime}\cup I^{\prime\prime}\,=\,\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}\cup\{i_{r+1},\dots,i_{l}\}

a partition of
I:=\{1,\dots,l\}
and we define the set

J\,=\,\{j\in I\mid\alpha_{j}=\overline{w}\,(-\alpha_{i})\ \text{for some}\ i\in I^{\prime}\}\,.

The partition
I^{\prime}\cup I^{\prime\prime}
defines a partition

\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\,=\,(v_{i_{r+1}},\dots,v_{i_{l}}),\quad\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext}\,=\,(v_{i_{1}},\dots,v_{i_{r}})

of the differential indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{l})
.

Recall from Section
3
that the Galois action
\gamma_{g}
for
g\in G(C)
on the parameters
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{f}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}
in the Bruhat decomposition of
\mathcal{Y}
is induced by the Bruhat decomposition of
\mathcal{Y}\,g
. For a standard parabolic subgroup

P_{J}\,=\,\bigcup_{w\in\mathcal{W}_{J}}B^{-}\,n(w)\,B^{-}

the following lemma determines the action of
n(w_{\alpha_{j}})\in N_{G}(T)
on the parameters for the simple reflections
w_{\alpha_{j}}
with
j\in J
which generate
\mathcal{W}_{J}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
,
\boldsymbol{e}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{l})
and
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
be indeterminates over
C
. For a simple root
\alpha_{j}\in\Delta
let
\alpha_{i}\in\Delta
be the unique simple root such that
\overline{w}\,(-\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{j}
. Then there exist
x\in C(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{y})\setminus C(\boldsymbol{x})
and
b\in B^{-}(C(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{y}))
such that

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{e})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{y})\,n(w_{\alpha_{j}})\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,u_{-\alpha_{i}}(x)\,n(\overline{w})\,b\,.

Proof.

For the given simple root
\alpha:=\alpha_{j}\in\Delta
the maximal unipotent group

U^{-}
can be written as

(19)

U^{-}\,=\,U_{-\alpha}\,U^{-}_{w_{\alpha}}\,=\,U^{-}_{w_{\alpha}}\,U_{-\alpha}\,,

where
U^{-}_{w_{\alpha}}:=U^{-}\cap n(w_{\alpha})\,U^{-}\,n(w_{\alpha})^{-1}
,
by
[
Hum75
, end of 28.1]
, exchanging the roles played by the positive and negative roots.
Moreover, the subgroups
U^{-}_{w_{\alpha}}
and
T
are normalized by

n(w_{\alpha})

and so we obtain

\begin{array}[]{rcl}U^{-}\,n(\overline{w})\,B^{-}\,n(w_{\alpha})&\!=&\!U^{-}\,n(\overline{w})\,T\,U^{-}\,n(w_{\alpha})\,=\,U^{-}\,n(\overline{w})\,U^{-}\,T\,n(w_{\alpha})\\[5.0pt]
&\!=&\!U^{-}\,n(\overline{w})\,U^{-}n(w_{\alpha})\,T\,=\,U^{-}\,n(\overline{w})\,U_{-\alpha}\,U^{-}_{w_{\alpha}}\,n(w_{\alpha})\,T\\[5.0pt]
&\!=&\!U^{-}\,n(\overline{w})\,U_{-\alpha}\,n(w_{\alpha})\,U^{-}_{w_{\alpha}}\,T\,.\end{array}

Applying this to the product of
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{e})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{y})
with
n(w_{\alpha})
, we obtain

(20)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&&\!\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{e})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{y})\,n(w_{\alpha})\\[5.0pt]
&\!=&\!\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,u_{2}\,t_{2}\,n(w_{\alpha})=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,u_{2}\,n(w_{\alpha})\,t_{3}\\[5.0pt]
&\!=&\!\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,u_{-\alpha}\,u_{w_{\alpha}}\,n(w_{\alpha})\,t_{3}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,u_{-\alpha}\,n(w_{\alpha})\,\widetilde{u}_{w_{\alpha}}\,t_{3}\,,\end{array}

where
t_{2}
,
t_{3}\in T
,
u_{2}=u_{-\alpha}u_{w_{\alpha}}\in U^{-}
with
u_{-\alpha}\in U_{-\alpha}
,
u_{w_{\alpha}}\in U^{-}_{w_{\alpha}}
and
\widetilde{u}_{w_{\alpha}}\in U^{-}_{w_{\alpha}}
.
Since we only rewrote the product

\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{e})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{y})\,n(w_{\alpha})\,,

whose factors are parametrized by
\boldsymbol{e}
and
\boldsymbol{y}
, we conclude that the parameter of
u_{-\alpha}
lying in the one-parameter subgroup
U_{-\alpha}
is an element of
C(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{y})\setminus C(\boldsymbol{x})
.
In the last product of (
20
) we investigate further the factor
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,u_{-\alpha}\,n(w_{\alpha})
. From
[
Hum75
, end of 28.1]
(now without exchanging the roles played by the positive and negative roots) it follows that on group level we have

(21)

U^{-}\,n(\overline{w})\,U_{-\alpha}\,n(w_{\alpha})\,=\,n(\overline{w})\,U^{+}\,U_{-\alpha}\,n(w_{\alpha})\,=\,n(\overline{w})\,U_{w_{\alpha}}\,U_{\alpha}\,U_{-\alpha}\,n(w_{\alpha})

with
n(\overline{w})^{-1}\,U^{-}\,n(\overline{w})=U^{+}
and
U^{+}=U_{w_{\alpha}}^{+}\,U_{\alpha}
and
U^{+}_{w_{\alpha}}:=U^{+}\cap n(w_{\alpha})U^{+}n(w_{\alpha})^{-1}
.
Since
U_{\alpha}\,U_{-\alpha}\,n(w_{\alpha})
is contained in the centralizer
C_{G}(\kernel(\alpha))
(the root
\alpha
is here considered as the map
\alpha:T\to C^{\times}
), the computation in
\mathrm{SL}_{2}

(22)

\begin{pmatrix}1&a_{1}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
a_{2}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\frac{a_{1}a_{2}+1}{a_{2}}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{a_{2}}&0\\
-1&a_{2}\end{pmatrix}

with
a_{1}
,
a_{2}\in C(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{y})
and
a_{2}\neq 0

combined with (
21
) implies that there exist

u_{+}\in U^{+}
and
u_{w_{\alpha}}\in U^{+}_{w_{\alpha}}
,
u_{\alpha}\in U_{\alpha}

with
u_{+}=u_{w_{\alpha}}u_{\alpha}
and
\widetilde{u}_{\alpha}\in U_{\alpha}
and
b\in B^{-}

such that

(23)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,u_{-\alpha}\,n(w_{\alpha})&\!=&\!n(\overline{w})\,u_{+}\,u_{-\alpha}\,n(w_{\alpha})\\[5.0pt]
&\!=&\!n(\overline{w})\,u_{w_{\alpha}}\,u_{\alpha}\,u_{-\alpha}\,n(w_{\alpha})\,=\,n(\overline{w})\,u_{w_{\alpha}}\,\widetilde{u}_{\alpha}\,b\,,\end{array}

where
\widetilde{u}_{\alpha}\,b=u_{\alpha}\,u_{-\alpha}\,n(w_{\alpha})
according to (
22
).
The longest Weyl group element
\overline{w}
induces a bijection between
\Delta
and
\Delta^{-}
and so there exists a unique
-\alpha_{i}\in\Delta^{-}
such that
\overline{w}(-\alpha_{i})=\alpha
. Thus, from the decomposition

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,=\,u_{w_{\alpha_{i}}}u_{-\alpha_{i}}(x_{i})\,=\,\Big(\prod_{-\alpha_{k}\in\Delta^{-}\setminus\{-\alpha_{i}\}}u_{-\alpha_{k}}(x_{k})\prod_{\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{c}\beta\in\Phi^{-}\\
0pt(\beta)<-1\end{array}}u_{\beta}\Big)\,u_{-\alpha_{i}}(x_{i})\,,

which we obtain from applying (
19
) for the simple root
\alpha_{i}
to
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})
,
it follows that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}n(\overline{w})^{-1}\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})&\!=&\!u_{+}\,=\,u_{w_{\alpha}}\,u_{\alpha}(x_{i})\\[7.5pt]
&\!=&\!\displaystyle\Big(\prod_{\alpha_{j_{k}}\in\Delta\setminus\{\alpha\}}u_{\alpha_{j_{k}}}(x_{k})\prod_{\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{c}\beta\in\Phi^{+}\\
0pt(\beta)>1\end{array}}u_{\beta}\Big)\,u_{\alpha}(x_{i}),\end{array}

where
\overline{w}\,(-\alpha_{k})=\alpha_{j_{k}}
and
u_{w_{\alpha_{i}}}\in U^{-}_{w_{\alpha_{i}}}
.
Since the parameter of
u_{\alpha}
is
x_{i}
and the parameter of
u_{-\alpha}
is an element of
C(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{y})\setminus C(\boldsymbol{x})
, say
1/x
, it follows from (
22
) that the parameter
\widetilde{x}
of
\widetilde{u}_{\alpha}
is

\widetilde{x}\,=\,x_{i}+x.

Further developing (
23
) by inserting
n(\overline{w})^{-1}n(\overline{w})
between
\widetilde{u}_{\alpha}
and
b
we obtain

\begin{array}[]{l}\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,u_{-\alpha}\,n(w_{\alpha})\,=\\[7.5pt]
\qquad\qquad\displaystyle\Big(\prod_{-\alpha_{k}\in\Delta^{-}\setminus\{-\alpha_{i}\}}u_{-\alpha_{k}}(x_{k})\prod_{\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{c}\beta\in\Phi^{-}\\
0pt(\beta)<-1\end{array}}u_{\beta}\Big)\,u_{-\alpha_{i}}(\widetilde{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,b\,.\end{array}

Finally, combining (
20
) and the last equation together with

u_{-\alpha_{i}}(\widetilde{x})\,=\,u_{-\alpha_{i}}(x_{i})\,u_{-\alpha_{i}}(x)\,,

we obtain for some
\widetilde{b}\in B^{-}
the decomposition

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{e})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{y})\,n(w_{\alpha})\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,u_{-\alpha_{i}}(x)\,n(\overline{w})\,\widetilde{b}\,.

∎

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
P_{J}
be a standard parabolic subgroup.
According to Definition
5.1

the set
J
uniquely determines the partition

I^{\prime}\cup I^{\prime\prime}\,=\,\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}\cup\{i_{r+1},\dots,i_{l}\}

of
I=\{1,\dots,l\}
.
Let

q\,:=\,|\Phi^{-}\setminus\langle\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,\alpha_{i_{r}}\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}-\mathrm{span}}|.

Then there exist elements
\boldsymbol{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{q})
with
p_{i}\in C[\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}]\subset C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
such that

\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}\,=\,F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p}).

Moreover, we may choose the first
l-r
entries
of
\boldsymbol{p}
to be the indeterminates

\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}=(v_{i_{r+1}},\dots,v_{i_{l}})
.
The indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext}=(v_{i_{1}},\dots,v_{i_{r}})
are not fixed by
P_{J}
and
P_{J}
is the largest subgroup of
G
fixing the indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
.

Proof.

Generally, if
u_{1}\,n(w)\,b_{1}
is the Bruhat decomposition of an element of
G
, then the Bruhat decomposition after multiplication on the right with an element
b_{2}\in B^{-}
is
u_{1}\,n(w)\,b_{3}
with
b_{3}=b_{1}\,b_{2}
.
Hence, the factor
u_{1}
does not change.

Let

b_{1}\,n(w)\,b_{2}\in P_{J}\,=\,\bigcup_{w\in\mathcal{W}_{J}}B^{-}\,n(w)\,B^{-}

with
b_{1}
,
b_{2}\in B^{-}
and
w\in\mathcal{W}_{J}=\langle w_{\alpha_{j}}\mid j\in J\rangle
.
Moreover, let
\widetilde{U}
be the unipotent subgroup of
U^{-}
generated by the root groups

U_{\beta_{i_{1}}},\,\dots,\,U_{\beta_{i_{r}}}\,

corresponding to the simple negative roots
\beta_{i_{1}},\dots,\beta_{i_{r}}
.
Then the above observation and Lemma
5.2
, applied to each factor in the product of simple reflections for
w
separately, imply that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}\,b_{1}\,n(w)\,b_{2}\!

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})\,b_{1}\,n(w)\,b_{2}

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,b_{3}\,n(w)\,b_{2}

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,u\,n(\overline{w})\,b_{4}\,b_{2}

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,u\,n(\overline{w})\,b_{5}\,,

where
b_{3}=\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})\,b_{1}
and
u\in\widetilde{U}(\mathcal{E})
obtained from applying successively Lemma
5.2
to the factors
n(w_{\alpha_{j}})
with
j\in J
in the product expression for
n(w)
,
b_{4}\in B^{-}
and
b_{5}=b_{4}\,b_{2}
.
Thus the Galois action of
b_{1}\,n(w)\,b_{2}
on
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\boldsymbol{f}
is determined by the Bruhat decomposition of the product

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,u\,.

Since
\widetilde{U}
is generated by the root groups corresponding to the negative simple roots
\beta_{i_{1}},\dots,\beta_{i_{r}}
, the elements
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}=(v_{i_{r+1}},\dots,v_{i_{l}})
are among the invariants of this action and the elements
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext}=(v_{i_{1}},\dots,v_{i_{r}})
are not. First we are going to determine the invariants for the action of
\widetilde{U}(C)
on
U^{-}(C)
.
Therefore, let
C[U^{-}]
be the coordinate ring of
U^{-}(C)
. Since
U^{-}(C)
is unipotent, the quotient
(U^{-}/\widetilde{U})(C)
is affine by
[
Bor91
, II.6, Corollary 6.9 (b)]
.
Moreover
(U^{-}/\widetilde{U})(C)
is isomorphic to
C^{q}
and so the ring of invariants

C[U^{-}/\widetilde{U}]\,\cong\,C[U^{-}]^{\widetilde{U}(C)}

is generated by
q
algebraically independent elements
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{p}}=(\widetilde{p}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{p}_{q})
. We extend now the field of definition from
C
to
\mathcal{E}
, that is we consider now the action of
\widetilde{U}(\mathcal{E})
on

U^{-}(\mathcal{E})
.
Since
\mathcal{E}[\widetilde{U}]\cong\mathcal{E}\otimes_{C}C[\widetilde{U}]
, we have

(C[U^{-}]\otimes_{C}\mathcal{E})^{\widetilde{U}(\mathcal{E})}\,\cong\,C[U^{-}]^{\widetilde{U}(C)}\otimes_{C}\mathcal{E}\,,

i.e., the invariant ring for the action of
\widetilde{U}(\mathcal{E})
on
U^{-}(\mathcal{E})
is generated by the

q
algebraically independent polynomials

\widetilde{p}_{1}\otimes 1,\dots,\widetilde{p}_{q}\otimes 1\in C[U^{-}]^{\widetilde{U}(C)}\otimes_{C}\mathcal{E}\,.

For the point
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\in U^{-}(\mathcal{E})
, the invariants have constant values on the orbit of
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})
under
\widetilde{U}(\mathcal{E})
.
Evaluating the above invariants
\widetilde{p}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{p}_{q}
at

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})
we obtain
\boldsymbol{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{q})
with
p_{i}\in C[\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}]\subset\mathcal{E}
which are
invariant under the action of
\widetilde{U}(\mathcal{E})
.
Since
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
are generators of the polynomial ring
C[\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}]
and they are invariant, we can choose the first components of
\boldsymbol{p}
to be these elements. We show that
\boldsymbol{p}
are algebraically independent over

F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
.
The differential field extension
\mathcal{E}^{B^{-}}=F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})
of
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
has transcendence degree
m
by the Fundamental Theorem of Differential Galois Theory and so the
m
elements
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}

are algebraically independent over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
. Thus
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}
are algebraically
independent over
F
and so the ring homomorphism

(24)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}F\otimes_{C}C[U^{-}]=F\otimes_{C}C[Y,\det(Y)^{-1}]/I_{U^{-}}&\!\to&\!F[\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}]\,,\\[5.0pt]
\overline{Y}_{i,j}:=Y_{i,j}+I_{U^{-}}&\!\mapsto&\!(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}))_{i,j}\end{array}
