is a ring isomorphism, where
I_{U^{-}}
is the defining ideal in
C[Y,\det(Y)^{-1}]
of
U^{-}
.
Since

\widetilde{p}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{p}_{q}
are algebraically independent over
F
, their images
p_{1},\dots,p_{q}

are also algebraically independent over
F
.
Assume that there exists

P(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{q})\in F\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\}[Z_{1},\dots,Z_{q}]

such that
P(p_{1},\dots,p_{q})=0
. Since the elements
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})
are algebraically independent over
F[Z_{1},\dots,Z_{q}]
, the
coefficients in
F[Z_{1},\dots,Z_{q}]
of
P(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{q})
considered as a polynomial in
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})
have to
vanish when substituting
p_{1},\dots,p_{q}
for
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{q}
. Since
p_{1},\dots,p_{q}
are algebraically independent over

F
, these coefficients are the zero polynomials implying that
P(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{q})
is also the zero polynomial.

By the Fundamental Theorem of Differential Galois Theory, the transcendence degree of
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
is

2m+l-\dim(P_{J})\,=\,2m+l-(m+l+(m-q))\,=\,q\,.

Since the transcendence degree of
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
is
q
and
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})\subset\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
,
we conclude that
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}=F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
(note that this implies that
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
is a differential field, i.e., all derivatives of the elements in

\boldsymbol{p}
are in
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
).

Let
g\in G\setminus P_{J}
. Then by Theorem
2.2
, formulated for
B^{-}
instead of
B^{+}
, there is a unique
w\in\mathcal{W}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{J}
such that

g\in(U^{-}_{w})^{\prime}\,n(w)\,B^{-}\,.

Since
w\notin\mathcal{W}_{J}
, there is at least one simple reflection
w_{\alpha_{i}}
appearing in the product of simple reflections for
w
with
i\notin J
.
Let
\alpha_{s}\in\Delta
such that
\overline{w}(-\alpha_{s})=\alpha_{i}
. Since
\overline{w}
induces a bijection between
\Delta
and
\Delta^{-}
, we conclude that
s\notin I^{\prime}
and so
s\in I^{\prime\prime}
. We conclude with Lemma
5.2
that the indeterminate
v_{s}
is not fixed by
g
and so
P_{J}
is the largest group fixing
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

We have the one-to-one correspondences

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{partitions}\\
I=I^{\prime}\cup I^{\prime\prime}\end{array}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1:1}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{standard parabolic}\\
\text{subgroups}\,P_{J}\end{array}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1:1}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\,\text{fixed by}\,P_{J}\,\text{and }\\
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext}\,\text{not fixed by}\,P_{J}\end{array}\right\}

The first correspondence follows from the definition of the partition
I^{\prime}\cup I^{\prime\prime}
of
I
and the set
J
.
The second correspondence is a consequence of Theorem
5.3
, since it states that the
fixed field
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
of
P_{J}
contains the differential indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
and does not contain the differential indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext}
.

Corollary 5.5
.

We have
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})=F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
.

Proof.

According to Theorem
5.3
the indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
are among the differential polynomials
\boldsymbol{p}
and since
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
is a differential field, also all derivatives of
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
are contained in
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
.
Thus we have inclusions of differential fields

F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle\subset F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\subset F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})=\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}\subset\mathcal{E}.

By the Fundamental Theorem of Differential Galois Theory
[
CH11
, Theorem 6.3.8 (1)]
, there exists a closed subgroup
\widetilde{G}
of
G

such that
\mathcal{E}^{\widetilde{G}}=F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
and
\widetilde{G}\geq P_{J}
.
Since
\widetilde{G}
also fixes
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
and
P_{J}
is according to Theorem
5.3
the largest subgroup of
G
fixing
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
, we conclude that

\widetilde{G}=P_{J}
and so
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})=F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
.
∎

Example 5.6
.

Let
G=\mathrm{SL}_{4}(C)
. The root system
\Phi
of
\mathrm{SL}_{4}
is of type
A_{3}
and
\Phi^{-}
consists of the six roots

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\,:=\,-\alpha_{1},\quad\beta_{2}\,:=\,-\alpha_{2},\quad\beta_{3}\,:=\,-\alpha_{3},

\displaystyle\beta_{4}\,:=\,-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2},\quad\beta_{5}\,:=\,-\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3},\quad\beta_{6}\,:=\,-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}\,.

For six indeterminates
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{6})
the respective parametrized root group elements are

\displaystyle u_{i}(x_{i})=E_{4}+x_{i}E_{i+1,i}\quad\text{for}\ i=1,2,3,

\displaystyle u_{4}(x_{4})=E_{4}+x_{4}E_{3,1},\ u_{5}(x_{5})=E_{4}+x_{5}E_{4,2}\quad\text{and}\quad u_{6}(x_{6})=E_{4}+x_{6}E_{4,1}.

We consider here the case where
I^{\prime}=\{1,3\}
and
I^{\prime\prime}=\{2\}
, that is
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext}=(v_{1},v_{3})
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}=(v_{2})
.
In other words, the indeterminate
v_{2}
will be fixed by the Galois action of
P_{J}
with
J=\{1,3\}
. The group
\widetilde{U}
(cf. Theorem
5.3
) is the product of the root groups
U_{\beta_{1}}
and
U_{\beta_{3}}
.
For two new indeterminates
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},y_{3})
the action of
\widetilde{U}
on
C[\boldsymbol{x}]
is described by recomputing the standard decomposition of

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})u_{\beta_{1}}(y_{1})u_{\beta_{3}}(y_{3}).

We find that the ring of invariants
C[\boldsymbol{x}]^{\widetilde{U}}
is generated over
C
by

(25)

x_{2},\ -x_{1}x_{2}+x_{4},\ x_{5},\ -x_{1}x_{5}+x_{3}x_{4}+x_{6}.

Following the construction presented in
[
Seia
]
for
A_{\mathrm{SL}_{4}}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
and its fundamental matrix
\mathcal{Y}
we find the following differential polynomials
in
C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
:

\displaystyle s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v})\!

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!v_{3}^{2}+v_{2}^{2}-v_{1}v_{2}-v_{2}v_{3}+v_{1}^{2}+v_{1}^{\prime}+v_{2}^{\prime}+v_{3}^{\prime}\,,

\displaystyle s_{2}(\boldsymbol{v})\!

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!4v_{1}v_{1}^{\prime}-v_{2}v_{1}^{\prime}-2v_{1}v_{2}^{\prime}+2v_{2}v_{2}^{\prime}-v_{2}v_{3}^{\prime}-v_{1}v_{2}^{2}\,+

\displaystyle\!v_{2}^{2}v_{3}+v_{1}^{2}v_{2}-v_{2}v_{3}^{2}+2v_{1}^{\prime\prime}+v_{2}^{\prime\prime}\,,

\displaystyle s_{3}(\boldsymbol{v})\!

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!v_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}+2v_{1}v_{1}^{\prime\prime}-v_{1}v_{2}^{\prime\prime}+2(v_{1}^{\prime})^{2}+2v_{1}v_{2}v_{1}^{\prime}-v_{2}^{2}v_{1}^{\prime}\,+

\displaystyle\!v_{2}v_{3}v_{1}^{\prime}-v_{3}^{2}v_{1}^{\prime}-v_{1}^{\prime}v_{2}^{\prime}-v_{1}^{\prime}v_{3}^{\prime}+v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{\prime}-2v_{1}v_{2}v_{2}^{\prime}\,-

\displaystyle\!v_{1}^{2}v_{3}^{\prime}+v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}^{\prime}+v_{1}^{2}v_{2}v_{3}-v_{1}^{2}v_{3}^{2}-v_{1}v_{2}^{2}v_{3}+v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle f_{4}(\boldsymbol{v})\!

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!v_{1}^{2}+v_{1}^{\prime}\,,

\displaystyle f_{5}(\boldsymbol{v})\!

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!v_{2}^{2}+v_{1}^{2}+v_{1}^{\prime}+v_{2}^{\prime}-v_{2}v_{1}\,,

\displaystyle f_{6}(\boldsymbol{v})\!

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!v_{1}^{3}-v_{1}^{2}v_{3}+3v_{1}v_{1}^{\prime}-v_{1}^{\prime}v_{3}+v_{1}^{\prime\prime}\,.

Substituting
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})
for
\boldsymbol{x}
in the invariants in (
25
) we obtain the invariants in
C[\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}]
under the Galois action of
P_{J}
.
We find

\displaystyle\mathrm{Inv}_{1}\,:=\,v_{2}\,,\quad\mathrm{Inv}_{2}\,:=\,-v_{2}v_{1}+v_{1}^{2}+v_{1}^{\prime}\,,\quad\mathrm{Inv}_{3}\,:=\,v_{2}^{2}+v_{1}^{2}+v_{1}^{\prime}+v_{2}^{\prime}-v_{2}v_{1}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Inv}_{4}\,:=\,-v_{2}^{2}v_{1}+v_{1}^{2}v_{2}+2v_{1}v_{1}^{\prime}-v_{2}^{\prime}v_{1}+v_{1}^{\prime\prime}\,.

According to Corollary
5.5
, we have
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}=C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{2}\rangle
.
We are going to check that
\mathrm{Inv}_{i}\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{2}\rangle
for
i=1,\ldots,4
.
Clearly,
\mathrm{Inv}_{1}\in C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{2}\}
and we observe that

\mathrm{Inv}_{4}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v})-v_{2}^{\prime}-v_{2}^{2}+v_{2}(s_{2}(\boldsymbol{v})-v_{2}^{\prime\prime}-2v_{2}^{\prime}v_{2}))\in C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{2}\}\,.

For the remaining two invariants consider the three differential polynomials

\begin{array}[]{rcl}w_{1}&\!:=&\!v_{2}^{4}-2v_{2}^{2}s_{1}+2v_{2}^{\prime}v_{2}^{2}+s_{1}^{2}-2s_{1}v_{2}^{\prime}+(v_{2}^{\prime})^{2}+4s_{3}-4\ \mathrm{Inv}_{4}^{\prime}\in C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{2}\},\\[5.0pt]
w_{2}&\!:=&\!-2(s_{1}-v_{2}^{\prime}-v_{2}^{2})^{\prime}+2(s_{2}-v_{2}^{\prime\prime}-2v_{2}^{\prime}v_{2})\in C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{2}\},\\[5.0pt]
w_{3}&\!:=&\!v_{1}^{2}-v_{2}v_{1}+v_{3}v_{2}-v_{3}^{2}+v_{1}^{\prime}-v_{3}^{\prime}\in C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}.\end{array}

The element
w_{1}
is actually the square
w_{3}^{2}
and the derivative of
w_{3}
is

w_{3}^{\prime}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}w_{2}-v_{2}w_{3}\,.

Differentiating
w_{1}=w_{3}^{2}
and using the expression for
w_{3}^{\prime}
and
w_{1}=w_{3}^{2}
we obtain

w_{1}^{\prime}\,=\,2w_{3}(\frac{1}{2}w_{2}-v_{2}w_{3})\,=\,w_{3}w_{2}-2v_{2}w_{3}^{2}\,=\,w_{3}w_{2}-2v_{2}w_{1}\,,

which is equivalent to
w_{1}^{\prime}+2v_{2}w_{1}=w_{3}w_{2}
.
The element
w_{1}^{\prime}+2v_{2}w_{1}\in C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{2}\}
factors in
C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
as
w_{2}w_{3}
and, since
w_{2}\in C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{2}\}
, we conclude that

w_{3}\,=\,\frac{w_{1}^{\prime}+2v_{2}w_{1}}{w_{2}}\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{2}\rangle.

We obtain that

\mathrm{Inv}_{2}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}(w_{3}+(s_{1}-v_{2}^{\prime}-v_{2}^{2}))\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Inv}_{3}\,=\,\mathrm{Inv}_{2}+v_{2}^{2}+v_{2}^{\prime}

are elements of
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{2}\rangle
. Note that it is in general not true that the subring of all differential polynomial invariants of
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{2}\rangle
is equal to
C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{2}\}
.

Remark 5.7
.

According to Corollary
5.5
the invariants
p_{1},\dots,p_{q}
in
C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
have a representation as a differential rational function in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
. We are going to explain how one can compute
p_{i,1}
and
p_{i,2}
in
C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}
such that
p_{i}=p_{i,1}/p_{i,2}
for
1\leq i\leq q
. For a differentiation order
d_{1}\geq 0
and a degree
d_{2}\geq 1
one considers all monomials
\mathrm{mon}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{mon}_{k}

up to degree
d_{2}
(including also the monomial
1
) in the new indeterminates

(26)

\partial^{j}(\widehat{s}_{1}),\dots,\partial^{j}(\widehat{s}_{l}),\partial^{j}(\widehat{v}_{i_{r+1}}),\dots,\partial^{j}(\widehat{v}_{i_{l}})\quad\text{for}\ 0\leq j\leq d_{1}.

For constant indeterminates
c_{1,1},\dots,c_{1,k}
and
c_{2,1},\dots,c_{2,k}
one makes the ansatz

\widehat{p}_{i,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}c_{1,j}\mathrm{mon}_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{p}_{i,2}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}c_{2,j}\mathrm{mon}_{j}

and substitutes in
\widehat{p}_{i,2}p_{i}-\widehat{p}_{i,1}=0
for the variables in (
26
)
the respective elements

(27)

\partial^{j}(s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v})),\dots,\partial^{j}(s_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})),\partial^{j}(v_{i_{r+1}}),\dots,\partial^{j}(v_{i_{l}})\quad\text{for}\ 0\leq j\leq d_{1}

in
C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
. Comparing coefficients in
C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
, one obtains a linear system in the indeterminates
c_{1,1},\dots,c_{1,k}
and
c_{2,1},\dots,c_{2,k}
over
C
.
If this system has a solution in
C^{2k}
,
then one
substitutes in
\widehat{p}_{i,1}
and
\widehat{p}_{i,2}
for the constant indeterminates this solution and for the variables in
(
26
) the
elements in (
27
)
and obtains
p_{i,1}

and
p_{i,2}
in
C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}
such that
p_{i}=p_{i,1}/p_{i,2}
. If the linear system has no solution, then one increases
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
and repeats the computation. Since by Corollary
5.5
such a representation of
p_{i}
exists, this process has to stop after finitely many iterations.

6.
The Structure Theorem for Parabolic Subgroups

Let
P_{J}
be a proper standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. Then the fixed field of
\mathcal{E}
under
P_{J}(C)
is

\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}\,=\,F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle

as we have seen in the previous section. By the Fundamental Theorem of Differential Galois Theory, the extension

\mathcal{E}
of
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
is a Picard-Vessiot extension for the normal form equation with differential Galois group
P_{J}(C)
.
Moreover, if

P_{J}(C)\,=\,L_{J}(C)\ltimes R_{u}(P_{J})(C)

is the standard Levi decomposition of
P_{J}
, then
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
with differential Galois group isomorphic to

L_{J}(C)\,\cong\,\big(P_{J}/R_{u}(P_{J})\big)(C)\,.

In this section we determine generators and a defining equation for
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
.

Over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
the normal form operator is not irreducible anymore. Let

(28)

L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,=\,L_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\cdots L_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)

be an irreducible factorization of
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)
over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
,
where each factor in this product is of order at least
1
and monic.
The dependence of
L_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)
on
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
indicates the ground field
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
for the factorization. If the ground field is clear from the context we shortly write

(29)

L_{1}(\partial)\,:=\,L_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial),\quad\dots,\quad L_{k}(\partial)\,:=\,L_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial).

Definition 6.1
.

We denote by

\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\,:=\,\LCLM(L_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial),\dots,L_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial))

the
least common left multiple
of the irreducible factors

L_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial),\quad\dots,\quad L_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)

of
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)
over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
. We denote its order by
n_{I^{\prime\prime}}
.

The unipotent radical
R_{u}(P_{J})
is generated by those root groups which correspond to the roots in
\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
. The following lemma implies that those
\mathrm{int}_{i}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}=(\mathrm{int}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{int}_{m})
with index
i
such that
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
are fixed by the action of
R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\beta_{j}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
and let

\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
and
y
be indeterminates over
C
.
Then the coefficients
\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{m}\in C[\boldsymbol{x},y]
in the standard decomposition

u_{\beta_{1}}(\tilde{x}_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(\tilde{x}_{m})

of the product

(30)

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,u_{\beta_{j}}(y)\,=\,\big(u_{\beta_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(x_{m})\big)\,u_{\beta_{j}}(y)

are uniquely determined.
Moreover, they satisfy
\tilde{x}_{j}\neq x_{j}
and
\tilde{x}_{i}=x_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
such that
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
.

Proof.

The first part follows from
[
Car89
, Theorem 5.3.3 (ii)]
applied for the set of negative roots
\Phi^{-}
.

The second part involves more work.
From
[
Car89
, Theorem 5.2.2]
we have for two roots
\beta
,
\beta^{\prime}\in\Phi^{-}
the exchange formula

(31)

u_{\beta^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})\,u_{\beta}(x)\,=\,u_{\beta}(x)\,u_{\beta^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})\prod_{a^{\prime},a>0}u_{a^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}+a\beta}(c_{a^{\prime},a,\beta,\beta^{\prime}}(-x)^{a^{\prime}}x^{\prime a})\,,

where the product is taken over all positive integers
a^{\prime}
,
a
such that
a^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}+a\beta\in\Phi^{-}
and where

c_{a^{\prime},a,\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Q}
. We apply formula (
31
) to the product (
30
) until we moved
u_{\beta_{j}}(y)
to the
j
-th factor
u_{\beta_{j}}(x_{j})
of the product
u_{\beta_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(x_{m})
. More precisely, we obtain

(32)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,u_{\beta_{j}}(y)\!

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!\big(u_{\beta_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(x_{m})\big)u_{\beta_{j}}(y)

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!u_{\beta_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{j-1}}(x_{j-1})u_{\beta_{j}}(x_{j})u_{\beta_{j}}(y)\prod_{i=j+1}^{m}u_{\beta_{i}}(x_{i})\,\widetilde{u}_{i}

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!u_{\beta_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{j-1}}(x_{j-1})u_{\beta_{j}}(x_{j}+y)\prod_{i=j+1}^{m}u_{\beta_{i}}(x_{i})\,\widetilde{u}_{i}\,,

where
\tilde{u}_{i}\in U^{-}(C[\boldsymbol{x},y])
. Moreover, each
\widetilde{u}_{i}
is a product of elements of root groups belonging to roots of
\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
, since with
\beta_{j}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
also

a^{\prime}\beta+a\beta_{j}

belongs to
\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
for every root
\beta\in\Phi^{-}
and
a^{\prime},\,a>0
.
Furthermore, the roots
a^{\prime}\beta+a\beta_{j}
are ranked higher than the roots
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{j}
. Thus in the product

(33)

\prod_{i=j+1}^{m}u_{\beta_{i}}(x_{i})\,\widetilde{u}_{i}

only elements of the root groups
U_{\beta_{j+1}},\dots,U_{\beta_{m}}
can appear as factors. We apply recursively formula (
31
) to these factors in increasing order of roots.
The above arguments show that this process creates only new factors which belong to higher ranked roots and so the process of bringing the factors into order stops after finitely many steps and we conclude that the product in (
33
) is rewritten as

u_{\beta_{j+1}}(\tilde{x}_{j+1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(\tilde{x}_{m}).

The rewriting process defined by (
31
) and (
32
) determines unique polynomials

\tilde{x}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{x}_{m}\in C[\boldsymbol{x},y]\,.

This proves
\tilde{x}_{j}=x_{j}+y\neq x_{j}
.

Assume we are in step
k=j+1,\dots,m
of the recursion, i.e., we have introduced the product

u_{\beta_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{j-1}}(x_{j-1})\,u_{\beta_{j}}(x_{j}+y)u_{\beta_{j+1}}(\tilde{x}_{j+1})\cdots u_{\beta_{k}}(\tilde{x}_{k})\,\widetilde{u}\,,

where
\widetilde{u}
is a product of root group elements belonging to
U_{\beta_{k+1}},\dots,U_{\beta_{m}}
.
Every time we applied formula (
31
), the newly created factors are root group elements belonging to roots in
\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
.
Thus only these root group elements can appear more than once as factors in the new product.
If
\beta_{k+1}\in\Psi^{-}
, then
u_{\beta_{k+1}}(x_{k+1})
is the only factor in the product
\tilde{u}
lying in the root group
U_{\beta_{k+1}}
. Swapping
u_{\beta_{k+1}}(x_{k+1})
to position
k+1
yields

u_{\beta_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{j-1}}(x_{j-1})u_{\beta_{j}}(x_{j}+y)u_{\beta_{j+1}}(\tilde{x}_{j+1})\cdot u_{\beta_{k}}(\tilde{x}_{k})u_{\beta_{k+1}}(x_{k+1})\,\widehat{u}\,,

where
\widehat{u}
is a product of root group elements belonging to
U_{\beta_{k+2}},\dots,U_{\beta_{m}}
. Thus, for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
we have
\tilde{x}_{i}=x_{i}
.

We further mention that if
\beta_{k+1}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
, then there might appear several factors

u_{\beta_{k+1}}(y_{1}),\quad\dots,\quad u_{\beta_{k+1}}(y_{t})

in the product
\tilde{u}
belonging to the root group
U_{\beta_{k+1}}
, where without loss of generality
y_{1}=x_{k+1}
and

y_{2},\dots,y_{t}\in C[\boldsymbol{x},y]
.
Collecting all these factors in
\tilde{u}
towards the left, we obtain

\displaystyle u_{\beta_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{j-1}}(x_{j-1})\,u_{\beta_{j}}(x_{j}+y)\,u_{\beta_{j+1}}(\tilde{x}_{j+1})\cdots u_{\beta_{k}}(\tilde{x}_{k})\,u_{\beta_{k+1}}(y_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{k+1}}(y_{t})\,\widehat{u}

\displaystyle=u_{\beta_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{j-1}}(x_{j-1})\,u_{\beta_{j}}(x_{j}+y)\,u_{\beta_{j+1}}(\tilde{x}_{j+1})\cdots u_{\beta_{k}}(\tilde{x}_{k})\,u_{\beta_{k+1}}(\tilde{x}_{k+1})\,\widehat{u}\,,

where
\tilde{x}_{k+1}=y_{1}+\dots+y_{t}
and
\widehat{u}
is a product of root group elements belonging to
U_{\beta_{k+2}},\dots,U_{\beta_{m}}
. Hence, for all
\beta_{k}
with
j+1\leq k\leq m
and
\beta_{k}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
we have either

\tilde{x}_{k}\neq x_{k}
or
\tilde{x}_{k}=x_{k}
.
∎

Theorem 6.3
.

For a standard parabolic subgroup
P_{J}
we consider its standard Levi decomposition

P_{J}\,=\,L_{J}\ltimes R_{u}(P_{J}).

Let
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}=F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
be as in Theorem
5.3
. Then the following statements hold:

(a)

The general extension field
\mathcal{E}

is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
with differential Galois group
P_{J}(C)
(cf. Figure
3
(a)).

(b)

We have

\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}=F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})\langle\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\rangle.

(c)

The differential field
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}

is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
with differential Galois group isomorphic to
L_{J}
and is defined by the linear differential equation

\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y\,=\,0

in the differential indeterminate
y
over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
(cf. Figure
3
(b)).

\mathcal{E}
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
P_{J}

(a)

(a)

\mathcal{E}
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
L_{J}\cong
R_{u}(P_{J})

(b)

(b)

Figure 3
.
The different subextensions of
\mathcal{E}/F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
.

Proof.

(a)
According to Theorem
5.3
the fixed field of
\mathcal{E}
under the action of
P_{J}(C)
is
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}=F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
and so by the Fundamental Theorem of Differential Galois Theory
\mathcal{E}
is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
with differential Galois group
P_{J}(C)
.

(b)
Multiplying the fundamental matrix

\mathcal{Y}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})

from the right by an element of
U^{-}(C)
and determining the Bruhat decomposition of the product only has an effect on the factor
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})
. Thus the parameters
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{f}
and
\boldsymbol{\exp}
are left fixed by
U^{-}(C)
and so by the unipotent radical
R_{u}(P_{J})(C)\leq U^{-}(C)
, too.
Recall that the unipotent radical
R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
is generated by those root groups which correspond to the roots in
\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
. We apply now Lemma
6.2
successively for each factor
u_{j}(y_{j})\in U_{\beta_{j}}(C)
to

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})\,\prod_{\beta_{j}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}}u_{j}(y_{j})

and conclude that
\mathrm{int}_{i}
is left fixed by
R_{u}(P_{J})
for every
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
and that
\mathrm{int}_{j}
is not left fixed by
R_{u}(P_{J})
for every
\beta_{j}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
.
With
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})=F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle

we conclude that

(34)

K\,:=\,F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})\langle\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\rangle\subset\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}\,.

By the Fundamental Theorem of Differential Galois Theory,
\mathcal{E}
is a purely transcendental extension of

F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
and of
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
with respective transcendence degrees

\trdeg_{F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle}(\mathcal{E})\,=\,2m+l\quad\mbox{and}\quad\trdeg_{\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}}(\mathcal{E})\,=\,|\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}|\,.

Then the transcendence degree of
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
is

\displaystyle\trdeg_{F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle}(\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\trdeg_{F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle}(\mathcal{E})-\trdeg_{\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}}(\mathcal{E})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2m+l-|\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m+l+|\Psi^{-}|\,,

where we used
|\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}|=m-|\Psi|
.
We prove that

\trdeg_{F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle}(K)\,=\,m+l+|\Psi^{-}|\,.

Since
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})=F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
according to Corollary
5.5
, we have on the one hand that

F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext}\rangle\,=\,F\langle\boldsymbol{v}\rangle\,.

On the other hand, the proofs of
[
RS23
, Proposition 7.1]
and
[
RS23
, Corollary 7.2]
imply that

F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,v_{1}^{(d_{1}-1)},\dots,v_{l},v_{l}^{\prime},\dots,v_{l}^{(d_{l}-1)})\,=\,F\langle\boldsymbol{v}\rangle

with
d_{1}+\dots+d_{l}=m
and so combined we have that

F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext}\rangle\,=\,F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,v_{1}^{(d_{1}-1)},\dots,v_{l},v_{l}^{\prime},\dots,v_{l}^{(d_{l}-1)})\,.

We conclude that the
2m+l
elements

(35)

v_{1},\,v_{1}^{\prime},\,\dots,\,v_{1}^{(d_{1}-1)},\,\dots,\,v_{l},\,v_{l}^{\prime},\,\dots,\,v_{l}^{(d_{l}-1)},\,\boldsymbol{\exp},\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}

are algebraically independent over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
, since they generate the general extension
\mathcal{E}
of
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
with differential Galois group
G(C)
of transcendence degree

\trdeg_{F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle}(\mathcal{E})=\dim(G)\,=\,2m+l\,.

Since
K
is generated over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
as a field by the generators of
\mathcal{E}
in (
35
) without the
m-|\Psi^{-}|
generators
\mathrm{int}_{i}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}=(\mathrm{int}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{int}_{m})
corresponding to the roots
\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
,
we conclude that

\trdeg_{F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle}(K)\,=\,m+l+|\Psi^{-}|\,.

Because the transcendence degrees coincide it follows with (
34
) that
K=\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
.

(c)
Recall from Definition
6.1
that

\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)

is the least common left multiple of the irreducible factors
L_{1}(\partial),\dots,L_{k}(\partial)

of
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)
over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})=F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
.
We denote by
n_{1},\dots,n_{k}
the orders of the irreducible factors
L_{1}(\partial),\dots,L_{k}(\partial)
and
we define the integers

n_{k}^{\prime}\,=\,n_{k},\ n_{k-1}^{\prime}\,=\,n_{k}+n_{k-1},\ \dots,\ n_{2}^{\prime}\,=\,n_{k}+\dots+n_{2},\ n_{1}^{\prime}\,=\,n_{k}+\dots+n_{1}\,.

According to the proof of
[
CS99
, Proposition 4.2]
there exists a basis
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
in
\mathcal{E}
of the solution space of

L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
such that the elements

(36)

\begin{array}[]{l}y_{1},\ \dots,\ y_{n_{k}^{\prime}},\\[5.0pt]
L_{k}(\partial)y_{n_{k}^{\prime}+1},\ \dots,\ L_{k}(\partial)y_{n_{k-1}^{\prime}},\\[5.0pt]
(L_{k-1}(\partial)\circ L_{k}(\partial))y_{n_{k-1}^{\prime}+1},\ \dots,\ (L_{k-1}(\partial)\circ L_{k}(\partial))y_{n_{k-2}^{\prime}},\ \dots,\\[5.0pt]
(L_{2}(\partial)\circ\cdots\circ L_{k}(\partial))y_{n_{2}^{\prime}+1},\ \dots,\ (L_{2}(\partial)\circ\cdots\circ L_{k}(\partial))y_{n_{1}^{\prime}}\end{array}

span the solution space of the equation

\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y\,=\,0\,.

It is also shown there that the Picard-Vessiot extension generated as a differential field over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
by the elements in (
36
) is equal to the fixed field
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
.
Hence, by the Fundamental Theorem of Differential Galois Theory
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
with differential Galois group

(P_{J}/R_{u}(P_{J}))(C)\,\cong\,L_{J}(C)\,.

∎

Remark 6.4
.

A
C
-basis of the solution space for the least common left multiple can be computed using the
C
-basis of the solution space of the normal form equation

L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y\,=\,0\,.

A
C
-basis of the latter is formed by the entries
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
of the first row of the matrix
B_{G}\mathcal{Y}
,
where
B_{G}
is the matrix we choose to gauge transform

A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
to the companion matrix which corresponds to the normal form equation.
The matrix
B_{G}
describes a change of a basis of a differential module for
A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
to a basis defined by a cyclic vector which can be taken from
[
Seib
]
. It is shown in Proposition
A.2
that
B_{G}\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\})
.
Let
(c_{i,j})
be an
n\times n
matrix of constant indeterminates and define

(\tilde{y}_{1},\dots,\tilde{y}_{n})^{tr}\,=\,(c_{i,j})(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})^{tr}.

Let
n^{\prime}_{k},\dots,n^{\prime}_{1}
be as in the proof of Theorem
6.3
.
One determines the first
n_{k}^{\prime}
rows of
(c_{i,j})
such that

\{\tilde{y}_{1},\,\dots,\,\tilde{y}_{n_{k}^{\prime}}\}
is a basis of the solution space of
L_{k}(\partial)
.
Then one computes
c_{i,j}
with
n_{k}^{\prime}+1\leq i\leq n_{k-1}^{\prime}
and
1\leq j\leq n
such that

\{L_{k}(\partial)\tilde{y}_{n_{k}^{\prime}+1},\dots,\,L_{k}(\partial)\tilde{y}_{n_{k-1}^{\prime}}\}

is a
C
-basis of the solution space of
L_{k-1}(\partial)
. Continuing in this way we find bases

(37)

\begin{array}[]{l}\{\tilde{y}_{1},\,\dots,\,\tilde{y}_{n_{k}^{\prime}}\},\{L_{k}(\partial)\tilde{y}_{n_{k}^{\prime}+1},\dots,\,L_{k}(\partial)\tilde{y}_{n_{k-1}^{\prime}}\},\\[5.0pt]
\{(L_{k-1}(\partial)\circ L_{k}(\partial))\tilde{y}_{n_{k-1}^{\prime}+1},\,\dots,(L_{k-1}(\partial)\circ L_{k}(\partial))\tilde{y}_{n_{k-2}^{\prime}}\},\,\dots,\\[5.0pt]
\{(L_{2}(\partial)\circ\cdots\circ L_{k}(\partial))\tilde{y}_{n_{2}^{\prime}+1},\,\dots,\,(L_{2}(\partial)\circ\cdots\circ L_{k}(\partial))\tilde{y}_{n_{1}^{\prime}}\}\end{array}

of the solution spaces of the irreducible factors
L_{k}(\partial),L_{k-1}(\partial)\dots,L_{1}(\partial)
. All these elements together generate the solution space of the least common left multiple, but need not be linearly independent.
Using Gaussian elimination we find a basis
y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
for the solution space of

(38)

\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y\,=\,0.

We fix a
C
-basis

y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}

in
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
of the solution space of the linear differential equation (
38
).
The next step is to express the parameters
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and the entries
\mathrm{int}_{i}
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}=(\mathrm{int}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{int}_{m})
with indices
i
such that
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
in differential algebraic terms with respect to
y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
. It is an immediate consequence of Theorem
6.3
that this is possible.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let

C[G]\,=\,C[\overline{Y}_{i,j}\mid i,j=1,\dots,n]=C[Y_{i,j}\mid i,j=1,\dots,n]/I_{G}

be the coordinate ring of
G
, where
I_{G}
is the defining ideal of
G
and
n
denotes the dimension of the representation of
G
. Then there exist
e_{1},\dots,e_{l}\in C[G]
and

\boldsymbol{z}\,=\,(\prod_{j=1}^{l}e_{j}^{a_{1,j}},\dots,\prod_{j=1}^{l}e_{j}^{a_{l,j}})\quad\text{with}\ a_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}

and
\boldsymbol{x}:=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
and
\boldsymbol{w}:=(w_{1},\dots,w_{m})
in the localization
\mathcal{M}^{-1}C[G]
of
C[G]
by the multiplicatively closed subset
\mathcal{M}
generated by
e_{1},\dots,e_{l}
such that

\overline{Y}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w})

is the Bruhat decomposition of
\overline{Y}:=(\overline{Y}_{i,j})
.

Proof.

For a proof see Propositions
D.2
and
D.3
in Appendix
D
.
∎

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
be a
C
-basis of the solution space of the least common left multiple and let
\boldsymbol{Z}=(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})
be differential indeterminates over
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
.

(a)

There exist
l
differential rational functions

\mathrm{EXP}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z}),\quad\dots,\quad\mathrm{EXP}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{l}(\boldsymbol{Z})

in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{Z}\rangle
such that

\mathrm{EXP}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}(y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})\,=\,\exp_{i}\,.

In other words, we have

\frac{\mathrm{EXP}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}(y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})^{\prime}}{\mathrm{EXP}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}(y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})}\,=\,g_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})\,.

(b)

There exist
l
differential rational functions

V_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{Z})\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{Z}\rangle

such that

V_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})\,=\,v_{i}\,.

(c)

For each index
i
such that
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
there exist differential rational functions

\mathrm{INT}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{Z})\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{Z}\rangle

such that

\mathrm{INT}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})\,=\,\mathrm{int}_{i}\,.

Moreover, all these rational functions can be chosen to be contained in a localization
D_{\exp}^{-1}C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\boldsymbol{Z}\}
by a multiplicatively closed subset
D_{\exp}
generated by
l
differential polynomials

E_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z}),\ \dots,\ E_{l}(\boldsymbol{Z})\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\boldsymbol{Z}\}

having the additional property that their evaluations

E_{1}(y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})\,=\,\exp^{\rm ass}_{1},\quad\dots,\quad E_{l}(y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})\,=\,\exp^{\rm ass}_{l}

are the exponential solutions of the associated equations (cf. Proposition
3.5
).

Proof.

According to Theorem
6.3
we have that
\exp_{j}\in\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
for
1\leq j\leq l
and that
\mathrm{int}_{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
for all indices
i
such that
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
.
Moreover, Theorem
6.3
also implies that

\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}\,=\,C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\langle y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\rangle\,.

This proves
(a)
and
(c)
.
For
(b)
we use Gaussian elimination to determine
\mathbb{Q}
-linear combinations of
g_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}),\dots,g_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})
to express
v_{1},\dots,v_{l}
.
In these expressions we substitute for
g_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}),\dots,g_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})
the differential rational functions

\frac{\mathrm{EXP}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})^{\prime}}{\mathrm{EXP}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})}\qquad\text{for}\ j=1,\dots,l

and obtain
V_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{Z})
.

To prove the supplement we consider the differential
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
-isomorphism of Picard-Vessiot rings

\psi\colon C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle\otimes_{C}C[G]\rightarrow C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathcal{Y}],\ \overline{Y}_{i,j}\mapsto\mathcal{Y}_{i,j}

for
A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
(cf.
[
vdPS03
, Theorem 1.28]
and note that here the torsor is trivial) and extend it to the differential
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
-isomorphism of Picard-Vessiot fields

\psi\colon C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle\otimes_{C}C(G)\rightarrow C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\mathcal{Y})=\mathcal{E},\ \overline{Y}_{i,j}\mapsto\mathcal{Y}_{i,j}\,,

which we also denote by
\psi
.
By the uniqueness of the Bruhat decomposition the inverse
\psi^{-1}
maps the Bruhat decomposition of
\mathcal{Y}
to the Bruhat decomposition of
\overline{Y}
from Proposition
6.5
.
More precisely, we have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\psi^{-1}(\mathcal{Y})&\!=&\!\psi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}))\\[5.0pt]
&\!=&\!\boldsymbol{u}(\psi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}))\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\psi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\exp}))\,\boldsymbol{u}(\psi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}))\\[5.0pt]
&\!=&\!\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w})=\overline{Y}\,,\end{array}

from which we conclude that
\psi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{v})=(x_{1},\dots,x_{l})
,
\psi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\exp})=\boldsymbol{z}
and that
\psi^{-1}(\mathrm{int}_{i})=w_{i}
fo all
1\leq i\leq m
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
.

We are going to prove that there exist exponents
a_{j,k}
,
b_{j,k}
,
c_{i,k}
in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that

\begin{array}[]{ll}\check{z}_{j}\,:=\,\exp_{j}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{k})^{a_{j,k}}&\quad\text{for}\ 1\leq j\leq l\,,\\[5.0pt]
\check{x}_{j}\,:=\,v_{j}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{k})^{b_{j,k}}&\quad\text{for}\ 1\leq j\leq l\,,\\[5.0pt]
\check{w}_{i}\,:=\,\mathrm{int}_{i}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{k})^{c_{i,k}}&\quad\text{for}\ 1\leq i\leq m\ \text{with}\ \beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\end{array}

are in the Picard-Vessiot ring
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathcal{Y}]
.
To this end, we refer to the proof of Proposition
D.2
and
D.3
, where
e_{1},\dots,e_{l}
of Proposition
6.5
are defined as the images of
\exp^{\rm ass}_{1},\dots,\exp^{\rm ass}_{l}
under
\psi^{-1}
.
According to Proposition
6.5
, the elements
e_{1},\dots,e_{l}
are contained in
C[G]
and
z_{1},\dots,z_{l}
are products of powers of
e_{1},\dots,e_{l}
with exponents in
\mathbb{Z}
.
Moreover, the same proposition yields that
x_{1},\dots,x_{l}
as well as those
w_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq m
such that
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
are
in the localization
\mathcal{M}^{-1}C[G]
, where
\mathcal{M}
is generated by
e_{1},\dots,e_{l}
.
We conclude that we can multiply
z_{1},\dots,z_{l}
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{l}
as well as
w_{i}
with suitable non-negative powers of
e_{1},\dots,e_{l}
to obtain elements in
C[G]
. Applying now
\psi
to these products yields the elements
\check{z}_{j}
,
\check{x}_{j}
and
\check{w}_{i}
in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathcal{Y}]
as claimed.

Finally, we are going to prove that the
\check{z}_{j}
,
\check{x}_{j}
and
\check{w}_{i}
as well as the
\exp^{\rm ass}_{j}
lie in the Picard-Vessiot ring

C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}\subset\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}

for the least common left multiple. This will then show the assertion of the supplement.
Theorem
6.3
implies that the
v_{j}
,
\exp_{j}
and
\mathrm{int}_{i}
are elements of
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
and with the
\exp_{j}
also the
\exp^{\rm ass}_{j}
are in
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
by Proposition
3.5
. Hence, the elements
\check{z}_{j}
,
\check{x}_{j}
and
\check{w}_{i}
are in
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
.
Since
\check{z}_{j}
,
\check{x}_{j}
and
\check{w}_{i}
lie in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathcal{Y}]
, they satisfy a linear differential equation over
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
according to
[
vdPS03
, Corollary 1.38]
. Clearly, the
\exp^{\rm ass}_{j}
also satisfy a linear differential equation over
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
.
Since
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle\subset C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
, the elements
\check{z}_{j}
,
\check{x}_{j}
,
\check{w}_{i}
and the
\exp^{\rm ass}_{j}

trivially satisfy a linear differential equation over
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
and so
the previous reference implies that they lie in the Picard-Vessiot ring

C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}

for the least common left multiple.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

We briefly recall that for any differential system

(39)

p_{1}=0,\quad\ldots,\quad p_{r}=0,\quad q_{1}\neq 0,\quad\ldots,\quad q_{s}\neq 0,

defined over a differential field
K
of characteristic zero,
where
p_{1},\ldots,p_{r},q_{1},\ldots,q_{s}\in K\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\}

are differential polynomials, a
Thomas decomposition
can be
computed in finitely many steps, which consists of finitely many
simple differential systems
S_{1}
, …,
S_{k}
such that the
solution set of (
39
) in formal power series
is the disjoint union
of the solutions set of
S_{1}
, …,
S_{k}
. This decomposition depends, in particular, on a chosen ranking on
K\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\}
.
Using an elimination ranking
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\gg x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{m}
produces simple differential systems with the property that those equations that do not
involve
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
generate all equations not involving
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
implied by the system.

Remark 6.8
.

We are going to explain how one can compute the rational functions of Proposition
6.6

(a)
,
(b)
and
(c)
such that they have the properties stated in the supplement.

Let
B_{G}
and
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
be as in Remark
6.4
. The process explained in that remark yields a basis
y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
of the solution space for

\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y\,=\,0

from
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
.
We repeat now the same construction with differential indeterminates
\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n}
over
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
instead of
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
.
More precisely, let

h_{1}(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n}),\quad\dots,\quad h_{n}(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n})

from Remark
6.4
be the linear differential polynomials in
\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n}
with coefficients in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
such that their evaluations
h_{1}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}),\dots,h_{n}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
are equal to the basis elements in (
37
).
We renumber
h_{1}(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n}),\dots,h_{n}(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n})
such that the first
n_{I^{\prime\prime}}
linear differential polynomials
are the ones defining the basis

y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}=h_{1}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}),\quad\dots,\quad y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}=h_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})

from Remark
6.4
.
We define with new differential indeterminates
\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
over
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
the differential polynomials

\displaystyle\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1}-h_{1}(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n}),\quad\ldots,\quad\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}-h_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n})

\displaystyle\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n},\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}.

Next we apply the linear change of variables

(40)

B_{G}^{-1}\mathrm{wr}(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n})\,=\,Y,\quad\text{where}\ Y=(Y_{i,j}),

to the defining ideal
I_{G}\unlhd C[Y,\det(Y)^{-1}]=C[\mathrm{GL}_{n}]
of
G
and obtain the ideal

I_{G}^{\rm comp}\unlhd C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\}[\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{1}^{(n-1)},\dots,\hat{y}_{n},\dots,\hat{y}_{n}^{(n-1)}].

Moreover, let
\overline{Y}=(\overline{Y}_{i,j})
be the matrix whose entries are the residue classes of
Y_{i,j}
in

C[Y,\det(Y)^{-1}]/I_{G}.

Compute the Bruhat decomposition of
\overline{Y}
and obtain for its coefficients
\boldsymbol{x}
,
\boldsymbol{z}
and
\boldsymbol{w}
rational functions in
\overline{Y}_{i,j}

as in Proposition
6.5
.
Recall from the proof of
Proposition
6.6
that
\psi
maps these rational functions to their corresponding counterpart in the Bruhat decomposition of
\mathcal{Y}
.
Moreover, the images under
\psi
of
e_{1},\dots,e_{l}
and
the numerators of
x_{1},\dots,x_{l}
,
z_{1},\dots,z_{l}
and of
w_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq m
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
are

\exp^{\rm ass}_{1},\dots,\exp^{\rm ass}_{l}
and
\check{x}_{1},\dots,\check{x}_{l}
,
\check{z}_{1},\dots,\check{z}_{l}
and
\check{w}_{i}
in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\}
(cf. the proof of Proposition
6.6
).
We are going to explain how one can compute a representation
of the latter elements
in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\}
using their preimages in
C[\overline{Y}_{i,j}]
.
To this purpose, let
f
be one of the above elements in
C[\overline{Y}_{i,j}]
and let
\check{f}
be its corresponding image in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\}
.
We apply the linear change of variables (
40
) to
f
and obtain
\tilde{f}\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle\{\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n}\}
. We compute now by differential elimination the intersection of the differential ideal generated by the generators of
I_{G}^{\rm comp}
and by

\begin{array}[]{ll}x-\tilde{f}(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n})\,,&\\[5.0pt]
\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}-h_{i}(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n})\,,&\quad i=1,\ldots,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}\,,\\[5.0pt]
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,\hat{y}_{j}\,,&\quad j=1,\ldots,n\,,\\[5.0pt]
L_{s}(\partial)\,\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}\,,&\quad\text{if}\ \hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}\ \text{is a solution of the factor}\ L_{s}(\partial),\,s=1,\ldots,k\,,\end{array}

with the differential polynomial ring
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{x,\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}
.
Using an appropriate elimination ranking as

\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n}\gg x\gg\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}

we obtain for each ideal a differential polynomial of the form

x-d

with
d\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}
.
Note that in general the differential Thomas decomposition returns several simple systems.
By substituting the basis elements
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
,
y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
of Remark
6.4
for
\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n}
,
\hat{y}_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,\hat{y}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
into the equations and inequations of the simple systems and taking one without a contradiction, one finds a valid relation
x-d=0
.
The differential polynomial
d
defines a differential polynomial in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\boldsymbol{Z}\}
with the property that when we substitute for
\boldsymbol{Z}
the basis elements
y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
we obtain
\check{f}
.
If
f
was
e_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq l
, then
d
defines the differential polynomial
E_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})
in the supplement of Proposition
6.6
.
If
f
was the numerator of
x_{j}
,
z_{j}
or
w_{i}
, then dividing the differential polynomial in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\boldsymbol{Z}\}
defined by
d
by the respective product of powers of
E_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z}),\dots,E_{l}(\boldsymbol{Z})
with exponents in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
yields the rational function

\mathrm{EXP}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})
,
V_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})
or
\mathrm{INT}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{Z})

of Proposition
6.6
having the properties of the supplement.

Example 6.9
.

For
G=\mathrm{SL}_{4}
the normal form operator is

L_{\mathrm{SL}_{4}}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,=\,\partial^{4}-s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v})\partial^{2}-s_{2}(\boldsymbol{v})\partial-s_{3}(\boldsymbol{v})

with coefficients

\begin{array}[]{rcl}s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v})&\!=&\!v_{3}^{2}+v_{2}^{2}-v_{1}v_{2}-v_{2}v_{3}+v_{1}^{2}+v_{1}^{\prime}+v_{2}^{\prime}+v_{3}^{\prime},\\[1.99997pt]
s_{2}(\boldsymbol{v})&\!=&\!4v_{1}v_{1}^{\prime}-v_{2}v_{1}^{\prime}-2v_{1}v_{2}^{\prime}+2v_{2}v_{2}^{\prime}-v_{2}v_{3}^{\prime}-v_{1}v_{2}^{2}\,+\\[1.99997pt]
&&\!v_{2}^{2}v_{3}+v_{1}^{2}v_{2}-v_{2}v_{3}^{2}+2v_{1}^{\prime\prime}+v_{2}^{\prime\prime},\\[1.99997pt]
s_{3}(\boldsymbol{v})&\!=&\!v_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}+2v_{1}v_{1}^{\prime\prime}-v_{1}v_{2}^{\prime\prime}+2(v_{1}^{\prime})^{2}+2v_{1}v_{2}v_{1}^{\prime}-v_{2}^{2}v_{1}^{\prime}\,+\\[1.99997pt]
&&\!v_{2}v_{3}v_{1}^{\prime}-v_{3}^{2}v_{1}^{\prime}-v_{1}^{\prime}v_{2}^{\prime}-v_{1}^{\prime}v_{3}^{\prime}+v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{\prime}-2v_{1}v_{2}v_{2}^{\prime}\,-\\[1.99997pt]
&&\!v_{1}^{2}v_{3}^{\prime}+v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}^{\prime}+v_{1}^{2}v_{2}v_{3}-v_{1}^{2}v_{3}^{2}-v_{1}v_{2}^{2}v_{3}+v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}^{2}.\end{array}

We consider here the case where
I^{\prime}=\{2,3\}
and
I^{\prime\prime}=\{1\}
. The longest Weyl group element
\overline{w}
maps
-\alpha_{2}
,
-\alpha_{3}
to
\alpha_{2}
,
\alpha_{1}
and so we have
J=\{1,2\}
. Over
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{1}\rangle

the normal form operator has the irreducible factorization
L_{\mathrm{SL}_{4}}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)=L_{1}(\partial)L_{2}(\partial)
with

\begin{array}[]{l}L_{1}(\partial):=\partial^{3}+v_{1}\partial^{2}-(s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v})-v_{1}^{2}-3v_{1}^{\prime})\partial-s_{2}(\boldsymbol{v})-v_{1}s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v})+3v_{1}^{\prime\prime}+5v_{1}v_{1}^{\prime}+v_{1}^{3},\\[1.99997pt]
L_{2}(\partial):=\partial-v_{1}.\end{array}

Moreover, with
J=\{1,2\}
we find that among the coefficients
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}
of the Bruhat decomposition of
\mathcal{Y}
the elements
\mathrm{int}_{3}
,
\mathrm{int}_{5}
and
\mathrm{int}_{6}
are not in the fixed field
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
.
From the coefficients of the Bruhat decomposition of
\overline{Y}
we obtain the following rational functions in
C(\overline{Y}_{i,j})
which correspond to
\exp^{\rm ass}_{1},\exp^{\rm ass}_{2},\exp^{\rm ass}_{3}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\mathrm{int}_{1}
,
\mathrm{int}_{2}
,
\mathrm{int}_{4}
, respectively:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}e_{1}&\!=&\!\overline{Y}_{1,4}\,,\quad e_{2}\,=\,\overline{Y}_{1,3}\overline{Y}_{2,4}-\overline{Y}_{1,4}\overline{Y}_{2,3}\,,\\[5.0pt]
e_{3}&\!=&\!\overline{Y}_{1,2}\overline{Y}_{2,3}\overline{Y}_{3,4}-\overline{Y}_{1,2}\overline{Y}_{2,4}\overline{Y}_{3,3}-\overline{Y}_{1,3}\overline{Y}_{2,2}\overline{Y}_{3,4}+\overline{Y}_{1,3}\overline{Y}_{2,4}\overline{Y}_{3,2}\,+\\[1.99997pt]
&&\!\overline{Y}_{1,4}\overline{Y}_{2,2}\overline{Y}_{3,3}-\overline{Y}_{1,4}\overline{Y}_{2,3}\overline{Y}_{3,2}\,,\\[5.0pt]
z_{1}&\!=&\!1/e_{3}\,,\quad z_{2}\,=\,1/e_{2}\,,\quad z_{3}\,=\,1/e_{1}\,,\\[5.0pt]
w_{1}&\!=&\!\displaystyle\frac{1}{e_{3}}(\overline{Y}_{1,1}\overline{Y}_{2,3}\overline{Y}_{3,4}-\overline{Y}_{1,1}\overline{Y}_{2,4}\overline{Y}_{3,3}-\overline{Y}_{1,3}\overline{Y}_{2,1}\overline{Y}_{3,4}+\overline{Y}_{1,3}\overline{Y}_{2,4}\overline{Y}_{3,1}\,+\\[5.0pt]
&&\!\overline{Y}_{1,4}\overline{Y}_{2,1}\overline{Y}_{3,3}-\overline{Y}_{1,4}\overline{Y}_{2,3}\overline{Y}_{3,1})\,,\\[5.0pt]
w_{2}&\!=&\!\displaystyle\frac{\overline{Y}_{1,2}\overline{Y}_{2,4}-\overline{Y}_{1,4}\overline{Y}_{2,2}}{e_{2}}\,,\quad w_{4}\,=\,\frac{\overline{Y}_{1,1}\overline{Y}_{2,4}-\overline{Y}_{1,4}\overline{Y}_{2,1}}{e_{2}}\,.\end{array}

Let
f
be one of the
e_{i}
or one of the numerators of

w_{j}
and perform the substitution of variables
\overline{Y}\mapsto\mathrm{wr}(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n})
to it.
Then the next step is the computation of the differential Thomas decomposition of the differential ideal in

C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{1}\}\{x,\hat{y}_{i},\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{i}\mid i=1,2,3,4\}

generated by

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}x-\tilde{f}(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n}),\\[5.0pt]
\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1}-L_{2}(\partial)\hat{y}_{1},\ \hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{2}-L_{2}(\partial)\hat{y}_{2},\ \hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{3}-L_{2}(\partial)\hat{y}_{3},\ \hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{4}-\hat{y}_{4},\\[5.0pt]
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,\hat{y}_{1},\ L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,\hat{y}_{2},L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,\hat{y}_{3},L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,\hat{y}_{4},\\[5.0pt]
L_{1}(\partial)\,\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},L_{1}(\partial)\,\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{2},L_{1}(\partial)\,\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{3},L_{2}(\partial)\,\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{4}\quad\text{and}\\[5.0pt]
\det(\mathrm{wr}(\hat{y}_{1},\hat{y}_{2},\hat{y}_{3},\hat{y}_{4}))-1.\end{array}

The numbering of the
\hat{y}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
is chosen according to the numbering of the first row
y_{1},\dots,y_{4}
in
\mathcal{Y}
. Note that
y_{4}
is a solution of
L_{2}(\partial)
and that
L_{2}(\partial)\,y_{1}
,
L_{2}(\partial)\,y_{2}
and
L_{2}(\partial)\,y_{3}
are solutions of
L_{1}(\partial)
. Using the elimination ranking defined by

\hat{y}_{1}>\hat{y}_{2}>\hat{y}_{3}>\hat{y}_{4}\gg x\gg\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1}>\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{2}>\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{3}>\hat{y}^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{4}\,,

the Thomas decompositions for these ideals consist of a single simple differential system.
In this system we find the linear relation of the form
x-d
with
d\in C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{1}\}\{\hat{y}_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\hat{y}_{2}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\hat{y}_{3}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\hat{y}_{4}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\}
. For the different choices of
f
, we find
d
as

\begin{array}[]{cl}\hat{y}_{4}^{I^{\prime\prime}}&\text{if}\,f=e_{1}\,,\\[5.0pt]
-\hat{y}_{3}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\hat{y}_{4}^{I^{\prime\prime}}&\text{if}\,f=e_{2}\,,\\[5.0pt]
\hat{y}_{2}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{y}_{3}^{I^{\prime\prime}})^{\prime}\hat{y}_{4}^{I^{\prime\prime}}-(\hat{y}_{2}^{I^{\prime\prime}})^{\prime}\hat{y}_{3}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\hat{y}_{4}^{I^{\prime\prime}}&\text{if}\,f=e_{3}\,,\\[5.0pt]
\hat{y}_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{y}_{3}^{I^{\prime\prime}})^{\prime}\hat{y}_{4}^{I^{\prime\prime}}-(\hat{y}_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}})^{\prime}\hat{y}_{3}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\hat{y}_{4}^{I^{\prime\prime}}&\text{if}\,f=w_{1}\,,\\[5.0pt]
-\hat{y}_{2}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\hat{y}_{4}^{I^{\prime\prime}}&\text{if}\,f=w_{2}\,,\\[5.0pt]
-\hat{y}_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\hat{y}_{4}^{I^{\prime\prime}}&\text{if}\,f=w_{4}\,.\end{array}

Replacing in the first three differential polynomials the indeterminates
\hat{y}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
by
Z_{i}
, we obtain

E_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z})
,
E_{2}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
E_{3}(\boldsymbol{Z})
. Performing the same replacement in the last three differential polynomials and dividing by
E_{3}(\boldsymbol{Z})
,
E_{2}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
E_{2}(\boldsymbol{Z})
respectively, we obtain
\mathrm{INT}_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z})
,
\mathrm{INT}_{2}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
\mathrm{INT}_{4}(\boldsymbol{Z})
. Moreover, we have

\mathrm{EXP}_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z})\,=\,\frac{1}{E_{3}(\boldsymbol{Z})},\quad\mathrm{EXP}_{2}(\boldsymbol{Z})\,=\,\frac{1}{E_{2}(\boldsymbol{Z})},\quad\mathrm{EXP}_{3}(\boldsymbol{Z})\,=\,\frac{1}{E_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z})}

and as
V_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z})
,
V_{2}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
V_{3}(\boldsymbol{Z})
we take the logarithmic derivative of
E_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z})
,
E_{2}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
E_{3}(\boldsymbol{Z})
respectively.

Remark 6.10
.

Continuing the discussion of Remark
6.7
,
each simple differential system
S_{i}
in a Thomas decomposition
admits an effective membership test to its associated radical differential ideal. More precisely,
let
\mathcal{I}(S_{i}^{=})
be the differential ideal of the differential polynomial ring

K\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\}
which is generated by the left hand sides of the equations in
S_{i}
.
Let
q
be the product of the initials and separants of the equations in
S_{i}
(determined by the ranking).
Then iterated pseudo-reductions of a given differential polynomial

p\in K\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\}
modulo the left hand sides of the equations in
S_{i}
decides whether
p
belongs to the radical differential ideal
\mathcal{I}(S_{i}^{=}):q^{\infty}
.
For more details we refer to
[
Rob14
]
.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
\varphi
be the differential homomorphism

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\varphi\colon C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{\boldsymbol{Z}\}&\to&C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}\,,\\[5.0pt]
\boldsymbol{Z}=(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})&\mapsto&(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})\,.\end{array}

Then there exist finitely many
\mathrm{REL}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{REL}_{k}\in C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{\boldsymbol{Z}\}
such that

\ker(\varphi)\,=\,\mathcal{I}:(\mathrm{REL}^{\neq})^{\infty}\,,

where
\mathcal{I}
is the differential ideal generated by

\mathrm{REL}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{REL}_{k}
and where
\mathrm{REL}^{\neq}
is the product of the initials and separants of these differential polynomials, defined with respect to a chosen
ranking on
C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{\boldsymbol{Z}\}
.

Proof.

Since
C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}
is contained in
\mathcal{E}
, it is an integral domain and so
\ker(\varphi)
is a prime differential ideal.

According to the proof of Theorem
6.3
, a basis

y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
of the solution space of

\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y\,=\,0

is obtained by applying the operators

L_{k}(\partial),\quad L_{k-1}(\partial)\circ L_{k}(\partial),\quad\dots,\quad L_{2}(\partial)\circ\cdots\circ L_{k}(\partial)\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle[\partial]

to the basis elements
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
of the solution space of the normal form operator. Let
\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1}=(\exp_{1}^{-1},\dots,\exp_{l}^{-1})
.
Since
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
are elements of

C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext},\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}\},

which is closed under these operators, the basis elements

y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
are also elements of this differential ring and so
Theorem
6.3

(b)
implies that they are even elements of

C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext},\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\}\,.

Hence, we have

(41)

\begin{array}[]{l}C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}\\[5.0pt]
\qquad\subset C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext},\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\}\,.\end{array}

Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}=(\widetilde{v}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{v}_{l})
,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}}=(\widetilde{\exp}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\exp}_{l})
and
\widetilde{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
be differential indeterminates over
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
and denote by
R
the differential ring

C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}}^{-1},\widetilde{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\}\,.

The integrand of
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with index
i
such that
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
is a polynomial expression

\mathrm{integrand}_{i}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\mathrm{int}_{j})

in the elements
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1}
and
\mathrm{int}_{j}
with indices
j
such that
\beta_{j}\in\Psi^{-}
and

|0pt(\beta_{j})|<|0pt(\beta_{i})|
.
Then the differential ideal
Q
in
R
generated by

\begin{array}[]{rl}\widetilde{\exp}^{\prime}_{i}\,\widetilde{\exp}^{-1}_{i}-g_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}})&\quad\text{for}\ i=1,\dots,l\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\widetilde{v}_{i}-v_{i}&\quad\text{for}\ v_{i}\in\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
s_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}})-s_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})&\quad\text{for}\ i=1,\dots,l\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\widetilde{\mathrm{int}}_{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{integrand}_{i}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}}^{-1},\widetilde{\mathrm{int}}_{j})&\quad\text{for}\ \beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\,,\end{array}

is the kernel of the surjective differential
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
-homomorphism

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext},\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\}

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\widetilde{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\exp},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}).

Thus
Q
is a prime differential ideal, since
R/Q
is isomorphic to an integral domain, and we obtain a differential isomorphism

\iota\colon C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext},\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\}\to R/Q\,.

We conclude with (
41
) that the problem reduces to computing the kernel of the differential homomorphism

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\varphi\colon C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{\boldsymbol{Z}\}&\to&R/Q,\\[5.0pt]
\boldsymbol{Z}=(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})&\mapsto&(\bar{y}_{1},\dots,\bar{y}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})\,,\end{array}

where
\bar{y}_{1},\dots,\bar{y}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
are the images of
y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
under
\iota
.
Let
\widetilde{y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
be the expressions obtained by replacing
in
y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
the elements
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1}
,
\mathrm{int}_{i}
by
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}}^{-1}
,
\widetilde{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
for
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
.
Moreover, let
\widetilde{Q}
be the differential ideal in

C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{\boldsymbol{Z},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}}^{-1},\widetilde{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\}

generated by the generators of
Q
and the numerators of

Z_{1}-\widetilde{y}_{1},\quad\ldots,\quad Z_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}-\widetilde{y}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\,.

Since
Q
is a prime differential ideal and the other generators of
\widetilde{Q}
are linear in the differential indeterminates
Z_{1}
, …,
Z_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
, we conclude that
\widetilde{Q}
is also prime.
We compute a Thomas decomposition
of the differential system defined by
\widetilde{Q}

with respect to an elimination ranking on
C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{\boldsymbol{Z},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}}^{-1},\widetilde{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\}
with

(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\exp}}^{-1},\widetilde{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-})\gg(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\boldsymbol{Z})\,,

extending a chosen ranking on the second block.
Since
\widetilde{Q}
is prime, the resulting Thomas decomposition contains a
uniquely determined generic simple system (cf.
[
Rob14
, Subsect. 2.2.3]
).
Now
\widetilde{Q}
is the intersection of the radical differential ideals defined by the simple differential systems of the Thomas decomposition.
Moreover, each of these differential ideals contains the differential ideal defined by the generic simple system.
Hence,
\widetilde{Q}
is equal to the latter ideal.
Now
\ker(\varphi)
is the intersection of this ideal with
C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{\boldsymbol{Z}\}
.
Due to the choice of the elimination ranking, the
left hand sides
\mathrm{REL}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{REL}_{k}
of the equations
in the generic simple system which only involve the indeterminates
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}
yield differential polynomials as required.
∎

7.
Reduction of the Normal Form Matrix into the Lie Algebra of a Parabolic Subgroup

In Sections
5
and
6
we have seen that for any standard parabolic subgroup
P_{J}
the general extension field

\mathcal{E}
is a Picard-Vessiot extension of

\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}\,=\,F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle

for
A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
with differential Galois group
P_{J}(C)
. In this section we will show how to compute a matrix

g_{1}\in G(\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}})
such that
g_{1}\mathcal{Y}\in P_{J}(\mathcal{E})
and

g_{1}.A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))\in\Lie(P_{J})(\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}).

This achieves a reduction of the normal form matrix.
Let

\Delta^{\prime}_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\Delta^{\prime}_{d}\,=\,\{\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,\alpha_{i_{r}}\}

be the unique partition of the subset
\{\alpha_{i}\mid i\in I^{\prime}\}\subset\Delta
of simple roots
such that
\Delta^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\Delta^{\prime}_{d}
are bases of maximal irreducible root subsystems

\Phi^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\Phi^{\prime}_{d}
of
\Phi
.
Furthermore, we denote the roots of

\Phi^{-}\setminus(\Phi_{1}^{\prime-}\cup\dots\cup\Phi_{d}^{\prime-})\subset\{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{m}\}\,=\,\Phi^{-}

by
\beta_{k_{1}},\dots,\beta_{k_{s}}
, where we choose the numbering such that
k_{i}<k_{j}
for
i<j
.

Lemma 7.1
.

For
i
,
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
with
i\neq j
let
\alpha\in\Phi_{i}^{\prime-}
and
\beta\in\Phi_{j}^{\prime-}
. Then
\alpha+\beta
is not a root of
\Phi^{-}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\alpha+\beta
is a root of
\Phi^{-}
. By
[
Hum78
, Corollary 10.2]
,
\alpha+\beta
can be written as a sum of negative simple roots in such a way that each partial sum is a root. Hence, after exchanging the roles of
i
and
j
if necessary, there are simple roots
\alpha^{i}_{1},\dots,\alpha^{i}_{u}\in\Delta_{i}^{\prime}
occurring in the representation of
\alpha
as linear combination of simple roots, and there is a simple root
\alpha^{j}\in\Delta^{\prime}_{j}
occurring in
\beta
such that

-\alpha^{i}_{1}-\dots-\alpha^{i}_{u}-\alpha^{j}

is a root of
\Phi^{-}
. We claim that
\alpha^{i}_{1},\dots,\alpha^{i}_{u}
are orthogonal to
\alpha^{j}
. Suppose that there is
\alpha^{i}\in\{\alpha^{i}_{1},\dots,\alpha^{i}_{u}\}
such that
\alpha^{i}
and
\alpha^{j}
are not orthogonal.
Since the irreducibility of
\Phi_{i}^{\prime}
implies the irreducibility of
\Delta_{i}^{\prime}
, we conclude that if we adjoin
\alpha^{j}
to
\Delta_{i}^{\prime}
it is not possible to write
\{\alpha^{j}\}\cup\Delta_{i}^{\prime}
as a disjoint union of two sets such that each simple root in one set is orthogonal to each root in the other.
This means that
\{\alpha^{j}\}\cup\Delta_{i}^{\prime}
is irreducible, contradicting the fact that
\Delta_{i}^{\prime}
was maximally irreducible.
Hence,
\alpha^{i}_{1},\dots,\alpha^{i}_{u}
are orthogonal to
\alpha^{j}
and so
\langle\alpha^{i}_{1}+\dots+\alpha^{i}_{u},\alpha^{j}\rangle=0
. Then the image of
\alpha^{i}_{1}+\dots+\alpha^{i}_{u}+\alpha^{j}
under the reflection
\sigma_{\alpha^{j}}
is

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma_{\alpha^{j}}(\alpha^{i}_{1}+\dots+\alpha^{i}_{u}+\alpha^{j})&\!=&\!\alpha^{i}_{1}+\dots+\alpha^{i}_{u}+\alpha^{j}-\langle\alpha^{i}_{1}+\dots+\alpha^{i}_{u}+\alpha^{j},\alpha^{j}\rangle\,\alpha^{j}\\[1.99997pt]
&\!=&\!\alpha^{i}_{1}+\dots+\alpha^{i}_{u}+\alpha^{j}-\langle\alpha^{j},\alpha^{j}\rangle\,\alpha^{j}\\[1.99997pt]
&\!=&\!\alpha^{i}_{1}+\dots+\alpha^{i}_{u}-\alpha^{j}\end{array}

by linearity of the first argument of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and
\langle\alpha^{j},\alpha^{j}\rangle=2
.
Since the simple root
\alpha^{j}
is not among the simple roots
\alpha^{i}_{1}+\dots+\alpha^{i}_{u}
,
we obtain a root of
\Phi
which is the sum of simple roots whose coefficients have different signs,
in contradiction to the properties of a basis,
cf.
[
Hum78
, Chapter 10.1]
.
∎

Proposition 7.2
.

Recall from above that

\{\beta_{k_{1}},\dots,\beta_{k_{s}}\}\,=\,\Phi^{-}\setminus(\Phi_{1}^{\prime-}\cup\dots\cup\Phi_{d}^{\prime-})\,.

There exist
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}\in\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
such that

g_{1}\,:=\,n(\overline{w})^{-1}u_{\beta_{k_{s}}}(x_{k_{s}})\cdots u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})

satisfies
g_{1}\mathcal{Y}\in P_{J}(\mathcal{E})
and

g_{1}.A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))\in\Lie(P_{J})(\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}})
.
Moreover, we have

g_{1}\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\in U_{\Psi^{+}}(\mathcal{E})\leq P_{J}(\mathcal{E})\,,

where
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})
is the first factor in the Bruhat decomposition of
\mathcal{Y}
and
U_{\Psi^{+}}
is the product of root groups corresponding to the roots in
\Psi^{+}
.

Proof.

Recall that the negative roots
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{m}
are numbered in such a way that
|0pt(\beta_{i})|\leq|0pt(\beta_{j})|
for
i\leq j
and so the same holds for the roots
\beta_{k_{1}},\dots,\beta_{k_{s}}
.
We prove now by induction on
i=1,\dots,s
that there are elements
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{i}}\in\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
such that in the standard decomposition

u_{\beta_{k_{i}}}(x_{k_{i}})\cdots u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,=\,u_{\beta_{1}}(y_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(y_{m})

as the product of elements of all root groups
U_{\beta_{1}}
, …,
U_{\beta_{m}}
(in that order)
the parameters
y_{k_{1}},\dots,y_{k_{i}}
are all zero.
Before we start with the induction, note that by Lemma
5.2
, for any
g\in P_{J}(C)
we have

(42)

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})\,g\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,u_{g}\,n(\overline{w})\,b\,,

where the matrix
u_{g}
is a product of root group elements corresponding to the roots in
\Phi^{\prime-}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Phi^{\prime-}_{d}
with parameter values in
\mathcal{E}
and where
b\in B^{-}(\mathcal{E})
. We will also use the exchange formula (cf.
[
Car89
, Theorem 5.2.2]
)

(43)

u_{\beta^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})\,u_{\beta}(x)\,=\,u_{\beta}(x)\,u_{\beta^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})\prod_{a^{\prime},a>0}u_{a^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}+a\beta}(c_{a^{\prime},a,\beta,\beta^{\prime}}(-x)^{a^{\prime}}x^{\prime a})\,

for two roots
\beta
,
\beta^{\prime}\in\Phi^{-}
,
where the product is taken over all positive integers
a^{\prime}
,
a
such that
a^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}+a\beta\in\Phi^{-}
and where

c_{a^{\prime},a,\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Let
i=1
. Then
\beta_{k_{1}}
is a negative simple root. By (
42
) the parameter value
\tilde{y}_{k_{1}}
in the standard decomposition

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,u_{g}\,=\,u_{\beta_{1}}(\tilde{y}_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(\tilde{y}_{m})

of
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,u_{g}
is
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})_{k_{1}}
for every
g\in P_{J}
showing that
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})_{k_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
, where
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})_{k_{1}}
means the
k_{1}
-th entry in the tuple
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})
.
We apply now successively the exchange formula to
u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})
with
x_{k_{1}}=-(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})_{k_{1}}
until we obtain

u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,=\,u_{\beta_{1}}(y_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(y_{m})\,,

where
y_{k_{1}}=0
and
y_{j}=(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})_{j}
for all
\beta_{j}\in\Phi^{\prime-}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Phi^{\prime-}_{d}
.

Suppose the induction hypothesis holds for
i-1
, that is there exist elements
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{i-1}}\in\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
such that in the standard decomposition

u_{\beta_{k_{i-1}}}(x_{k_{i-1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,=\,u_{\beta_{1}}(y_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(y_{m})

the parameters
y_{k_{1}},\dots,y_{k_{i-1}}
are all zero. For
\sigma_{g}\in{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}})
we compute with (
42
) that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}u_{\beta_{1}}(\sigma_{g}(y_{1}))\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(\sigma_{g}(y_{m}))&\!=&\!\sigma_{g}(u_{\beta_{1}}(y_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(y_{m}))\\[5.0pt]
&\!=&\!\sigma_{g}(u_{\beta_{k_{i-1}}}(x_{k_{i-1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}))\\[5.0pt]
&\!=&\!u_{\beta_{k_{i-1}}}(x_{k_{i-1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})\sigma_{g}(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}))\\[5.0pt]
&\!=&\!u_{\beta_{k_{i-1}}}(x_{k_{i-1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})u_{g}\\[5.0pt]
&\!=&\!u_{\beta_{1}}(y_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(y_{m})\,u_{g}\,.\end{array}

The element
u_{g}
is a product of root group elements corresponding to the roots in
\Phi^{\prime-}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Phi^{\prime-}_{d}
(cf. (
42
)) and by Lemma
7.1
no intermediate product involves a root group element corresponding to a root in
\Phi^{-}\setminus(\Phi^{\prime-}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Phi^{\prime-}_{d})
.
Let
u_{\beta}
be the first factor in this product and let

u_{\beta_{1}}(y_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(y_{m})\,u_{\beta}\,=\,u_{\beta_{1}}(\tilde{y}_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(\tilde{y}_{m})

be the standard decomposition obtained by applying successively formula (
43
).
The parameter values among
y_{1},\dots,y_{m}
of the root group elements corresponding to all roots in
\Phi^{-}\setminus(\Phi^{\prime-}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Phi^{\prime-}_{d})
with height less than
|0pt(\beta_{k_{i}})|
are zero by induction hypothesis.
Therefore, applying successively formula (
43
) only affects the parameter values of the root group elements belonging to the roots in
\Phi^{\prime-}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Phi^{\prime-}_{d}
and to the roots in
\Phi^{-}\setminus(\Phi^{\prime-}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Phi^{\prime-}_{d})
with height greater than
|0pt(\beta_{k_{i}})|
in absolute value.
Using induction on the number of factors and the same reasoning as for the first factor shows that
\tilde{y}_{k_{i}}=y_{k_{i}}
in the standard decomposition

u_{\beta_{1}}(y_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(y_{m})\,u_{g}\,=\,u_{\beta_{1}}(\tilde{y}_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(\tilde{y}_{m})

and so
y_{k_{i}}\in\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
. Now we apply formula (
43
) to

u_{\beta_{k_{i}}}(-y_{k_{i}})\,u_{\beta_{k_{i-1}}}(x_{k_{i-1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,=\,u_{\beta_{k_{i}}}(-y_{k_{i}})\,u_{\beta_{1}}(y_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(y_{m}).

As above the successive application of formula (
43
) until one reaches the standard decomposition creates only new parameter values in root group elements belonging to roots in
\Phi^{-}\setminus(\Phi^{\prime-}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Phi^{\prime-}_{d})
with height greater than
|0pt(\beta_{k_{i}})|
and to
\beta_{k_{i}}
. In the latter case the parameter value becomes zero. This completes the induction.

The induction statement for
i=s
implies that in the standard decomposition

(44)

u_{\beta_{k_{s}}}(x_{k_{s}})\dots u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,=\,u_{\beta_{1}}(y_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(y_{m})

the parameter values
y_{k_{1}},\dots,y_{k_{s}}
are zero and so the right hand side of (
44
) is a product of root group elements corresponding only to roots in
\Phi^{\prime-}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Phi^{\prime-}_{d}
. Since the reflection
\sigma_{\overline{w}}
maps the roots in
\Phi^{\prime-}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Phi^{\prime-}_{d}
to roots in
\Psi^{+}
, we conclude with (
44
) that

n(\overline{w})^{-1}\,u_{\beta_{k_{s}}}(x_{k_{s}})\cdots u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\in U_{\Psi^{+}}(\mathcal{E})\leq P_{J}(\mathcal{E}).

Since
\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})
is clearly an element of
B^{-}(\mathcal{E})\subset P_{J}(\mathcal{E})
, we have that

g_{1}\mathcal{Y}\,=\,n(\overline{w})^{-1}\,u_{\beta_{k_{s}}}(x_{k_{s}})\cdots u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})\in P_{J}(\mathcal{E})

with
g_{1}:=n(\overline{w})\,u_{\beta_{k_{s}}}(x_{k_{s}})\cdots u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})\in G(\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}})
, where we recall that according to the induction statement
x_{k_{s}},\dots,x_{k_{1}}\in\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
.
Finally, we conclude with
g_{1}\mathcal{Y}\in P_{J}(\mathcal{E})
and Remark
4.2
that
