\displaystyle\ell\delta(g_{1}\mathcal{Y})\,=\,g_{1}.\ell\delta(\mathcal{Y})\,=\,g_{1}.A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))\in\Lie(P_{J})(\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}).

∎

Remark 7.3
.

The parameter values

x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}\in\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}=C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle

for the root groups
U_{\beta_{k_{1}}},\dots,U_{\beta_{k_{s}}}
of Proposition
7.2
can be algorithmically determined.
Indeed, one successively multiplies
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})
from the left with

u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}}),\dots,u_{\beta_{k_{s}}}(x_{k_{s}})

and applies effectively in each multiplication step multiple times the exchange formula until one obtains a standard decomposition. If

u_{\beta_{k_{i-1}}}(x_{k_{i-1}})\dots u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}(x_{k_{1}})\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,=\,u_{\beta_{1}}(y_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(y_{m})

is the standard decomposition in the
(i-1)
-st multiplication step, then according to the proof of Proposition
7.2
the parameter value
x_{k_{i}}
for the
i
-th multiplication step is simply
-y_{k_{i}}
.
Since applying the exchange formula only involves operations in
C[\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}]
and the
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}
are invariant, we conclude that

x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}\in C[\boldsymbol{p}]\,=\,\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}\cap C[\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}]\subset C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}\,.

Now we use Thomas decomposition to determine representations of
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}
in
C[\boldsymbol{p}]
.
More precisely, for differential indeterminates
\widehat{\boldsymbol{p}}=(\widehat{p}_{1},\dots,\widehat{p}_{q})
compute the normal form of
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}
with respect to the differential ideal in
C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
generated by

\widehat{p}_{1}-p_{1},\quad\dots,\quad\widehat{p}_{q}-p_{q}\in C\{\boldsymbol{v},\widehat{\boldsymbol{p}}\}

and an elimination ranking
\boldsymbol{v}\gg\widehat{\boldsymbol{p}}
. We obtain expressions for
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}
in
C[\widehat{\boldsymbol{p}}]
.
According to Remark
5.7
we can compute for
1\leq i\leq q
elements
p_{1,i}
and
p_{2,i}
in
C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}
such that
p_{i}=p_{1,i}/p_{2,i}
.
Thus, if we substitute in
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}
for the variables

\widehat{p}_{i}
the rational functions
p_{1,i}/p_{2,i}
, we obtain representations of
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}
as rational functions in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
. Note that the denominators of
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}
are in the multiplicatively closed subset of
C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}
generated by
p_{2,1},\dots,p_{2,q}.

We determine the Levi decomposition of the matrix
g_{1}\mathcal{Y}\in P_{J}(\mathcal{E})
, where
g_{1}
is as in Proposition
7.2
. It will be the uniquely determined product

g_{1}\mathcal{Y}\,=\,(g_{1}\mathcal{Y}_{\rm red})\cdot\mathcal{Y}_{\rm rad}

with
g_{1}\mathcal{Y}_{\rm red}\in L_{J}(\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})})
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\rm rad}\in R_{u}(P_{J})(\mathcal{E})
with entries in
\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
, where
L_{J}
is the standard Levi group of
P_{J}
.
To this end, we denote the roots of
\Psi^{-}
by

(45)

\{\beta_{j_{1}},\dots,\beta_{j_{k}}\}\,=\,\Psi^{-}

and the roots of the complement
\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
by

(46)

\{\beta_{j_{k+1}},\dots,\beta_{j_{m}}\}\,=\,\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}\,.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
be indeterminates over
C
. We have a unique factorization

(47)

u_{\beta_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots u_{\beta_{m}}(x_{m})\,=\,u_{\beta_{j_{1}}}(y_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{k}}}(y_{j_{k}})\cdot u_{\beta_{j_{k+1}}}(y_{j_{k+1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{m}}}(y_{j_{m}})

with
y_{j_{1}}=x_{j_{1}},\dots,y_{j_{k}}=x_{j_{k}}
and
y_{j_{k+1}},\dots,y_{j_{m}}\in C[\boldsymbol{x}]\setminus C[x_{j_{1}},\dots,x_{j_{k}}]
.

Proof.

Let
\beta\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
. Moreover, let
\widetilde{\beta}_{1}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
and
\widetilde{\beta}_{2}\in\Psi^{-}
. If for
a^{\prime},a>0
the sum
a^{\prime}\beta+a\widetilde{\beta}_{i}
is a root, then
a^{\prime}\beta+a\widetilde{\beta}_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
.
Thus applying the exchange formula (
43
) to a root group element corresponding to a root in
\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
until all factors belonging to roots in
\Psi^{-}
have been moved to the left of it, creates only new factors belonging again to roots in
\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
.
Hence, in each step the parameters of the factors corresponding to the roots of
\Psi^{-}
are unchanged.
The exchange formula (
43
) implies that the parameters of the newly created factors are monomials of degree greater than one.
We conclude that the parameter
y_{j}
of a factor in the final product (
47
) belonging to a root
\beta_{j}
with
j\in\{j_{k+1},\dots,j_{m}\}
is the sum of the indeterminate
x_{j}
and a polynomial of degree greater than one in
C[\boldsymbol{x}]
and so
y_{j}\in C[\boldsymbol{x}]\setminus C[x_{j_{1}},\dots,x_{j_{k}}]
.

The uniqueness of the factorization follows from the fact that the product map

\displaystyle U_{\beta_{k_{1}}}\times\dots\times U_{\beta_{k_{j}}}\times U_{\beta_{k_{j+1}}}\times U_{\beta_{k_{m}}}\to U^{-},

\displaystyle(u_{\beta_{k_{1}}},\dots,u_{\beta_{k_{j}}},u_{\beta_{k_{j+1}}},\dots,u_{\beta_{k_{m}}})\mapsto u_{\beta_{k_{1}}}\cdots u_{\beta_{k_{j}}}u_{\beta_{k_{j+1}}}\cdots u_{\beta_{k_{m}}}

is an isomorphism of varieties (cf.
[
Hum75
, end of Section 28.5]
).
∎

Remark 7.5
.

Applying Lemma
7.4
to the matrix
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})
,
we obtain the decomposition

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})\,=\,u_{\beta_{j_{1}}}(\mathrm{int}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{k}}}(\mathrm{int}_{j_{k}})\cdot u_{\beta_{j_{k+1}}}(y_{j_{k+1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{m}}}(y_{j_{m}})

with
y_{j_{k+1}},\dots,y_{j_{m}}\in C[\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]\setminus C[\mathrm{int}_{j_{1}},\dots,\mathrm{int}_{j_{k}}]
.
Since
\{\beta_{j_{1}},\dots,\beta_{j_{k}}\}=\Psi^{-}
, it follows that

u_{\beta_{j_{1}}}(\mathrm{int}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{k}}}(\mathrm{int}_{j_{k}})\in U_{\Psi^{-}}

and from Theorem
6.3

(b)
that
\mathrm{int}_{j_{1}},\dots,\mathrm{int}_{j_{k}}
are elements of
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
.
We define

\mathcal{Y}_{\rm red}\,:=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,u_{j_{1}}(\mathrm{int}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{j_{k}}(\mathrm{int}_{j_{k}})\in G(\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}).

Moreover,
g_{1}\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})n(\overline{w})\in U_{\Psi^{+}}(\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})})
according to Proposition
7.2
and so, because
\Psi
is the root system of the Levi group
L_{J}
, we have

g_{1}\mathcal{Y}_{\rm red}\in L_{J}(\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}).

Since the unipotent radical
R_{u}(P_{J})
is the direct product of root groups corresponding to the roots in
\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}=\{\beta_{j_{k+1}},\dots,\beta_{j_{m}}\}
, we conclude that

\mathcal{Y}_{\rm rad}\,:=\,u_{\beta_{j_{k+1}}}(y_{j_{k+1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{m}}}(y_{j_{m}})\in R_{u}(P_{J})(\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})})\,,

because
C[\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]\setminus C[\mathrm{int}_{j_{1}},\dots,\mathrm{int}_{j_{k}}]\subset\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J}})
.
Clearly the Levi decomposition of
g_{1}\mathcal{Y}
is

g_{1}\mathcal{Y}\,=\,g_{1}\mathcal{Y}_{\rm red}\cdot\mathcal{Y}_{\rm rad}\,.

Part III
Computing the Galois Group of a Specialized Normal Form

8.
A Parabolic Bound for the Differential Galois Group of a Specialization

Let
\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}=(\overline{s}_{1},\dots,\overline{s}_{l})
be
l
rational functions in
F=C(z)
and let

\sigma_{0}\colon C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\}\to F,\,\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\mapsto\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}

be the differential homomorphism
which specializes the differentially algebraically independent polynomials
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})
to
\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}
.
We consider now the specialized normal form matrix

\sigma_{0}(A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})))=A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}).

Assumption 1
.

We assume that the rational functions
\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}
are chosen such that
no denominator of the coefficients of the associated operators and of their Riccati equations of Definition
3.4
specializes under
\sigma_{0}
to zero.

Remark 8.1
.

Assumption
1
guarantees that we can also apply
\sigma_{0}
to the normal form equation
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
.
Indeed, the coefficients of
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
are the entries of the last row of the companion matrix
B_{G}.A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
.
Since we have
B_{G}\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\})
by Proposition
A.2
, it follows that the coefficients of
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
are elements of the differential ring
C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\}
.

In this section we are going to determine a standard parabolic subgroup
P_{J}(C)
which will contain the differential Galois group
H(C)
. To this end, we will present an algorithm which determines a partition
I=I^{\prime}\cup I^{\prime\prime}
as introduced in Part
II
for the specialized normal form matrix
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
.
The algorithm uses
F
-rational solutions of the specialized Riccati equations corresponding to the specialized associated operators.

Consider the two differential ideals

S\,:=\,\langle s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v})-\overline{s}_{1},\dots,s_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})-\overline{s}_{l}\rangle

and

S_{\mathrm{Ric}}\,:=\,\langle\mathrm{Ric}_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{Ric}_{l}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{l})\rangle

of
F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
, where

\mathrm{Ric}_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{1}),\quad\dots,\quad\mathrm{Ric}_{l}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{l})\in F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}

are the differential polynomials obtained by replacing in the
i
-th Riccati polynomial

\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),y)
(cf. Definition
3.4
) the elements
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})
and
y
by
\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}
and
v_{i}
, respectively.

Lemma 8.2
.

The differential ideal
S
is prime.

Proof.

The proof of
[
RS23
, Proposition 7.1 (b)]
is easily adapted to the case of the ideal
S
, using the fact that each
s_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})
involves one term which is a derivative of a certain indeterminate
v_{j}
with constant coefficient.
Thus
F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}/S
is isomorphic to a polynomial ring in finitely many variables. Therefore,
F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}/S
is an integral domain and so
S
is prime.
∎

The relation between the solutions of the Riccati equations and the differential ideal
S
is given by the following proposition.

Proposition 8.3
.

The differential ideal
S_{\mathrm{Ric}}
is contained in
S
, i.e.
S_{\mathrm{Ric}}\subset S
.

Proof.

We show that the images of
\mathrm{Ric}_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{Ric}_{l}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{l})
under

\pi\colon F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}\to F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}/S,\quad v_{i}\mapsto v_{i}+S

are zero. But this follows easily from the fact that substituting the differential indeterminate
v_{i}
for
y
in
\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),y)
yields
0
.
Indeed, we then have

\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{i}+S)\,=\,\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})+S,v_{i}+S)\,=\,\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),v_{i})+S\,=\,0+S\,.

∎

Roughly speaking Proposition
8.3
means that the variety defined by
S
is contained in the variety defined by
S_{\mathrm{Ric}}
.
We are interested in common points of both varieties having the property that as many coordinates as possible are in the rational function field
F
.
To this end, we compute all rational solutions in
F
of the Riccati equations

\mathrm{Ric}_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{1})\,=\,0\,,\quad\dots,\quad\mathrm{Ric}_{l}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{l})\,=\,0

using the known algorithms. More precisely, it is possible to decide algorithmically whether or not a Riccati equation has a solution in
F
(cf.
[
vdPS03
, Proposition 4.9]
and
[
Bek94
]
,
[
Sch68
]
).
It is also possible to determine algorithmically all rational solutions of a Riccati equation with coefficients in
F
.
To be more specific, if the Riccati equation has rational solutions, then one can compute
solutions
u_{i}\in C(z)
with
i=1,\dots,s
of the Riccati equation and for each
i
a finite dimensional
C
-vector space
W_{i}\subset C[z]
containing
C
and a basis
\{w_{i,1},\dots,w_{i,m_{i}}\}
of
W_{i}
such that the set

(48)

\bigcup_{i=1}^{s}\left\{u_{i}+\frac{c_{i,1}(w_{i,1})^{\prime}+\dots+c_{i,m_{i}}(w_{i,m_{i}})^{\prime}}{c_{i,1}w_{i,1}+\dots+c_{i,m_{i}}w_{i,m_{i}}}\,\middle|\,c_{i,k}\in C\ \text{not all zero}\right\}

is the set of all solutions in
F
of the Riccati equation (cf.
[
vdPS03
, Proposition 4.9]
).
We are going to combine with Algorithm
1
as many rational solutions
\overline{v}_{i}\in F
as possible into a proper differential ideal

S_{\mathrm{inter}}\,:=\,\langle s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v})-\overline{s}_{1},\dots,s_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})-\overline{s}_{l},v_{i_{r+1}}-\overline{v}_{i_{r+1}},\dots,v_{i_{l}}-\overline{v}_{i_{l}}\rangle\subset F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}

using the differential Thomas decomposition (cf.
[
Rob14
]
), i.e., we determine a partition

I\,=\,I^{\prime}\cup I^{\prime\prime}\,=\,\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}\cup\{i_{r+1},\dots,i_{l}\}

with
r
minimal such that
S_{\mathrm{inter}}\unlhd F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
is proper.
Algorithm
1
starts with the computation of all solutions in
F
of all
l
Riccati equations.
This is done because if a specialization of some
v_{i}
in a Picard-Vessiot field for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
lies in
F
, then the corresponding Riccati equation must have at least one solution in
F
.
But not all found solutions in
F
can be used to define a proper differential ideal
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
.
It may be possible that one needs to leave out potential
\overline{v}_{i}\in F
and needs to keep instead the respective differential indeterminate
v_{i}
.
It may also be the case that only particular combinations of rational solutions to the Riccati equations lead to a proper differential ideal by specialization.
To overcome these problems Algorithm
1
simply checks with the differential Thomas decomposition all finitely many possibilities and one takes a consistent one, that is
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
is a proper differential ideal,
for which the number of Riccati equations
\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{i})=0
admitting rational solutions is maximal.
In case a Riccati equation
\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{i})=0
has infinitely many rational solutions, that is the vector space
W_{i}
is not trivial, we add the additional differential equations
c_{i,k}^{\prime}=0
to
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
and with a suitable ranking the differential Thomas decomposition delivers the conditions on the
c_{i,k}
such that
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
is consistent.

Input:

\mathrm{Ric}_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{1}),\,\dots,\,\mathrm{Ric}_{l}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{l})

and
s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v})-\overline{s}_{1},\dots,s_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})-\overline{s}_{l}
.

Output:

A partition

I\,=\,\{1,\dots,l\}\,=\,I^{\prime}\cup I^{\prime\prime}\,=\,\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}\cup\{i_{r+1},\dots,i_{l}\}

with minimal
r
and rational solutions
\overline{v}_{i_{r+1}},\dots,\overline{v}_{i_{l}}
in
F
of the Riccati equations
\mathrm{Ric}_{i_{r+1}}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{i_{r+1}})=0,\,\dots,\,\mathrm{Ric}_{i_{l}}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{i_{l}})=0
such that

S_{\mathrm{inter}}\,=\,\langle s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v})-\overline{s}_{1},\quad\ldots,\quad s_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})-\overline{s}_{l},\quad v_{i_{r+1}}-\overline{v}_{i_{r+1}},\quad\dots,\quad v_{i_{l}}-\overline{v}_{i_{l}}\rangle

of
F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
is proper.

I^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow\emptyset

for

k=1
,
2
, …,
l

do

1

\mathrm{Sol}_{k}\leftarrow\{v_{k}\}

if

\mathrm{Ric}_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{k})=0
has rational solutions

then

2

I^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow I^{\prime\prime}\cup{\{k\}}

Compute the set of rational solutions

\mathrm{RatSol}_{k}\,=\,\left\{u^{k}_{i}+\frac{c^{k}_{i,1}(w^{k}_{i,1})^{\prime}+\dots+c^{k}_{i,m_{i}}(w^{k}_{i,m_{i}})^{\prime}}{c^{k}_{i,1}w^{k}_{i,1}+\dots+c^{k}_{i,m_{i}}w^{k}_{i,m_{i}}}\,\middle|\,i=1,\dots,r_{k}\right\}

of
\mathrm{Ric}_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},v_{k})=0
, where
u_{i}^{k}
,
w_{i,j}^{k}
are as in (
48
),
r_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
c_{i,j}^{k}
are constant indeterminates.

\mathrm{Sol}_{k}\leftarrow\mathrm{Sol}_{k}\cup\mathrm{RatSol}_{k}

Define
P
as the list of elements of the Cartesian product
\mathrm{Sol}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathrm{Sol}_{l}
.

Sort
P
in ascending order comparing the number of components of tuples that are differential indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}
.

while

P\neq\emptyset

do

3

Take the first element
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{sol}}=(\mathrm{sol}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{sol}_{l})
of
P
and remove it from
P
.

Consider the differential system
\mathcal{J}
corresponding to the differential ideal of
F[c_{i,j}^{k}]\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
that is obtained from

\langle s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v})-\overline{s}_{1},\dots,s_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})-\overline{s}_{l},\,v_{1}-b_{1}^{-1}\mathrm{sol}_{1},\dots,v_{l}-b_{l}^{-1}\mathrm{sol}_{l}\rangle\subset F(c_{i,j}^{k})\{\boldsymbol{v}\}

by clearing denominators involving
c_{i,j}^{k}
where
b_{i}
as in Proposition
3.5
.

Treat the constants
\boldsymbol{c}=(c_{i,j}^{k})
as differential indeterminates with vanishing derivatives, i.e., add the equations

\partial c_{i,j}^{k}=0
to
\mathcal{J}
.

Compute a Thomas decomposition of
\mathcal{J}
together with the above cleared denominators as inequations with respect to a ranking of
F\{\boldsymbol{v},\mathbf{c}\}
for which
c_{i,j}^{k}
are ranked lowest, and let
\mathcal{S}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{S}_{t}
be the resulting simple differential systems.

if

t>0

then

4

Remove indices
k
from
I^{\prime\prime}
where
\mathrm{sol}_{k}
is a variable
v_{k}
.

Set
I^{\prime}=\{1,\dots,l\}\setminus I^{\prime\prime}
.

Choose a simple system
\mathcal{S}
of the Thomas decomposition and
read off
the equations and inequations which only involve the indeterminates
\boldsymbol{c}=(c_{i,j}^{k})
. Choose a constant point
\boldsymbol{\overline{c}}=(\overline{c}_{i,j}^{k})
satisfying these equations and inequations and determine the explicit solution
\overline{\mathrm{sol}}_{k}
in

F
defined by
\boldsymbol{\overline{c}}
.

return
(
I^{\prime}
,
I^{\prime\prime}
,
\overline{v}_{k}=\overline{\mathrm{sol}}_{k}
for
k\in I^{\prime\prime}
)

Algorithm 1

Compute Consistent System

Proposition 8.4
.

Algorithm
1
is correct and terminates.

Proof.

Since all sets
\mathrm{Sol}_{k}
are finite, the list
P
also consists only of finitely many elements.
The sorted list
P
defined in step 8 and 9 contains always as the last element the tuple which consists of all differential variables
\boldsymbol{v}
. For this tuple the ideal considered in step 12 becomes the differential ideal
S
which is prime by Lemma
8.2
, and therefore a Thomas decomposition of the corresponding differential system
\mathcal{J}
contains at least one simple system
\mathcal{S}
, that is
t>0
. Since the last tuple of the list is the tuple of all differential indeterminates, we obtain
I^{\prime\prime}=\emptyset
and
I^{\prime}=\{1,\dots,l\}
in step 16 and 17 and there are no rational solutions. Thus the algorithm always terminates.

The minimality of
r
is guaranteed by the sorting of the list
P
. Note that since we added the denominators as inequations to the input of the differential Thomas decomposition the choice of any simple system cannot lead into loosing a rational solution.

Since we choose a specialization
\boldsymbol{\overline{c}}=(\overline{c}_{i,j}^{k})
of the indeterminates
\boldsymbol{c}=(c_{i,j}^{k})
according to the equations and inequations of the simple system
\mathcal{S}
only involving the
\boldsymbol{c}
, the linear differential polynomials
v_{k}-\overline{\mathrm{sol}}_{k}
for
k\in I^{\prime\prime}
together with the differential polynomials

s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v})-\overline{s}_{1},\quad\dots,\quad s_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})-\overline{s}_{l}

form a proper differential ideal
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
of
F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
.
∎

We are going to use
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
to construct a Picard-Vessiot extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
of
F
for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
. Our respective fundamental matrix
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
will have the Bruhat decomposition

\mathcal{\overline{Y}}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})

with
\overline{v}_{i}\in F
for
i\in I^{\prime\prime}
and
\overline{v}_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{E}}\setminus F
for
i\in I^{\prime}
which will force the differential Galois group
H
of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
over
F
to be a subgroup of the standard parabolic group
P_{J}
.

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
I=I^{\prime}\cup I^{\prime\prime}
and
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
be the result of Algorithm
1
for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
and let
J
be as in Definition
5.1
for the partition
I^{\prime}\cup I^{\prime\prime}
.

(a)

Then there exists a Picard-Vessiot extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
of
F
for

A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
with fundamental matrix

\mathcal{\overline{Y}}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})

such that
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}
extends the tuple of
\overline{v}_{i}
with
i\in I^{\prime\prime}
given by Algorithm
1
with certain
\overline{v}_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{E}}\setminus F
for
i\in I^{\prime}
and where
\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}\in(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{\times})^{l}
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}\in\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{m}
.

(b)

The differential Galois group
H
of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
over
F
with representation induced by
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
is contained in
P_{J}
.

(c)

Let
\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1}=(\exp_{1}^{-1},\dots,\exp_{l}^{-1})
. The map

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm PV}\colon F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]\to\overline{\mathcal{E}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}\mapsto\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\,\boldsymbol{\exp}\mapsto\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}\mapsto\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}

is a differential homomorphism and the kernel of
\sigma_{\rm PV}|_{F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}}
contains
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
.

(d)

If
H=L\ltimes R_{u}(H)

is a Levi decomposition of
H
for some Levi group
L
, then
L
is
\widetilde{L}
-irreducible for a Levi group
\widetilde{L}
of
P_{J}
and
R_{u}(H)\leq R_{u}(P_{J})
.

Proof.

(a)
Since
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
is a proper differential ideal of
F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
, we can choose a maximal differential ideal
S_{\mathrm{max}}
of
F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
containing
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
. The quotient
F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}/S_{\mathrm{max}}
is differentially simple and finitely generated over
F
, namely by the residue classes

v_{1}+S_{\mathrm{max}},\quad\dots,\quad v_{1}^{(d_{1})}+S_{\mathrm{max}},\quad\dots,\quad v_{l}+S_{\mathrm{max}},\quad\dots,\quad v_{l}^{(d_{l})}+S_{\mathrm{max}}\,.

Hence, by
[
vdPS03
, Lemma 1.17]
the field of fractions

\Frac(F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}/S_{\mathrm{max}})

has constants
C
. Now one uses the standard method to construct a Picard-Vessiot extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
of

\Frac(F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}/S_{\mathrm{max}})

for the matrix
A_{\rm Liou}(\boldsymbol{v}+S_{\mathrm{max}})
defining the Liouvillian part. More precisely,
we consider the differential ring

\Frac(F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}/S_{\mathrm{max}})[X_{i,j},\det(X_{i,j})^{-1}],

where the derivation on
X_{i,j}
is defined by
\partial(X_{i,j})=A_{\rm Liou}(\boldsymbol{v}+S_{\mathrm{max}})(X_{i,j})
(cf. Theorem
3.2
).
Since
A_{\rm Liou}(\boldsymbol{v}+S_{\mathrm{max}})\in\mathfrak{b}^{-}(\Frac(F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}/S_{\mathrm{max}}))
, we can choose a maximal differential ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}
in
this differential ring such that
I_{\mathrm{max}}
contains the defining ideal of
B^{-}
. Then

\overline{\mathcal{E}}\,:=\,\Frac(\Frac(F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}/S_{\mathrm{max}})[X_{i,j},\det(X_{i,j})^{-1}]/I_{\rm max})

is a Picard-Vessiot extension for
A_{\rm Liou}(\boldsymbol{v}+S_{\mathrm{max}})
and the fundamental matrix
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\mathrm{Liou}}:=X+I_{\mathrm{max}}
has the property that
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\mathrm{Liou}}\in B^{-}(\overline{\mathcal{E}})
. Thus there exist unique elements

\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}=(\overline{\exp}_{1},\dots,\overline{\exp}_{l})
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}=(\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{1},\dots,\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{m})
in
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
such that
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\mathrm{Liou}}
has the Bruhat decomposition

\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\mathrm{Liou}}\,=\,\boldsymbol{t}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})\in B^{-}(\overline{\mathcal{E}})\,.

It follows from the construction that the matrix

(49)

\mathcal{\overline{Y}}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{v}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})

is a fundamental matrix for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
with the property that
\overline{v}_{i}\in F
for
i\in I^{\prime\prime}
.
Clearly, we have
F(\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{i,j})\subset\overline{\mathcal{E}}
.
By the uniqueness of the Bruhat decomposition the Bruhat decomposition of

\overline{\mathcal{Y}}\in G(F(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}))
over
F(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j})
has to coincide with the one in (
49
) over
\overline{\mathcal{E}}\supset F(\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{i,j})
.
Thus
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}
are elements of
F(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j})
and so
F(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j})=\overline{\mathcal{E}}
.
We conclude that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F
for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
.
The remaining
\overline{v}_{i}
with
i\in I^{\prime}
are not in
F
, since otherwise the set
I^{\prime\prime}
returned by Algorithm
1
would have been a proper superset.

(b)
Let
H
be the differential Galois group of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
over
F
in the representation induced by
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
. Then
H
has to fix all
\overline{v}_{i}\in F
with
i\in I^{\prime\prime}
. This means that
H
has to fix all indeterminates

v_{i}
with
i\in I^{\prime\prime}
, since
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
contains the linear differential polynomials

v_{i_{r+1}}-\overline{v}_{i_{r+1}},\dots,v_{i_{l}}-\overline{v}_{i_{l}}\,.

According to Theorem
5.3
, the largest subgroup of
G
fixing these elements is the standard parabolic subgroup
P_{J}
and so
H
is contained in
P_{J}
.

(c)
Clearly
\sigma_{\rm max}\colon F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}\to\Frac(F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}/S_{\mathrm{max}})
is a differential homomorphism whose kernel contains
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
. The elements
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}
are algebraically independent over
F\langle\boldsymbol{v}\rangle

and the elements in
\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
are non-zero, i.e., they are invertible. Thus
the differential homomorphism
\sigma_{\rm max}
extends uniquely in the obvious way to a homomorphism of rings

\sigma_{\rm PV}\colon F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]\to\Frac(F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}/S_{\mathrm{max}})[\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}^{-1},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}]\,.

The derivative of
\exp_{i}
is
g_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})\exp_{i}
(cf. Theorem
3.2
) and by construction the derivative of
\overline{\exp}_{i}
is
\sigma_{\rm max}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{v}))\,\overline{\exp}_{i}
.
Thus we have
\partial(\sigma_{\rm PV}(\exp_{i}))=\sigma_{\rm PV}(\partial(\exp_{i}))
. Recall that the integrand of
\mathrm{int}_{i}
is a polynomial expression in
\boldsymbol{\exp}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1}
and those
\mathrm{int}_{j}
with
|0pt(\beta_{j})|<|0pt(\beta_{i})|
.
Since
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{Liou}}=\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})
is the fundamental matrix for
A_{\rm Liou}(\boldsymbol{v})
and
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\mathrm{Liou}}=\boldsymbol{t}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})
the one for
\sigma_{\rm max}(A_{\rm Liou}(\boldsymbol{v}))
, the derivative of
\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
is the same polynomial expression but now in
\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}^{-1}
and
\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{j}
.
We conclude that
\sigma_{\rm PV}
is a differential homomorphism.

(d)
Since
H\leq P_{J}
, the Levi group
L
is contained in
P_{J}
. We are going to show that
P_{J}
is minimal among the parabolic subgroups of
G
with respect to containing
L
. Since
L
is reductive it will then follow from
Proposition
2.6
that
L
is
\widetilde{L}
-irreducible for some Levi group
\widetilde{L}
of
P_{J}
.
Let
P
be a further parabolic subgroup of
G
such that
L
is contained in
P
and such that
P\leq P_{J}
.
There is a unique standard parabolic subgroup
P_{\widetilde{J}}
and

g\in G
such that
gPg^{-1}=P_{\widetilde{J}}
. Since by Algorithm
1
the set
J
has the property that as many indeterminates as possible of
\boldsymbol{v}
are fixed by
P_{J}
, the group
P_{\widetilde{J}}
can only fix the same number or a smaller number of indeterminates
\boldsymbol{v}
. We conclude by Theorem
5.3
that
|\tilde{J}|\geq|J|
and so

\dim(P)\,=\,\dim(P_{\widetilde{J}})\,\geq\,\dim(P_{J})

forcing
P=P_{J}
, since
P\leq P_{J}
.

The radical
R_{u}(H)
is a connected unipotent subgroup and so it is contained in a Borel subgroup of
G
. We are going to show that this Borel subgroup is
B^{-}
.
It then follows from
[
Hum75
, Corollary A, Section 30.3]
and the last sentence of the proof of
[
Hum75
, Proposition 30.3]
that there is a standard parabolic subgroup
P_{\widetilde{J}}
such that
R_{u}(H)\leq R_{u}(P_{\widetilde{J}})
and
N_{G}(R_{u}(H))\leq P_{\widetilde{J}}
which implies that
H\leq P_{\widetilde{J}}
.
Since
R_{u}(H)
is unipotent the Picard-Vessiot extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{R_{u}(H)}
corresponds to a tower of one-dimensional anti-derivative extensions with Galois groups
\mathbb{G}_{a}
.
Because every
\overline{\exp}_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{E}}
defines an exponential extension of
F\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}\rangle
with Galois group a subgroup of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, we conclude that
\overline{\exp}_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{R_{u}(H)}
, implying that its logarithmic derivative
\overline{v}_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{R_{u}(H)}
.
By the construction of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
, we have that

\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{B^{-}}\,=\,\Frac(F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}/S_{\mathrm{max}})\,=\,F\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}\rangle

and so we obtain the inclusion
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{B^{-}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{R_{u}(H)}
.
It follows from the Fundamental Theorem of Differential Galois Theory that
R_{u}(H)\leq B^{-}
as claimed.
Since
H\leq P_{J}
and
H\leq P_{\widetilde{J}}
, we conclude that
H\leq P_{J\cap\widetilde{J}}
. Since
P_{J}
is minimal among the standard parabolic subgroups with respect to containing
H
and we have
P_{J\cap\widetilde{J}}\leq P_{J}
, it follows that
J\cap\widetilde{J}=J
and so
P_{J}\leq P_{\widetilde{J}}
. Because
R_{u}(P_{J})
and
R_{u}(P_{\widetilde{J}})
are generated by the root groups corresponding to roots in
\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}_{J}
and in
\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}_{\widetilde{J}}
respectively, it follows from
\Psi^{-}_{J}\subseteq\Psi^{-}_{\widetilde{J}}
that
R_{u}(P_{J})\geq R_{u}(P_{\widetilde{J}})
, where

\Psi_{J}\,:=\,\Phi\cap\langle\alpha_{j}\mid j\in J\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{-span}}

and
\Psi_{\widetilde{J}}
is defined similarly.
Thus with the above we obtain that
R_{u}(P_{J})\geq R_{u}(H)
.
∎

9.
Specializing the Parameters of the Reductive Part

We extend the specialization

\sigma_{0}\colon C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\}\to F,\,\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\mapsto\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}

from Section
8
to a specialization of
C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}
using the ideal
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
.
More precisely, we define

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma_{\rm inter}\colon C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}&\to&F\,\cong\,F\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}/S_{\mathrm{inter}}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})&\mapsto&\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}=(v_{i_{r+1}},\dots,v_{i_{l}})&\mapsto&\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}=(\overline{v}_{i_{r+1}},\dots,\overline{v}_{i_{l}})\,.\end{array}

Assumption 2
.

Recall that in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle[\partial]
the generic operator
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)
has the irreducible factorization

L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,=\,L_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\cdots L_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)

and we have determined the least common left multiple

\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\,=\,\LCLM(L_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial),\dots,L_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial))

of these irreducible factors. In addition to Assumption
1
we further assume the following:

(a)

The denominators of the coefficients in the irreducible factors and in their least common left multiple do not specialize to zero under
\sigma_{\rm inter}
.

(b)

The denominators
p_{2,1},\dots,p_{2,q}\in C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}
of the representation of the invariants
p_{1},\dots,p_{q}
in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
computed in Remark
5.7
do not specialize to zero under
\sigma_{\rm inter}
.

(c)

The denominators of the coefficients of
E_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z}),\dots,E_{l}(\boldsymbol{Z})
from Proposition
6.6
do not specialize to zero under
\sigma_{\rm inter}
.

(d)

The denominators of the coefficients of the numerators of
\mathrm{EXP}_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})
,
V_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
\mathrm{INT}_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z})
do not specialize to zero under
\sigma_{\rm inter}
.

(e)

The order of the least common left multiple of the specialized factors

\overline{L}_{1}(\partial)\,:=\,L_{1}(\sigma_{\rm inter}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}),\partial),\,\dots,\,\overline{L}_{k}(\partial)\,:=\,L_{k}(\sigma_{\rm inter}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}),\partial)

(cf. Definition
9.1
below) is equal to the order of
\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)
.

According to Assumption
2

(a)
we are able to specialize the generic irreducible factors and their least common left multiple.

Definition 9.1
.

For the irreducible factorization

L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,=\,L_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\cdots L_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)

in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle[\partial]
we denote its specialization under
\sigma_{\rm inter}
by

L_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\partial)\,=\,\overline{L}_{1}(\partial)\cdots\overline{L}_{k}(\partial)

in
F[\partial]
. Moreover, for the least common left multiple

\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\,=\,\LCLM(L_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial),\dots,L_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial))

in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle[\partial]
we denote its specialization under
\sigma_{\rm inter}
by

\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,.

Let
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
be a basis of the solution space
V
of
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
in
\mathcal{E}
such that for
i=1,\dots,k
the elements
\widehat{y}_{i,1},\dots,\widehat{y}_{i,\widehat{n}_{i}}
defined as

(50)

\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{y}_{k,1}=y_{1},\ \dots,\ \widehat{y}_{k,\widehat{n}_{k}}=y_{n_{k}^{\prime}},\\[5.0pt]
\widehat{y}_{k-1,1}=L_{k}(\partial)y_{n_{k}^{\prime}+1},\ \dots,\ \widehat{y}_{k-1,\widehat{n}_{k-1}}=L_{k}(\partial)y_{n_{k-1}^{\prime}},\\[5.0pt]
\widehat{y}_{k-2,1}=(L_{k-1}(\partial)\circ L_{k}(\partial))y_{n_{k-1}^{\prime}+1},\ \dots,\\[5.0pt]
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\widehat{y}_{k-2,\widehat{n}_{k-2}}=(L_{k-1}(\partial)\circ L_{k}(\partial))y_{n_{k-2}^{\prime}},\\[5.0pt]
\vdots\\[5.0pt]
\widehat{y}_{1,1}=(L_{2}(\partial)\circ\cdots\circ L_{k}(\partial))y_{n_{2}^{\prime}+1},\ \dots,\ \widehat{y}_{1,\widehat{n}_{1}}=(L_{2}(\partial)\circ\cdots\circ L_{k}(\partial))y_{n_{1}^{\prime}}\end{array}

form a basis of the solution space
V_{i}
of
L_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0
in
\mathcal{E}
(cf. the proof of Theorem
6.3
and Remark
6.4
).

Remark 9.2
.

Let
D\subset C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}
be the multiplicatively closed subset generated by the denominators addressed in Assumption
2

(a)
–
(d)
.
Then the set
D
specializes under
\sigma_{\rm inter}
to a multiplicatively closed subset of
F
which does not contain zero.
Thus, the differential homomorphism
\sigma_{\rm PV}
of Proposition
8.5
extends to the localization by
D
, i.e. to the differential homomorphism

\sigma_{\rm PV}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]\to\overline{\mathcal{E}}\,,

which we also denote by
\sigma_{\rm PV}
. Indeed,
since the kernel of
\sigma_{\rm PV}
contains the ideal
S_{\mathrm{inter}}
,
we have
\ker(\sigma_{\rm PV})\cap D=\emptyset
. Since for
i=1,\dots,k
the basis elements
\widehat{y}_{i,1},\dots,\widehat{y}_{i,\widehat{n}_{i}}
of the solution space
V_{i}
of
L_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0
and the basis elements
y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
of the solution space of
\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0

are contained in

D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]\,,

we can specialize them to elements in
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
via
\sigma_{\rm PV}
.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
\sigma_{\rm PV}
be as in Remark
9.2
.

(a)

The specialization
\sigma_{\rm PV}
induces a
C
-vector space isomorphism

\sigma_{\rm PV}|_{V}\colon V\to\overline{V},\,y_{i}\mapsto\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{i})

from
V
to the solution space
\overline{V}
of
L_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\partial)\,y=0
in
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
.

(b)

For
i=1,\dots,k
the specialization
\sigma_{\rm PV}
induces a
C
-vector space isomorphism

\sigma_{\rm PV}|_{V_{i}}\colon V_{i}\to\overline{V}_{i},\,\widehat{y}_{i,j}\mapsto\sigma_{\rm PV}(\widehat{y}_{i,j})\quad\mathrm{with}\ j=1,\dots,\widehat{n}_{i}

from
V_{i}
to the solution space
\overline{V}_{i}
of
\overline{L}_{i}(\partial)y=0
in
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
.

(c)

The spaces
V_{i}
and
\overline{V}_{i}
are invariant under
P_{J}(C)
and
H(C)
, respectively.
For
g\in H(C)
the homomorphism
\sigma_{\rm PV}
is compatible with the induced isomorphisms
\gamma_{g}\colon V_{i}\to V_{i}
and
\overline{\gamma}_{g}\colon\overline{V}_{i}\to\overline{V}_{i}
, i.e.
\sigma(\gamma_{g}(v))=\overline{\gamma}_{g}(\sigma_{\rm PV}(v))
for all
v\in V_{i}
.

Proof.

(a)
We have that
B_{G}\mathcal{Y}
is a Wronskian matrix for
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)y=0
and so the elements
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
of its first row form a basis for
V
. Then Proposition
8.5

(a)
and
(c)
imply that

\sigma_{\rm PV}(B_{G}\mathcal{Y})\,=\,\sigma_{\rm PV}(B_{G})\,\mathcal{\overline{Y}}

is a Wronskian matrix for
L_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\partial)\,y=0
with entries in
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
. Since
\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})=1
and
\det(\sigma_{\rm PV}(B_{G}))\in C^{\times}
, the entries in the first row

\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{1}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{n})
are
C
-linearly independent by
[
vdPS03
, Lemma 1.12]
.

(b)
Let
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and let
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
be a basis of
V
with the property that the elements in (
50
) form bases of
V_{1},\dots,V_{k}
. Since
\sigma_{\rm PV}
is a differential homomorphism, the images
\sigma_{\rm PV}(\widehat{y}_{i,1}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm PV}(\widehat{y}_{i,\widehat{n}_{i}})
are solutions of
\overline{L}_{i}(\partial)\,y=0
. According to
(a)
the elements
\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{1}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{n})
are
C
-linearly independent. One can use this to show by induction on
i=k,\dots,1
that
\sigma_{\rm PV}(\widehat{y}_{i,1}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm PV}(\widehat{y}_{i,\widehat{n}_{i}})
are
C
-linearly independent. Since the order of
\overline{L}_{i}(\partial)
is the same as the order of
L_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)
(recall that
L_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)
is monic), we conclude that
\sigma_{\rm PV}(\widehat{y}_{i,1}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm PV}(\widehat{y}_{i,\widehat{n}_{i}})
form a
C
-basis of
\overline{V}_{i}
.

(c)
The first part follows from
[
Sin96
, Lemma 2.2]
applied to the generic and specialized situation separately.
For
g\in H(C)\leq P_{J}(C)
let
\gamma_{g}\in{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}})
and

\overline{\gamma}_{g}\in{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}/F)
be given by

\gamma_{g}(\mathcal{Y})=\mathcal{Y}\,g
and
\overline{\gamma}_{g}(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})=\mathcal{\overline{Y}}\,g
.
Since
B_{G}\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\})
and
\sigma_{\rm PV}(B_{G})\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)
, the action of
\gamma_{g}
respectively
\overline{\gamma}_{g}
on
V
and
\overline{V}
is induced by

\gamma_{g}(B_{G}\,\mathcal{Y})\,=\,\gamma_{g}(B_{G})\,\gamma_{g}(\mathcal{Y})\,=\,B_{G}\,\mathcal{Y}\,g

and

\overline{\gamma}_{g}(\sigma_{\rm PV}(B_{G})\,\mathcal{\overline{Y}})\,=\,\overline{\gamma}_{g}(\sigma_{\rm PV}(B_{G}))\,\overline{\gamma}_{g}(\overline{\mathcal{Y}})\,=\,\sigma_{\rm PV}(B_{G})\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}\,g

respectively. According to Proposition
8.5
we have
\sigma_{\rm PV}(\mathcal{Y})=\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
and so we conclude that

\sigma_{\rm PV}(\gamma_{g}(B_{G}\mathcal{Y}))\,=\,\sigma_{\rm PV}(B_{G}\,\mathcal{Y}\,g)\,=\,\sigma_{\rm PV}(B_{G})\,\mathcal{\overline{Y}}\,g\,=\,\overline{\gamma}_{g}(\sigma_{\rm PV}(B_{G})\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}})\,.

By restricting the action on
V
and
\overline{V}
to
V_{i}
and
\overline{V}_{i}
, respectively, the claim follows.
∎

Proposition 9.4
.

(a)

The specialized factorization

L_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\partial)\,=\,\overline{L}_{1}(\partial)\cdots\overline{L}_{k}(\partial)

is an irreducible factorization in
F[\partial]
.

(b)

The specialized least common left multiple
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)
is the least common left multiple of
\overline{L}_{1}(\partial),\dots,\overline{L}_{k}(\partial)
in
F[\partial]
, that is

\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,=\,\LCLM(\overline{L}_{1}(\partial),\dots,\overline{L}_{k}(\partial))\,.

(c)

The differential homomorphism
\sigma_{\rm PV}
of Remark
9.2
induces a
C
-vector space isomorphism

\sigma_{\rm PV}\colon V_{I^{\prime\prime}}\to\overline{V}_{I^{\prime\prime}},\,(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})\mapsto(\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}))

between the solution space
V_{I^{\prime\prime}}
of

\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)y=0
in
\mathcal{E}
and the solution space of

\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0
in
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

(a)

Recall that the irreducible factorization of
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0

induces a flag

(51)

V^{\prime}_{k}\subset V^{\prime}_{k-1}\subset\dots\subset V^{\prime}_{1}=V

on the solution space
V
of
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
, where the subspaces
V^{\prime}_{k},\dots,V^{\prime}_{1}
are the solution spaces of the operators

\displaystyle L_{k}(\partial),\ L_{k-1}(\partial)\circ L_{k}(\partial),\ \dots,\ L_{1}(\partial)\circ\cdots\circ L_{k}(\partial)=L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial),

respectively. Since
P_{J}(C)
is the Galois group of
\mathcal{E}
over
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle
, the parabolic subgroup
P_{J}(C)
stabilizes the flag (
51
) by
[
Sin96
, Lemma 2.2]
.
Moreover,
P_{J}(C)
does not stabilize any refinement of the flag in (
51
).
Indeed, assume that there is a subspace
\tilde{V}^{\prime}
such that
\tilde{V}^{\prime}
is a proper subspace of
V_{i}^{\prime}
,
V_{i+1}^{\prime}
is a proper subspace of
\tilde{V}^{\prime}
and
P_{J}(C)
stabilizes the flag

V^{\prime}_{k}\subset\dots\subset V^{\prime}_{i+1}\subset\tilde{V}^{\prime}\subset V^{\prime}_{i}\subset\dots\subset V^{\prime}_{1}=V.

Then by
[
Sin96
, Lemma 2.2]
there exists an operator
\tilde{L}(\partial)\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle[\partial]

with solution space
\tilde{V}^{\prime}
.
Since
\tilde{V}^{\prime}\subset V^{\prime}_{i}
and
V^{\prime}_{i+1}\subset\tilde{V}^{\prime}
, there exist operators
Q_{1}(\partial),Q_{2}(\partial)\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle[\partial]
of order at least one such that

L_{i}(\partial)\circ\dots\circ L_{k}(\partial)\,=\,Q_{1}(\partial)\circ\tilde{L}(\partial)\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{L}(\partial)\,=\,Q_{2}(\partial)\circ L_{i+1}(\partial)\circ\dots\circ L_{k}(\partial).

We obtain a contradiction to the irreducibility of the operator
L_{i}(\partial)
.

Suppose that there is at least one generic irreducible factor
L_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)
such that its specialization
\overline{L}_{i}(\partial)
is not irreducible over
F
, i.e., there exist operators
\overline{L}_{i,1}(\partial)
and
\overline{L}_{i,2}(\partial)
in
F[\partial]
of order at least one such that
\overline{L}_{i}(\partial)=\overline{L}_{i,1}(\partial)\circ\overline{L}_{i,2}(\partial)
.
According to Lemma
9.3
the generic flag specializes under
\sigma
to a flag

\overline{V}^{\prime}_{k}\subset\overline{V}^{\prime}_{k-1}\subset\dots\subset\overline{V}^{\prime}_{1}=\overline{V}

of the solution space
\overline{V}
of
L_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\partial)\,y=0
and this flag is stabilized by
H(C)
, since
H(C)\leq P_{J}(C)
.
Our assumption implies now that this flag becomes finer, i.e., there exists a subspace
\overline{V}^{\prime}_{i,2}
of positive dimension such that
\overline{V}^{\prime}_{i,2}
is a proper subspace of
\overline{V}^{\prime}_{i}
and
\overline{V}^{\prime}_{i+1}
is a proper subspace of
\overline{V}^{\prime}_{i,2}
and
H(C)
stabilizes the flag

\overline{V}^{\prime}_{k}\subset\dots\subset\overline{V}^{\prime}_{i+1}\subset\overline{V}^{\prime}_{i,2}\subset\overline{V}^{\prime}_{i}\subset\dots\subset\overline{V}^{\prime}_{1}=\overline{V}.

The stabilizer of this flag is a parabolic subgroup
P(C)
of
G(C)
containing
H(C)
.
Since this flag is finer than the one in (
51
) and
P_{J}(C)
does not stabilize any refinement of it,
P(C)
is a proper subgroup of
P_{J}(C)
.
But this contradicts the fact that
P_{J}(C)
is the smallest parabolic subgroup containing the Levi groups of
H(C)
(cf. the proof of Proposition
8.5

(d)
). We conclude that

L_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\partial)\,=\,\overline{L}_{1}(\partial)\dots\overline{L}_{k}(\partial)

is an irreducible factorization over
F
.

(b)
Since
L_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)
divides
\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)
on the right and
\sigma_{\rm inter}
is a differential homomorphism and so can be extended to

D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}[\partial]\to F[\partial]\,,

we obtain that each irreducible operator
\overline{L}_{i}(\partial)
divides
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)
on the right for every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.
By Assumption
2

(e)
, the order of
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)
coincides with the order of

\LCLM(\overline{L}_{1}(\partial),\dots,\overline{L}_{k}(\partial))
.
Hence, the statement follows from the uniqueness of the least common left multiple.

(c)
By
[
Sin96
, Lemma 2.12]
the solution spaces of
\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)
and
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)
are
V_{1}+\dots+V_{k}
and
\overline{V}_{1}+\dots+\overline{V}_{k}
, respectively.
Since the least common left multiples have the same order, their solution spaces have the same dimension. The differential homomorphism
\sigma_{\rm PV}
induces a
C
-linear map between these solution spaces.
Let
\overline{v}\in\overline{V}_{1}+\dots+\overline{V}_{k}
.
Then there exist
\overline{v}_{i}\in\overline{V}_{i}
with
i=1,\dots,k
such that
\overline{v}=\overline{v}_{1}+\dots+\overline{v}_{k}
.
Since
\sigma_{\rm PV}
restricts to isomorphisms
V_{i}\to\overline{V}_{i}
by Lemma
9.3

(b)
, there exist
v_{i}\in V_{i}
such that
\sigma_{\rm PV}(v_{i})=\overline{v}_{i}
and so
\sigma_{\rm PV}(v_{1}+\dots+v_{k})=\overline{v}_{1}+\dots+\overline{v}_{k}=\overline{v}
.
Thus the
C
-linear map between the solution spaces is surjective and so an isomorphism.∎

Remark 9.5
.

Note that Assumption
2

(a)
might imply Assumption
2

(e)
.
For this claim one has to prove that the Wronskian determinant of
y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
is the denominator of the coefficient of the second highest order term in
\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)
.
Then Assumption
2

(a)
implies that the basis
y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
specializes to a
C
-linearly independent set in
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
which can be shown to be a basis of the least common left multiple of
\overline{L}_{1}(\partial),\dots,\overline{L}_{k}(\partial)
.
This forces this least common left multiple to have the same order as
\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)
.

Definition 9.6
.

We denote by
Q
a maximal differential ideal in the differential ring

F[X_{i,j},\det(X_{i,j})^{-1}\mid i,j=1,\dots,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}]\,=\,F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]\,,

where the derivation on
X_{i,j}
is defined by

\partial(X)\,=\,A_{\rm comp}\,X\,,\qquad X=(X_{i,j})\,,

where
A_{\rm comp}
denotes the companion matrix corresponding to the differential equation
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0
(cf. Proposition
9.4
).
Moreover, we define

E_{\rm red}\,:=\,\Frac(F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]/Q)\,,

which is – by the standard construction method – a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F
for
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0
.

Remark 9.7
.

The maximal differential ideal
Q
of Definition
9.6
can be computed
using the results presented in
[
CS99
]
and
[
vHW97
]
. More precisely, according to

[
CS99
, Proposition 4.2]
the differential Galois group of
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0
is a Levi group of the differential Galois group
H
for the specialized normal form equation
L_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\partial)\,y=0
and so is reductive. Hence, we can use the algorithm presented in
[
CS99
, Section 4.1]
to compute generators
q_{1},\dots,q_{s}
of
Q
.

Proposition 9.8
.

(a)

The differential homomorphism

\sigma_{\rm PV}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]\to\overline{\mathcal{E}}

from Remark
9.2
restricts to a differential homomorphism

\sigma_{\rm PV}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}]\to\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{R_{u}(H)}\,.

(b)

We have

\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{R_{u}(H)}\,=\,F\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\rangle\,=\,F\langle\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})\rangle\,.

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
6.3
describing the generic situation that
R_{u}(P_{J})
fixes the elements
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
.
Since by Proposition
8.5

(d)
we have the inclusion
R_{u}(H)\leq R_{u}(P_{J})
, we conclude that the specialized elements
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
are fixed by
R_{u}(H)
. Thus, we obtain the inclusions

\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{R_{u}(H)}\supseteq F\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\rangle\supset F\{\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}\}[\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}^{-1},\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}]\,.

This shows
(a)
and one inclusion in
(b)
.

By Proposition
9.4

(c)
the elements
\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})
in
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
form a
C
-basis of the solution space of
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0
and so

K_{\rm PV}:=F(\mathrm{wr}(\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})))

is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F
for that equation. Since the generic elements
y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
are fixed by
R_{u}(H)\leq R_{u}(P_{J})
(cf. Theorem
6.3
), the specialized elements

\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\quad\dots,\quad\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})

are fixed by
R_{u}(H)
and so

K_{\rm PV}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{R_{u}(H)}
.
Since
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{R_{u}(H)}
is also a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F
for the same equation (cf.
[
CS99
, Proposition 4.2]
), we conclude that

K_{\rm PV}=\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{R_{u}(H)}
.
By Remark
9.2
and Theorem
6.3
the elements
y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
are contained in

D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]\cap F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm ext},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\rangle.

Hence, their specializations
\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})
are contained in

F\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\rangle\,.

From this we obtain the inclusion

\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{R_{u}(H)}=K_{\rm PV}\subseteq F\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\rangle\,.

∎

Proposition 9.9
.

There are rational functions

\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\,:=\,(\widehat{v}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{v}_{l}),\quad\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}}\,:=\,(\widehat{\exp}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\exp}_{l})\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\quad(\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-})

in
F(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})
such that the map

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma_{\rm inter}\colon C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}&\to&F\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})&\mapsto&\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}=(v_{i_{r+1}},\dots,v_{i_{l}})&\mapsto&\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}=(\overline{v}_{i_{r+1}},\dots,\overline{v}_{i_{l}})\end{array}

extends to a differential homomorphism

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma_{\rm red}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}]&\to&E_{\rm red}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\boldsymbol{v}&\mapsto&\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}+Q\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\boldsymbol{\exp}&\mapsto&\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}}+Q\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\mathrm{int}_{i}&\mapsto&\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}+Q\end{array}

mapping the basis
y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
of
V_{I^{\prime\prime}}
to the basis
\sigma_{\rm red}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm red}(y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})
of
the solution space
\overline{V}_{I^{\prime\prime}}
of
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0
in
E_{\rm red}
.

Proof.

Since by Proposition
9.4

(c)
,
\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})
form a basis of the solution space
\overline{V}_{I^{\prime\prime}}
of
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0
, the differential field

K_{\rm PV}:=F(\mathrm{wr}(\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})))

is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F
for
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0
, where
\mathrm{wr}
denotes the Wronskian matrix. Indeed, as a subfield of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
its constants are
C
. Trivially, there exists a fundamental matrix in
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}(K_{\rm PV})

for
A_{\rm comp}
and

K_{\rm PV}
is
generated as field over
F
by the entries of this fundamental matrix.
According to Proposition
9.8

(a)
and
(b)
the differential homomorphism
\sigma_{\rm PV}

of Remark
9.2
restricts to a differential homomorphism

\sigma_{\rm PV}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}]\to K_{\rm PV}\,.

Since both differential fields
E_{\rm red}
and

K_{\rm PV}

are Picard-Vessiot extensions of
F
for the same differential equation
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0
, there exists a differential
F
-isomorphism

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\varphi\colon K_{\rm PV}&\!\to&\!\Frac(F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]/Q)\,=\,E_{\rm red}\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{wr}(\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\dots,\sigma_{\rm PV}(y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}))&\!\mapsto&\!\mathrm{wr}(\overline{X}_{1,1},\dots,\overline{X}_{1,{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}})\,M\end{array}

for a constant matrix
M\in\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}(C)
. Composing
\sigma_{\rm PV}
with
\varphi
we obtain a differential homomorphism

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm red}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}]\to E_{\rm red}\,.

The rational functions

\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\,:=\,(\widehat{v}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{v}_{l}),\quad\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}}\,:=\,(\widehat{\exp}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\exp}_{l})\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\quad(\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-})

can be chosen as preimages in
\Frac(F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}])
of

\sigma_{\rm red}(\boldsymbol{\exp})
,
\sigma_{\rm red}(\boldsymbol{v})
and
\sigma_{\rm red}(\mathrm{int}_{i})
in

\Frac(F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]/Q)
under the canonical map

F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]_{Q}\to\Frac(F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]/Q)

from the localization of
F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]
at the prime ideal
Q
.
The images of the basis elements
y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
under
\sigma_{\rm red}
are the entries in the vector
(\overline{X}_{1,1},\dots,\overline{X}_{1,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})M
and they form a basis of the solution space
\overline{V}_{I^{\prime\prime}}
of
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0
in
E_{\rm red}
, because
M\in\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}(C)
.
∎

Remark 9.10
.

Note that we can restrict the domain of definition of
\sigma_{\rm red}
to

R_{\rm rest}\,:=\,D^{-1}C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\}

and obtain a differential homomorphism
\eta
. Recall that
in Proposition
6.6
and Remark
6.8
we determined

\mathrm{EXP}_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{Z}),\quad V_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{Z})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{INT}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{Z})

in the localization
D_{\rm exp}^{-1}C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\boldsymbol{Z}\}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{EXP}_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})\,=\,\exp_{i},\quad V_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})\,=\,v_{i}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathrm{INT}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})\,=\,\mathrm{int}_{i}\,,

where
D_{\rm exp}
is the multiplicatively closed set generated by

E_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z}),\dots,E_{l}(\boldsymbol{Z})\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\rangle\{\boldsymbol{Z}\}.

Proposition
6.6
and Assumption
2

(c)
imply that the elements

E_{1}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})\,=\,\exp^{\rm ass}_{1},\quad\dots,\quad E_{l}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})\,=\,\exp^{\rm ass}_{l}

are in
R_{\rm rest}
and so we can apply
\sigma_{\rm red}
to them.
Proposition
3.5
implies that the exponential solutions
\exp^{\rm ass}_{1},\dots,\exp^{\rm ass}_{l}
of the associated equations
are products of powers of
\exp_{1},\dots,\exp_{l}
with exponents in
\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\exp_{1},\dots,\exp_{l}
are units in

D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\mathrm{int}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}]\,,

we conclude that
\exp^{\rm ass}_{1},\dots,\exp^{\rm ass}_{l}
do not lie in the kernel of
\sigma_{\rm red}
. Hence, we can
extend
\eta
to the localization of
R_{\rm rest}
by the multiplicatively closed subset generated by
\exp^{\rm ass}_{1},\dots,\exp^{\rm ass}_{l}
. This localization has now the important property that it contains the elements
\boldsymbol{\exp}
,
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
.

Lemma 9.11
.

For an invertible matrix of constants
(\overline{c}_{i,j})\in\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}(C)
define

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\eta\colon D^{-1}C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\}&\!\to&\!F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]/Q\,,\\[5.0pt]
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})&\!\mapsto&\!\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}\,,\\[5.0pt]
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}&\!\mapsto&\!\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}\,,\\[5.0pt]
y_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}&\!\mapsto&\!\sum_{j=1}^{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\overline{c}_{j,i}\overline{X}_{1,j}\,.\end{array}

Then
\eta
is a differential homomorphism
if and only if we have

(52)

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathrm{REL}_{i}(\sum\overline{c}_{j,1}\overline{X}_{1,j},\dots,\sum\overline{c}_{j,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\overline{X}_{1,j},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})&\!=&\!0\,,\quad i=1,\dots,k\,,\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{REL}^{\neq}(\sum\overline{c}_{j,1}\overline{X}_{1,j},\dots,\sum\overline{c}_{j,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\overline{X}_{1,j},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})&\!\neq&\!0\,,\end{array}

where
\mathrm{REL}_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})
and
\mathrm{REL}^{\neq}(\boldsymbol{Z},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})
are obtained from
\mathrm{REL}_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
\mathrm{REL}^{\neq}(\boldsymbol{Z})
respectively as in Proposition
6.11
by applying
\sigma_{\rm inter}
to their coefficients.

Proof.

Extending the differential homomorphism
\varphi
from
Proposition
6.11
to

\varphi\colon D^{-1}C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{\boldsymbol{Z}\}\to D^{-1}C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}\,,

the differential homomorphism

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\widehat{\eta}\colon D^{-1}C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}&\!\to&\!F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]/Q\,,\\[5.0pt]
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})&\!\mapsto&\!\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}\,,\\[5.0pt]
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}&\!\mapsto&\!\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}\,,\\[5.0pt]
Z_{i}&\!\mapsto&\!\sum_{j=1}^{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\overline{c}_{j,i}\overline{X}_{1,j}\end{array}

induces a differential homomorphism
\eta
as required if and only if
it factors as indicated in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{-1}C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{-1}C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]/Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

This is equivalent to

p\big(\sum_{j=1}^{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\overline{c}_{j,1}\overline{X}_{1,j},\ldots,\sum_{j=1}^{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\overline{c}_{j,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\overline{X}_{1,j},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}\big)\,=\,0\quad\text{for all}\ p\in\ker(\varphi)\,,

which by Proposition
6.11
is equivalent to (
52
).
∎

We are ready now to present an algorithm which computes representatives

\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\,:=\,(\widehat{v}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{v}_{l}),\quad\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}}\,:=\,(\widehat{\exp}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\exp}_{l})\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\quad\mbox{for }\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}

in
F(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})
of residue classes in
E_{\rm red}

defining a specialization
\sigma_{\rm red}
as in Proposition
9.9
.
Lemma
9.11
gives a criterion such that the map
\eta
associating to the generic basis a basis of the solution space of

\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y\,=\,0

in
E_{\rm red}
is a differential homomorphism.
Once we know the differential homomorphism
\eta
, we obtain
residue classes

\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}+Q
,
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}}+Q
and
\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}+Q
as the images under
\eta
(applied to numerator and denominator) of

\begin{array}[]{l}\exp_{1}\,=\,\mathrm{EXP}_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\quad\dots,\quad\exp_{l}\,=\,\mathrm{EXP}_{l}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\\[5.0pt]
\qquad\mathrm{int}_{i}\,=\,\mathrm{INT}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})\quad\text{for}\ \beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}\end{array}

and of

v_{1}\,=\,V_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\quad\ldots,\quad v_{l}\,=\,V_{l}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}).

To determine the differential homomorphism
\eta
we make for each
y_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}
the ansatz

c_{i,1}X_{1,1}+\dots+c_{i,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}X_{1,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}

with
(c_{i,j})
an invertible matrix of constant indeterminates.
Then we use the differential Thomas decomposition to compute polynomial conditions on
c_{i,j}
such that

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{REL}_{s}(\sum c_{j,1}X_{1,j},\dots,\sum c_{j,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}X_{1,j},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})=0\ \text{mod}\ Q\quad\text{for}\ s=1,\dots,k\,,\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{REL}^{\neq}(\sum c_{j,1}X_{1,j},\dots,\sum c_{j,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}X_{1,j},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\neq 0\ \text{mod}\ Q\,.\end{array}

Input:

•

Generators
q_{1},\dots,q_{s}
of
Q\unlhd F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]
from Definition
9.6
,

•

the specialized least common left multiple
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)
,

•

the specialized
\mathrm{REL}_{s}(\boldsymbol{Z},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})

and
\mathrm{REL}^{\neq}(\boldsymbol{Z},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})
for
s=1,\dots,k
,

•

\mathrm{EXP}_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
V_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{Z})
for
j=1
, …,
l
and

\mathrm{INT}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{Z})
for
i=1,\dots,m
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
.

Output:

Representatives
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
in
F(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})
of the residue classes in
\Frac(F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]/Q)
corresponding to
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\mathrm{int}_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,m
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
as in Proposition
9.9
.

Let
S_{\mathrm{eqs}}=\emptyset
and
S_{\mathrm{ineqs}}=\emptyset
.

Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
and
c_{i,j}
with
i,j=1,\dots,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}
be differential indeterminates over
F
. We will compute a differential Thomas decomposition of a system of equations and inequations in
F\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}},c_{i,j}\}

Apply the substitution

X_{i,j}\mapsto x_{j}^{(i-1)}

to the generators
q_{1},\dots,q_{s}
of
Q
and append the resulting differential polynomials to
S_{\mathrm{eqs}}
.

For
j=1,\dots,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}
append
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,x_{j}
to
S_{\mathrm{eqs}}
.

For
i,j=1,\ldots,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}
append
c_{i,j}^{\prime}
to
S_{\mathrm{eqs}}
and
\det(c_{i,j})
to
S_{\mathrm{ineqs}}
.

Append the differential polynomials

\displaystyle\mathrm{REL}_{s}(\sum_{r}c_{1,r}x_{r},\dots,\sum_{r}c_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},r}x_{r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\quad\text{for}\ s=1,\dots,k

to
S_{\mathrm{eqs}}
and
\mathrm{REL}^{\neq}(\sum_{r}c_{1,r}x_{r},\dots,\sum_{r}c_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},r}x_{r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})
to
S_{\mathrm{ineqs}}
.

For
j=1,\dots,l
and for
i=1,\dots,m
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
append to
S_{\mathrm{ineqs}}
the denominators of

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{EXP}_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\sum_{r}c_{1,r}x_{r},\dots,\sum_{r}c_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},r}x_{r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\,,\\[5.0pt]
V_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\sum_{r}c_{1,r}x_{r},\dots,\sum_{r}c_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},r}x_{r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\quad\mathrm{and}\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{INT}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\sum_{r}c_{1,r}x_{r},\dots,\sum_{r}c_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},r}x_{r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}).\end{array}

With respect to an elimination ranking satisfying

x_{1}>\dots>x_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\gg c_{1,1}>\dots>c_{1,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}>\dots>c_{l,1}>\dots>c_{l,n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}

compute a Thomas decomposition of the differential system with equations
S_{\mathrm{eqs}}
and inequations
S_{\mathrm{ineqs}}
.

Choose a simple system and determine a solution
\overline{c}_{i,j}
of the equations and inequations only involving
c_{i,j}
.

Define for
j=1,\dots,l
and for
i=1,\dots,m
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
the rational functions

\displaystyle\widehat{\exp}_{j}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathrm{EXP}_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{1,r}X_{1,r},\dots,\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},r}X_{1,r})\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{j}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle V_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{1,r}X_{1,r},\dots,\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},r}X_{1,r})\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathrm{INT}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{1,r}X_{1,r},\dots,\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},r}X_{1,r}))\,.

return
(
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}=(\widehat{v}_{1},\dots,\widehat{v}_{l})
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\exp}}=(\widehat{\exp}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\exp}_{l})
,
\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\ \text{with}\ \beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
)

Algorithm 2

Match Bases

Proposition 9.12
.

Algorithm
2
is correct and terminates.

Proof.

The algorithm terminates, since the Thomas decomposition terminates.

Since the map

\begin{array}[]{rcl}F\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}/Q_{1}&\!\to&\!F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]/Q\,,\\[5.0pt]
\overline{x}_{j}^{(i-1)}&\!\mapsto&\!\overline{X}_{i,j}\,,\end{array}

where
Q_{1}
is the differential ideal generated by the differential polynomials obtained from substituting
X_{i,j}
in
q_{1},\dots,q_{s}
by

x_{j}^{(i-1)}
and the differential polynomials
\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,x_{j}=0
, is a differential isomorphism, the ideal
Q_{1}
is a maximal differential ideal.
Let
\widehat{Q}
be the differential ideal in
F\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}},c_{i,j}\}
generated by the elements of
S_{\rm eqs}
obtained in step 6, i.e. by the generators of
Q_{1}
and
c_{i,j}^{\prime}
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{REL}_{s}(\sum_{r}c_{1,r}x_{r},\dots,\sum_{r}c_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},r}x_{r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\quad\text{for}\ s=1,\dots,k.

We are going to show that
\widehat{Q}
is a proper differential ideal which does not contain the inequations
S_{\rm ineqs}
so that the Thomas decomposition consists of at least one simple differential system.
To this end, observe first that
\widehat{Q}
contains the ideal
(Q_{1})
generated by
Q_{1}
. Now Proposition
9.9
implies the existence of a matrix
(\overline{c}_{i,j})\in\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}(C)
such that the restriction of
\sigma_{\rm red}
to

D^{-1}C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\}

yields a differential homomorphism
\eta
as in Lemma
9.11
(cf.
\varphi
in the proof of Proposition
9.9
).
It follows now from Lemma
9.11
that substituting
c_{i,j}
by
\overline{c}_{i,j}
maps the differential ideal
\widehat{Q}
of
F\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}},c_{i,j}\}
to the differential ideal
Q_{1}
of
F\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}
showing that
\widehat{Q}
is proper.
We now address the inequalities.
Lemma
9.11
implies that substituting
c_{i,j}
by
\overline{c}_{i,j}
does not map

\mathrm{REL}^{\neq}(\sum_{r}c_{1,r}x_{r},\dots,\sum_{r}c_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},r}x_{r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})

into the differential ideal
Q_{1}
and hence this differential polynomial does not lie in
\widehat{Q}
. Moreover, it follows from Remark
9.10
that the denominators of

\mathrm{EXP}_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}),\ V_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{INT}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})

do not lie in the kernel of
\eta
. In other words, if we substitute
c_{i,j}
by
\overline{c}_{i,j}
in the denominators of

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{EXP}_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\sum_{r}c_{1,r}x_{r},\dots,\sum_{r}c_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},r}x_{r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\,,\\[5.0pt]
V_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\sum_{r}c_{1,r}x_{r},\dots,\sum_{r}c_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},r}x_{r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\quad\mathrm{and}\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{INT}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(\sum_{r}c_{1,r}x_{r},\dots,\sum_{r}c_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},r}x_{r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\,,\end{array}

the obtained differential polynomials in
F\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}
do not lie
Q_{1}
.
Therefore, these denominators do not lie in
\widehat{Q}
. Finally, since
\widehat{Q}\cap F\{c_{i,j}\}=(0)
, we have that
c_{i,j}^{\prime}
and
\det(c_{i,j})
are not contained in
\widehat{Q}
. Overall, we conclude that the Thomas decomposition applied to
S_{\rm eqs}
and
S_{\rm ineqs}
returns at least one simple system.

Since the
c_{i,j}
are ranked lowest, each simple system returned by the differential Thomas decomposition computed in step 8 has the following elimination property:
each
(\overline{c}_{i,j})\in\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}(C)
satisfying all equations and inequations of the simple system that only involve the indeterminates
c_{i,j}
can be extended to a solution
(\overline{x}_{k},\overline{c}_{i,j})
of the simple system,
where
\overline{x}_{k}
belong to some differential extension field of
F
.
Thus each such
(\overline{c}_{i,j})
yields a surjective differential homomorphism

\varphi\colon F\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}},c_{i,j}\}\to F\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\},c_{i,j}\mapsto\overline{c}_{i,j}

with the property that
\varphi(\widehat{Q})
is a proper differential ideal of
F\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\}

containing
Q_{1}
. Since
Q_{1}
was maximal, we have
Q_{1}=\varphi(\widehat{Q})
and so

\displaystyle\mathrm{REL}_{s}(\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{1,i}x_{r},\dots,\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},i}x_{r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\quad\text{for}\ s=1,\dots,k

are elements of
Q_{1}
. Moreover, by the definition of
S_{\rm ineqs}
we have that

\mathrm{REL}^{\neq}(\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{1,i}x_{r},\dots,\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},i}x_{r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\notin Q_{1}.

We conclude that

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{REL}_{s}(\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{1,i}X_{1,r},\dots,\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},i}X_{1,r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\in Q\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{REL}^{\neq}(\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{1,i}X_{1,r},\dots,\sum_{r}\overline{c}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}},i}X_{1,r},\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base})\notin Q\end{array}

and so Lemma
9.11
implies that the map

\displaystyle\eta\colon D^{-1}C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\{y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}\}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle F[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}]/Q\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}\,,

\displaystyle y_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}\overline{c}_{j,i}\overline{X}_{1,j}

is a differential homomorphism.
By the definition of
S_{\rm ineqs}
the denominators of
