\mathrm{EXP}_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})
,
V_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})
for
j=1,\ldots,l
and
\mathrm{INT}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}})
for
1\leq i\leq m
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
do not lie in the kernel of
\eta
. Thus, we can extend
\eta
to localizations of its domain and codomain respectively such that these denominators
and their images are contained in the respective multiplicatively closed subsets and denote this map again by
\eta
. Then

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\eta(\mathrm{EXP}_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}))&\!=&\!\widehat{\exp}_{j}+Q,\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle\eta(V_{j}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}))&\!=&\!\widehat{v}_{j}+Q,\\[5.0pt]
\eta(\mathrm{INT}_{i}^{I^{\prime\prime}}(y_{1}^{I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,y_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}^{I^{\prime\prime}}))&\!=&\!\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}+Q.\end{array}

∎

10.
The Structure of the Reductive Part

In this section we extend the specialization
\sigma_{\rm red}
computed in Section
9
to a specialization

\underline{\sigma}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]\to\underline{E},

where the differential field
\underline{E}
is the field of fractions of a certain differential integral domain
\underline{R}
containing
E_{\rm red}
.
For maximal differential ideals
I_{\mathrm{max}}\unlhd\underline{R}
we construct Picard-Vessiot extensions
\overline{\mathcal{E}}=\Frac(\underline{R}/I_{\mathrm{max}})
of
F
,
each of which in turn allows us to construct the announced specialization

\sigma_{\rm PV}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]\to\overline{\mathcal{E}}.

We study the relationship between the group
\underline{H}
of differential
F
-automorphisms of
\underline{E}
and the differential Galois group
H
of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
depending on the choice of
I_{\mathrm{max}}
.
The group
\underline{H}
is the semidirect product of the unipotent radical
R_{u}(P_{J})
of the parabolic group
P_{J}
computed in Section
8
and a Levi factor of the differential Galois group
H
.
The choice of
I_{\mathrm{max}}
determines the unipotent radical
R_{u}(H)\leq R_{u}(P_{J})
of
H
as well as which Levi groups of
\underline{H}
are Levi groups of
H
.
More precisely, the Levi groups of
\underline{H}
are all conjugate by elements of
R_{u}(P_{J})
. In case
R_{u}(H)
is properly contained in
R_{u}(P_{J})
, the set of Levi groups may decompose into different orbits under
R_{u}(H)
. The Levi groups of
H
will correspond to one such orbit.

For the construction of the extension
\underline{\sigma}
and
\underline{E}
let
\mathrm{intrad}_{i}
for
\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
be differential indeterminates over
E_{\rm red}
and let
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathrm{int}}}
be the
m
-tuple whose
i
-th entry, for
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
, is a rational function
\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
as in Proposition
9.9
,
and whose
i
-th entry with
\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
is the indeterminate
\mathrm{intrad}_{i}
. Let

I_{\mathrm{uni}}\,:=\,\langle\mathrm{intrad}_{i}^{\prime}-\text{integrand of}\,\mathrm{int}_{i}\rangle

be the differential ideal in
E_{\rm red}\{\mathrm{intrad}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}\}
,
where in the integrand of
\mathrm{int}_{i}
, that is
\mathrm{int}_{i}^{\prime}
, one substitutes
\boldsymbol{\exp}
,
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
by the respective residue classes of
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}}
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
in
E_{\rm red}
and
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
by the respective
indeterminate
\mathrm{intrad}_{i}
.
We define

\underline{R}\,:=\,E_{\rm red}\{\mathrm{intrad}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}\}/I_{\mathrm{uni}}.

Remark 10.1
.

The differential ring
\underline{R}
is an integral domain, since
I_{\mathrm{uni}}

is generated by differential polynomials which involve the pairwise distinct highest derivatives
\mathrm{intrad}_{i}^{\prime}
only linearly with constant coefficient and the remaining terms lie in

E_{\rm red}[\mathrm{intrad}_{j}\mid\beta_{j}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}\ \text{with}\ |0pt(\beta_{j})|<|0pt(\beta_{i})|]

implying triangularity.
We denote its field of fractions by
\underline{E}=\Frac(\underline{R})
.

Then
\sigma_{\rm red}
extends to a differential homomorphism

(53)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\underline{\sigma}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]&\!\to&\!\underline{E}\,,\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{int}_{i}&\!\mapsto&\!\underline{\mathrm{intrad}}_{i}\quad\text{for}\quad\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}.\end{array}

Since
\underline{\sigma}
is a differential homomorphism, the specialization of the generic fundamental matrix

\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,:=\,\underline{\sigma}(\mathcal{Y})\in G(\underline{R})

satisfies

\partial(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})\,=\,A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}.

If we denote by
\underline{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}
the residue classes of
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{f}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}
in
\underline{R}
, respectively, then the matrix
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}=\underline{\sigma}(\mathcal{Y})
decomposes explicitly as

(54)

\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}).

Note that
\underline{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
are elements of
E_{\rm red}
.

The differential ring
\underline{R}
is not necessarily differentially simple, unless
I_{\mathrm{uni}}
is a maximal differential ideal. If this is not the case, we can choose a maximal differential ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}
in
\underline{R}

and then take the quotient

\overline{R}\,:=\,\underline{R}/I_{\mathrm{max}}

obtaining a differentially simple ring
\overline{R}
.
Since
I_{\mathrm{max}}
is prime,
\overline{R}
is an integral domain and we define

\overline{\mathcal{E}}\,:=\,\Frac(\overline{R}).

From
\underline{\sigma}
we construct our final extension of the differential homomorphism
\sigma_{\rm red}
to the differential homomorphism

(55)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma_{\rm PV}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]&\!\to&\!\overline{\mathcal{E}},\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{int}_{i}&\!\mapsto&\!\underline{\mathrm{intrad}}_{i}+I_{\mathrm{max}}\quad\text{for}\quad\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}.\end{array}

Moreover, we denote by
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and

\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}
the residue classes of
\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}
in
\overline{R}
, respectively.
Note that the elements
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and

\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
for
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
are elements of
E_{\rm red}
and

\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}=\overline{\mathrm{intrad}}_{i}
for
\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
lies in

\overline{R}\setminus E_{\rm red}
. We let

(56)

\mathcal{\overline{Y}}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})\,.

Recall from Section
7
that we denoted the roots of
\Psi^{-}
by
\beta_{j_{1}},\dots,\beta_{j_{k}}

and the roots of the complement
\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
by

\beta_{j_{k+1}},\dots,\beta_{j_{m}}
.
Similarly as in Remark
7.5
we apply Lemma
7.4
to the matrix

\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})
and
\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})
and obtain the decompositions

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u_{\beta_{j_{1}}}(\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{k}}}(\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{k}})\cdot u_{\beta_{j_{k+1}}}(\underline{y}_{j_{k+1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{m}}}(\underline{y}_{j_{m}})

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u_{\beta_{j_{1}}}(\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{k}}}(\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{k}})\cdot u_{\beta_{j_{k+1}}}(\overline{y}_{j_{k+1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{m}}}(\overline{y}_{j_{m}})

with
\underline{y}_{j_{k+1}},\dots,\underline{y}_{j_{m}}\in\underline{R}\setminus E_{\rm red}
and
\overline{y}_{j_{k+1}},\dots,\overline{y}_{j_{m}}\in\overline{R}
.
Thus the matrices

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}&:=&\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,u_{j_{1}}(\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{j_{k}}(\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{k}})\in G(E_{\rm red})\,,\\[5.0pt]
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}&:=&\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,u_{j_{1}}(\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{j_{k}}(\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{k}})\in G(E_{\rm red})\,,\\[5.0pt]
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}&:=&u_{\beta_{j_{k+1}}}(\underline{y}_{j_{k+1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{m}}}(\underline{y}_{j_{m}})\in R_{u}(P_{J})(\underline{R})\,,\\[5.0pt]
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}&:=&u_{\beta_{j_{k+1}}}(\overline{y}_{j_{k+1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{m}}}(\overline{y}_{j_{m}})\in R_{u}(P_{J})(\overline{R})\end{array}

satisfy
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}=\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}
and
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}=\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}
. Note that
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}=\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
.

Proposition 10.2
.

Using the above notation we have:

(a)

The differential field
E_{\rm red}
is generated as a field over
F
by
\underline{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
and we have

E_{\rm red}\subset\Frac(F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}])\,=\,\underline{E}\,.

(b)

The differential field
E_{\rm red}
is generated as a field over
F
by
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
and we have

E_{\rm red}\subset\Frac(F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])\,=\,\overline{\mathcal{E}}.

(c)

The differential ring
F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}]

is a Picard-Vessiot ring over
F
for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
with fundamental matrix
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
.
Its differential Galois group
H(C)
is a subgroup of
P_{J}(C)
.

(d)

The differential ring
E_{\rm red}[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}]
is a Picard-Vessiot ring over
E_{\rm red}
for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
with fundamental matrix
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
. Its differential Galois group is a subgroup of
R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
.

Proof.

(a)
Recall from Theorem
6.3
that
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
for

(57)

\LCLM(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y\,=\,0

and that
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
is generated as a field by
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{f}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
.
As a consequence we have that
the basis elements
y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\dots,y^{I^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
of the solution space of (
57
) and their derivatives can be expressed as rational functions in
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{f}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
over
F\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle(\boldsymbol{p})
.
From Proposition
9.4

(c)
we obtain that
\sigma_{\rm PV}
specializes this basis to a basis in
E_{\rm red}
of the specialized least common left multiple.
Since the specialized basis and its derivatives generate
E_{\rm red}
as a field over
F
, the same is true for
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}=\underline{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}=\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}=\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}=\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
.
From
[
Seia
, Lemma 4.2]
and the Bruhat decomposition in (
54
) we conclude that all parameters
\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}
are in
\Frac(F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}])
and so
E_{\rm red}
is contained in
\Frac(F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}])
.
Since
\underline{E}
is generated as a field by
\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
over
E_{\rm red}
and these elements are contained in
\Frac(F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}])
, it follows that

\underline{E}=\Frac(F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}])
.

(b)
Since
F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}]\subset\overline{\mathcal{E}}
, the ring

F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}]
is an integral domain and so we can consider
\Frac(F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])
. As above we conclude from
[
Seia
, Lemma 4.2]
and the Bruhat decomposition in (
56
) that all parameters
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}
are in
\Frac(F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])
.
Since
\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
are the same elements in
E_{\rm red}
as the elements
\underline{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
, the first statement follows from
(a)
. Since for
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
we have that
\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\in\Frac(F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])
and these elements generate
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
as a field over
E_{\rm red}
, we conclude that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}=\Frac(F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])
.

(c)
Recall that the constants of
E_{\rm red}
are
C
. Since
\underline{R}/I_{\mathrm{max}}=\overline{R}
is differentially simple and finitely generated over
E_{\rm red}
, the constants of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}=\Frac(F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])
are
C
.
Clearly
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
is a fundamental matrix for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
and its entries are contained in

\Frac(F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])
.
Obviously,
\Frac(F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])
is generated as a field over
F
by the entries of
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
and so

\Frac(F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])
is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F
for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
with fundamental matrix
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
. It follows now from the proof of
[
vdPS03
, Proposition 1.22]
that
F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}]
is a Picard-Vessiot ring for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
over
F
with fundamental matrix
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
.

Since by the construction of
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
the parameters
\overline{v}_{i_{r+1}},\dots,\overline{v}_{i_{l}}
are in
F
,
Theorem
5.3
implies that the differential Galois group
H
is contained in
P_{J}
.

(d)

Since
E_{\rm red}\subset\Frac(F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])
by
(b)
, we conclude that

\Frac(F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])\,=\,\Frac(E_{\rm red}[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])

and so it follows from
(c)
that
\Frac(E_{\rm red}[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])

is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
E_{\rm red}
for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
with fundamental matrix
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
.
Again, the proof of
[
vdPS03
, Proposition 1.22]
shows that
E_{\rm red}[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}]

is a Picard-Vessiot ring over
E_{\rm red}
for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
.
Let
\gamma
be a differential
E_{\rm red}
-automorphism of
\Frac(E_{\rm red}[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}])
and
C_{\gamma}\in P_{J}(C)
such that
\gamma(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})=\mathcal{\overline{Y}}\,C_{\gamma}
.
Then we obtain

\gamma(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad})\,=\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,\gamma(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad})\,=\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}\,C_{\gamma}\,,

which is equivalent to
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}^{-1}\gamma(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad})=C_{\gamma}
. Since the algebraic group
R_{u}(P_{J})
is defined over
C
, we obtain that
\gamma(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad})\in R_{u}(P_{J})(\Frac(E_{\rm red}[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}]))
and so
C_{\gamma}\in R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
.
∎

Next we prove that
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
is a fundamental matrix of a matrix differential equation over
F
and that it induces a representation of
{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(E_{\rm red}/F)
which is contained in the standard Levi group of
P_{J}
.

Proposition 10.3
.

Let

A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}\,:=\,\ell\delta(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}).

Then
A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}\in\Lie(G)(F)
and
E_{\rm red}=\Frac(F[\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red},\det(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}])
is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F
for

A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}
with fundamental matrix
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
. Its differential Galois group
L_{\rm red}(C)
in the representation induced by
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
is contained in the standard Levi group
L_{J}(C)
of
P_{J}(C)
.

Proof.

We show that
A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}=\ell\delta(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\in\Lie(G)(F)
.
To this end we use a result from Section
12
where we shall compute reduction matrices to reduce
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
over an algebraic extension of
F
. Let

\overline{g}_{1}\,:=\,n(\overline{w})^{-1}\,u_{k_{s}}(\overline{x}_{k_{s}})\cdots u_{k_{1}}(\overline{x}_{k_{1}})\in G(F)

be as in Proposition
12.1
. Since
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}=\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
, it follows with Proposition
12.1
that
\overline{g}_{1}\mathcal{\overline{Y}}\in P_{J}(\overline{\mathcal{E}})
and that it decomposes into a product

\overline{g}_{1}\,\mathcal{\overline{Y}}\,=\,(\overline{g}_{1}\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}

with
\overline{g}_{1}\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\in L_{J}(E_{\rm red})
and
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}\in R_{u}(P_{J})(\overline{R})
.
Its logarithmic derivative is

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\ell\delta((\overline{g}_{1}\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad})&\!=&\!\ell\delta(\overline{g}_{1}\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})+(\overline{g}_{1}\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\,\ell\delta(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad})(\overline{g}_{1}\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}\\[5.0pt]
&\!=&\!\overline{g}_{1}.A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})\\[5.0pt]
&\!\in&\!\Lie(G)(F)\,=\,\Lie(L_{J})(F)\oplus\Lie(R_{u}(P_{J}))(F).\end{array}

Since
\ell\delta(\overline{g}_{1}\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\in\Lie(L_{J})
and
(\overline{g}_{1}\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\,\ell\delta(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad})(\overline{g}_{1}\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}\in\Lie(R_{u}(P_{J}))
and the sum decomposition is direct, it follows from the
F
-rationality of
\overline{g}_{1}.A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
that

\ell\delta(\overline{g}_{1}\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\in\Lie(L_{J})(F)
. Gauge transforming
\ell\delta(\overline{g}_{1}\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})
with
\overline{g}_{1}^{-1}\in G(F)
yields that
\ell\delta(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\in\Lie(G)(F)
.

Clearly we have
\Frac(F[\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red},\det(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}])\subset E_{\rm red}
.
The Bruhat decomposition of
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
is given by

\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,u_{j_{1}}(\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{j_{k}}(\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{k}})

and it lies in the big cell. It follows then from
[
Seia
, Lemma 4.2]
that all parameters

\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}

and
\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
are elements of
\Frac(F[\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red},\det(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}])
. Since by Proposition
10.2

(b)
these elements generate
E_{\rm red}
, we have that

\Frac(F[\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red},\det(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}])\,=\,E_{\rm red}\,.

We conclude that
E_{\rm red}
is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F
for
A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}
with fundamental matrix
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
.

Let
\gamma
be a differential
F
-automorphism of
E_{\rm red}
and let
C_{\gamma}\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(C)
be such that
\gamma(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})=\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}C_{\gamma}
. Then we have
\gamma(\overline{g}_{1}\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})=\overline{g}_{1}\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,C_{\gamma}

and since the standard Levi group
L_{J}
of
P_{J}
is defined over
C
, we obtain from

\overline{g}_{1}\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\in L_{J}
that
\gamma(\overline{g}_{1}\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})
is also an element of
L_{J}
.
Hence,
C_{\gamma}\in L_{J}(C)
and so the induced representation
L_{\rm red}(C)
of the differential Galois group is contained in
L_{J}(C)
.
∎

For an
n\times n
matrix
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}=(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j})
of indeterminates over
F
we consider now the substitution homomorphisms

\begin{array}[]{rclrcl}\underline{\varphi}\colon F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]&\!\to&\!F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]\,,&\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}&\!\mapsto&\!\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}\,,\\[5.0pt]
\overline{\varphi}\colon F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]&\!\to&\!F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}]\,,&\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}&\!\mapsto&\!\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{i,j}\,,\\[5.0pt]
\overline{\varphi}_{\rm red}\colon F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]&\!\to&\!F[\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red},\det(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}]\,,&\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}&\!\mapsto&\!(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})_{i,j}\end{array}

and we denote their kernels by
\underline{Q}
,
\overline{Q}
and
\overline{Q}_{\rm red}
, respectively.

Proposition 10.4
.

Denote by
\Stab(\underline{Q})
and
\Stab(\overline{Q})
and
\Stab(\overline{Q}_{\rm red})
the
stabilizer of the ideal
\underline{Q}
and
\overline{Q}
and
\overline{Q}_{\rm red}
, respectively,
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(C)
for the action
g\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}g
.

(a)

Then
H(C)=\Stab(\overline{Q})
and
L_{\rm red}(C)=\Stab(\overline{Q}_{\rm red})
are the differential Galois groups of the Picard-Vessiot
rings
F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}]
and
F[\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red},\det(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}]
,
respectively.

(b)

We define

\underline{H}(C)\,:=\,\Stab(\underline{Q})\,.

Then
\underline{H}(C)
is a linear algebraic group and it is the group of differential
F
-automorphisms of
F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]
. Moreover, we have
\underline{Q}\subset\overline{Q}
and
P_{J}(C)\geq\underline{H}(C)\geq H(C)
.

Proof.

(a)
Extending the derivation of
F
to
F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]

by
\partial(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})=A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})\,\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}
and by
\partial(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})=A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}\,\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}
respectively, turns the surjective homomorphisms
\overline{\varphi}
and
\overline{\varphi}_{\rm red}
into differential homomorphisms.
Thus, the ideals
\overline{Q}
and
\overline{Q}_{\rm red}
are maximal differential ideals and so their stabilizers define the respective differential Galois groups.
Since the induced differential isomorphisms send fundamental matrices to fundamental matrices, we obtain that
H(C)=\Stab(\overline{Q})
and
L_{\rm red}(C)=\Stab(\overline{Q}_{\rm red})
.

(b)
Using the same arguments as in the proof which shows that the differential Galois group is a linear algebraic group (cf.
[
vdPS03
, Thm. 1.27 (1)]
), we obtain that
\underline{H}
is a linear algebraic group.
By definition,
the differential homomorphism
\overline{\varphi}
factors as
\psi\circ\underline{\varphi}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

Thus we have
\underline{Q}\subset\overline{Q}
and so
\Stab(\underline{Q})\geq\Stab(\overline{Q})
.

Every matrix
g\in\Stab(\underline{Q})
induces by right multiplication on
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}
a differential
F
-automorphism of
F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]/\underline{Q}
.
Since

\rho\colon F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]/\underline{Q}\to F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}],\ \widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}+\underline{Q}\mapsto\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}

is a differential
F
-isomorphism mapping
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}+\underline{Q}
to
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
, the matrix
g
also induces a differential
F
-automorphism on
F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]
by
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\mapsto\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,g
, i.e.,
\rho
is
\underline{H}
-equivariant.
Thus the values
\overline{v}_{i_{r+1}},\dots,\overline{v}_{i_{l}}
of the parameters of the root groups
U_{-\alpha_{i_{r+1}}},\dots,U_{-\alpha_{i_{l}}}
in the Bruhat decomposition of
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
have to be the same in the Bruhat decomposition of
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,g
.
By Lemma
5.2
this is only possible for elements of
P_{J}(C)
and so
\Stab(\underline{Q})\leq P_{J}(C)
.
∎

Lemma 10.5
.

For a field
K\supset C
and
\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\in K^{m}
,
\boldsymbol{x}_{2}\in(K^{\times})^{l}
and
\boldsymbol{x}_{3}\in K^{m}
let

Y\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{x}_{2})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x}_{3})\,.

Moreover, for
p_{1}
,
p_{2}\in P_{J}(C)
let

\ell_{1}
,
\ell_{2}\in\widetilde{L}(C)
and
u_{1}
,
u_{2}\in R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
be the unique elements such that
p_{1}=\ell_{1}\,u_{1}
and
p_{2}=\ell_{2}\,u_{2}
, where
\widetilde{L}
is a Levi group of
P_{J}
.
Assume that the coefficients
\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\boldsymbol{a}_{3},\boldsymbol{b}_{1},\boldsymbol{b}_{3}\in K^{m}
and
\boldsymbol{a}_{2},\boldsymbol{b}_{2}\in(K^{\times})^{l}
in the Bruhat decompositions

Yp_{1}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{a}_{1})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{a}_{2})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{a}_{3})\qquad\text{and}\qquad Yp_{2}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{b}_{1})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{b}_{2})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{b}_{3})

satisfy
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}=\boldsymbol{b}_{1}
,

\boldsymbol{a}_{2}=\boldsymbol{b}_{2}
and

a_{3,i}=b_{3,i}
for all
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
.
Then
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{a}_{3})
(resp.
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{b}_{3})
) means a product of root group elements of fixed order with parameter values
\boldsymbol{a}_{3}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{b}_{3}
).
Applying Lemma
7.4
to
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{a}_{3})
and
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{b}_{3})
we obtain

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{a}_{3})&\!=&\!u_{\beta_{j_{1}}}(a_{3,j_{1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{k}}}(a_{3,j_{k}})\cdot u_{\beta_{j_{k+1}}}(y_{j_{k+1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{m}}}(y_{j_{m}})\quad\text{and}\\[5.0pt]
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{b}_{3})&\!=&\!u_{\beta_{j_{1}}}(b_{3,j_{1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{k}}}(b_{3,j_{k}})\cdot u_{\beta_{j_{k+1}}}(x_{j_{k+1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{m}}}(x_{j_{m}})\,,\end{array}

where

\begin{array}[]{rcl}u_{\beta_{j_{k+1}}}(y_{j_{k+1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{m}}}(y_{j_{m}})&\!\in&\!R_{u}(P_{J})(K)\quad\text{and}\\[5.0pt]
u_{\beta_{j_{k+1}}}(x_{j_{k+1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{m}}}(x_{j_{m}})&\!\in&\!R_{u}(P_{J})(K).\end{array}

Since

a_{3,i}=b_{3,i}
for all
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
, we have that

u_{\beta_{j_{1}}}(a_{3,j_{1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{k}}}(a_{3,j_{k}})\,=\,u_{\beta_{j_{1}}}(b_{3,j_{1}})\cdots u_{\beta_{j_{k}}}(b_{3,j_{k}})\,.

Normality of
R_{u}(P_{J})(K)
implies now that there exists
\widetilde{u}\in R_{u}(P_{J})(K)
such that

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{a}_{3})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{b}_{3})^{-1}\,=\,\widetilde{u}\,\iff\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{a}_{3})\,=\,\widetilde{u}\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{b}_{3})\,.

Normality again implies that there exists
u\in R_{u}(P_{J})(K)
such that
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{a}_{3})=\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{b}_{3})\,u
and so we find that

(58)

Yp_{1}\,=\,Yp_{2}\,u\,\iff\,\ell_{1}\,u_{1}\,=\,\ell_{2}\,u_{2}\,u\,\iff\,\ell_{2}^{-1}\,\ell_{1}\,=\,u_{2}\,u\,u_{1}^{-1}\,.

Since for the two factors of the semidirect product
P_{J}(K)=\widetilde{L}(K)\ltimes R_{u}(P_{J})(K)
, we have that

\widetilde{L}(K)\cap R_{u}(P_{J})(K)=\{\mathrm{id}\}
and
\ell_{2}^{-1}\ell_{1}\in\widetilde{L}(K)
and
u_{2}\,u\,u_{1}^{-1}\in R_{u}(P_{J})(K)
, it follows from (
58
) that

\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}
and
u=u_{2}^{-1}\,u_{1}
.
∎

The following lemma shows that the group of differential
F
-automorphisms of
\underline{E}
allows to recover the differential Galois group of the reductive part
E_{\rm red}
over
F
.

Lemma 10.6
.

Denote by
\Aut_{\partial}(\underline{E}/F)
the group of differential
F
-automorphisms of
\underline{E}
induced by right multiplication of elements of
\underline{H}(C)
on
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Then every
\gamma\in\Aut_{\partial}(\underline{E}/F)
restricts to a differential
F
-automorphism of
E_{\rm red}
. Moreover, the map

(59)

\mathrm{Aut}_{\partial}(\underline{E}/F)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(E_{\rm red}/F),\ \gamma\mapsto\gamma\big|_{E_{\rm red}}

is a surjective group homomorphism.

Proof.

Since the extension
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
is a Picard-Vessiot extension with differential Galois group
P_{J}(C)
and since
R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
is normal in
P_{J}(C)
, every differential
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
-automorphism
\gamma
of
\mathcal{E}
restricts to a differential
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
-automorphism of
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{x}_{1}\in\mathcal{E}^{m}
,
\boldsymbol{x}_{2}\in(\mathcal{E}^{\times})^{l}
and
\boldsymbol{x}_{3}\in\mathcal{E}^{m}
be the coefficients of the Bruhat decomposition

(60)

\displaystyle\gamma(\mathcal{Y})\!

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!\mathcal{Y}\,g\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})\,g

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{x}_{2})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x}_{3})

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\!\boldsymbol{u}(\gamma(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}))\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\gamma(\boldsymbol{\exp}))\,\boldsymbol{u}(\gamma(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}))\,.

Since by Theorem
6.3

(b)
the elements
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{f}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
generate
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
over
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
and so in particular lie in
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{j})}
, we obtain that
\boldsymbol{x}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{x}_{2}
and
x_{3,i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
are again elements of
\mathcal{E}^{R_{u}(P_{J})}
, i.e., they are rational expressions over
\mathcal{E}^{P_{J}}
in
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{f}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
and
\mathrm{int}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
.

We are going to show below that for every
g\in\underline{H}(C)\leq P_{J}(C)
we can specialize the elements
\boldsymbol{x}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{x}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{x}_{3}
to
\underline{E}
. Since
\underline{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}

with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
are elements of
E_{\rm red}
, it will then follow that the specialized elements
\boldsymbol{x}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{x}_{2}
and
x_{3,i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
are again in
E_{\rm red}
.
Since according to Proposition
10.2

(a)
the elements
\underline{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}

\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
generate
E_{\rm red}
over
F
, we conclude that the differential
F
-automorphism of
\underline{E}
induced by
g
restricts to a differential
F
-automorphism of
E_{\rm red}
.

Recall from (
53
) the specialization

\underline{\sigma}\colon D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]\to\underline{E}\,.

Since the entries of
\mathcal{Y}
are contained in
D^{-1}F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]
and the entries of
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
are contained in the image of
\underline{\sigma}
, we obtain a specialization

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\widetilde{\sigma}\colon F[\mathcal{Y},\det(\mathcal{Y})^{-1}]&\!\to&\!\Frac(F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}])\,=\,\underline{E}\,,\\[5.0pt]
\mathcal{Y}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})&\!\mapsto&\!\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})\,=\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,.\end{array}

Since for
g\in\underline{H}(C)\leq P_{J}(C)
and
a(\mathcal{Y})\in F[\mathcal{Y},\det(\mathcal{Y})^{-1}]
we have

\widetilde{\sigma}(a(\mathcal{Y}).g)\,=\,\widetilde{\sigma}(a(\mathcal{Y}g))\,=\,a(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}g)\,=\,a(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}).g\,=\,\widetilde{\sigma}(a(\mathcal{Y})).g\,,

we conclude that
\widetilde{\sigma}
is an
\underline{H}
-equivariant differential
F
-homomorphism.
Thus, the kernel
\widetilde{Q}
of
\widetilde{\sigma}
is stabilized by
\underline{H}(C)
.

Let
C[G]=C[\overline{X},\det(\overline{X})^{-1}]
be the coordinate ring of
G
.
We are going to show that the Bruhat decomposition

\overline{X}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{y})

specializes to the Bruhat decompositions of both
\mathcal{Y}
and
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
, where
\boldsymbol{x}
,
\boldsymbol{z}
and
\boldsymbol{y}
are rational functions over
C
in the coordinates
\overline{X}
of
G
.
The varieties
U^{-}\times T\times U^{-}
and
G
are birationally equivalent; more precisely, according to the proof of
[
Seia
, Lemma 4.2]
the product morphism

\varphi\colon U^{-}\times T\times U^{-}\to G,\,(u_{1},t,u_{2})\mapsto u_{1}\,n(\overline{w})\,t\,u_{2}

is an isomorphism onto the open subset
U^{-}n(\overline{w})B^{-}
of
G
. Thus, the denominators of these rational functions do not vanish at any point of
U^{-}(K)\,n(\overline{w})\,B^{-}(K)\subset G(K)
for any field extension
K
of
C
.
Since

\mathcal{Y}\in U^{-}(\mathcal{E})\,n(\overline{w})\,B^{-}(\mathcal{E})\subset G(\mathcal{E})\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\in U^{-}(\underline{E})\,n(\overline{w})\,B^{-}(\underline{E})\subset G(\underline{E})\,,

the coefficients
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}
and
\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}},\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}
of the respective Bruhat decomposition are obtained by evaluating
\boldsymbol{x}
,
\boldsymbol{z}
and
\boldsymbol{y}
at
\mathcal{Y}
and
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
, respectively.

Let
\widetilde{p}
be one of the components of
\boldsymbol{x}
,
\boldsymbol{z}
,
\boldsymbol{y}
and let
a_{1}(\overline{X})
,
a_{2}(\overline{X})\in C[\overline{X},\det(\overline{X})^{-1}]
such that

\widetilde{p}=a_{1}(\overline{X})/a_{2}(\overline{X})
. Then by the above we obtain the respective component
p
of
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{\exp}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}
and the respective component
\underline{p}
of
\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}},\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}
by evaluating
\widetilde{p}
at
\mathcal{Y}
and
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
respectively, i.e., we have

p\,=\,\widetilde{p}(\mathcal{Y})\,=\,\frac{a_{1}(\mathcal{Y})}{a_{2}(\mathcal{Y})}\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{p}\,=\,\widetilde{p}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})\,=\,\frac{a_{1}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})}{a_{2}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})}

with
a_{2}(\mathcal{Y})\neq 0
and
a_{2}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})\neq 0
.
Since
\widetilde{\sigma}(\mathcal{Y})=\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
, we have
\widetilde{\sigma}(a_{2}(\mathcal{Y}))=a_{2}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})\neq 0
implying that
a_{2}(\mathcal{Y})\notin\widetilde{Q}
.
For
g\in\underline{H}(C)
the induced differential
F
-automorphism
\gamma
maps
a_{1}(\mathcal{Y})/a_{2}(\mathcal{Y})
to

\gamma(p)\,=\,\gamma(a_{1}(\mathcal{Y})/a_{2}(\mathcal{Y}))\,=\,x_{k,i}\,=\,a_{1}(\mathcal{Y}g)/a_{2}(\mathcal{Y}g)\quad\text{(cf.\ \eqref{eq:specializebruhat})}.

Since
\widetilde{Q}
is stabilized by
\underline{H}(C)
and
a_{2}(\mathcal{Y})\notin\widetilde{Q}
, we conclude that for every
g\in\underline{H}(C)
the element
a_{2}(\mathcal{Y}g)\notin\widetilde{Q}
and so
\widetilde{\sigma}(a_{2}(\mathcal{Y}g))\neq 0
. Hence, for every element of
\underline{H}(C)
we can extended
\widetilde{\sigma}
to a localization of its domain containing
\boldsymbol{x}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{x}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{x}_{3}
.

It is left to show the surjectivity of the restriction map in (
59
).
Since
R_{u}(H)(C)
is normal in
H(C)
and
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{R_{u}(H)}=E_{\rm red}
, we obtain that the map

{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}/F)\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Gal}_{\partial}(E_{\rm red}/F),\ \gamma\mapsto\gamma\big|_{E_{\rm red}}

is surjective.
So for
\gamma_{\rm red}\in\mathrm{Gal}_{\partial}(E_{\rm red}/F)
let
g\in H(C)
be such that the automorphism
\gamma\in{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}/F)
induced by
g
restricts to
\gamma|_{E_{\rm red}}=\gamma_{\rm red}
.
Since
g\in H(C)\leq\underline{H}(C)\leq P_{J}(C)
, the induced differential
F
-automorphism
\underline{\gamma}
of
\underline{E}
restricts by the first part of our proof to a differential
F
-automorphism
\underline{\gamma}|_{E_{\rm red}}
of
E_{\rm red}
.
We show that
\underline{\gamma}|_{E_{\rm red}}
coincides with
\gamma|_{E_{\rm red}}=\gamma_{\rm red}
proving surjectivity.
To this end we prove that the images of the generators
of
E_{\rm red}
under
\underline{\gamma}|_{E_{\rm red}}
and
\gamma|_{E_{\rm red}}
agree using the uniqueness of the Bruhat decomposition.
Consider the Bruhat decompositions of
\underline{\gamma}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})=\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,g
and
\gamma(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})=\mathcal{\overline{Y}}\,g
, i.e.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,g&\!=&\!\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\underline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{2})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{3})\\[1.99997pt]
&\!=&\!\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\gamma}(\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}))\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\underline{\gamma}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}))\,\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\gamma}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}))\qquad\text{and}\\[5.0pt]
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}\,g&\!=&\!\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{2})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{3})\\[1.99997pt]
&\!=&\!\boldsymbol{u}(\gamma(\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}))\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\gamma(\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}))\,\boldsymbol{u}(\gamma(\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}))\,,\end{array}

and the differential
F
-homomorphism

\widehat{\sigma}\colon F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]\to F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}],\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\mapsto\mathcal{\overline{Y}}

completing the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\mathcal{Y},\det(\mathcal{Y})^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}})^{-1}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\sigma}|_{F[\mathcal{Y},\det(\mathcal{Y})^{-1}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\rm PV}|_{F[\mathcal{Y},\det(\mathcal{Y})^{-1}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\sigma}}

As in case of
\widetilde{\sigma}
one proves that
\widehat{\sigma}
is
H
-equivariant and that one can extend
\widehat{\sigma}
to the parameters
\underline{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}
and
\underline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{2}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{3}
of the Bruhat decompositions of
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
and
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}g
respectively.
Since
\widehat{\sigma}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})=\mathcal{\overline{Y}}
, we conclude with the uniqueness of the Bruhat decomposition that

\widehat{\sigma}(\underline{\boldsymbol{v}})=\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\quad\widehat{\sigma}(\underline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,=\,\overline{\boldsymbol{f}},\quad\widehat{\sigma}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,=\,\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\quad\widehat{\sigma}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})\,=\,\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}}\,.

By construction of
\underline{E}
and
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
we have

\underline{\boldsymbol{v}}\,=\,\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\quad\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}\,=\,\overline{\boldsymbol{f}},\quad\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}\,=\,\overline{\boldsymbol{\exp}},\quad\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\,=\,\overline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\quad\text{with}\ \beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}

in
E_{\rm red}
and since these elements generate
E_{\rm red}
, it follows that
\widehat{\sigma}
is the identity on
E_{\rm red}
. Hence, we obtain

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\widehat{\sigma}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,g)&\!=&\!\boldsymbol{u}(\widehat{\sigma}(\underline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}))\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\widehat{\sigma}(\underline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{2}))\,\boldsymbol{u}(\widehat{\sigma}(\underline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{3}))\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\underline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{2})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\widehat{\sigma}(\underline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{3}))\\[5.0pt]
&\!=&\!\mathcal{\overline{Y}}\,g\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{2})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{3}),\end{array}

where the
i
-th entry in
\widehat{\sigma}(\underline{\boldsymbol{x}}_{3})
such that
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}

satisfies
\widehat{\sigma}(\underline{x}_{3,i})=\underline{x}_{3,i}
.
It follows then from the uniqueness of the Bruhat decomposition that the images of the generators of
E_{\rm red}
under
\underline{\gamma}|_{E_{\rm red}}
and
\gamma|_{E_{\rm red}}
coincide.
∎

Remark 10.7
.

Let
u\in R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
. It follows from Lemma
6.2
that the coefficients

\boldsymbol{x}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{x}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{x}_{3}
in the Bruhat decomposition

\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,u\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})\,u\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{x}_{2})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x}_{3})

satisfy
\boldsymbol{x}_{1}=(\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}})
,
\boldsymbol{x}_{2}=\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
x_{3,i}=\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}

with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
. Hence, the unipotent radical
R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
fixes the elements
\underline{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}}
and
\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}

with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
and so leaves
E_{\rm red}
elementwise fixed.

The following theorem shows that the Levi groups of
\underline{H}(C)
are conjugate by elements of
R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
to the Levi groups of
H(C)
. But not all Levi groups of
\underline{H}(C)
are Levi groups of
H(C)
. Only those Levi groups of
\underline{H}(C)
appear as Levi groups of
H(C)
which stabilize the ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}\lhd\underline{R}
, where
g\in\underline{L}(C)
acts on
\underline{R}
as usually by

\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,g\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}})\,g\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{x}_{2})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x}_{3}).

Theorem 10.8
.

Let
\underline{L}(C)
and
L(C)
be Levi groups of
\underline{H}(C)
and
H(C)
, respectively. Let
L_{\rm red}
be as in Proposition
10.3
.

(a)

We have
R_{u}(\underline{H})(C)=R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
and
\underline{H}(C)=\underline{L}(C)\ltimes R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
is a Levi decomposition of
\underline{H}(C)
.

(b)

The group
L_{\rm red}(C)
is contained in
\underline{H}(C)
.

(c)

The groups
L_{\rm red}(C)
and
L(C)
are Levi groups of
\underline{H}(C)
, i.e., the groups
\underline{L}(C)
,
L(C)
and
L_{\rm red}(C)
are conjugate by elements in
R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
.

Proof.

(a)
We need only to show that
R_{u}(\underline{H})(C)=R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
.
According to Proposition
8.5

(d)
the group
L(C)
is
\widetilde{L}(C)
-irreducible for a Levi group
\widetilde{L}(C)
of
P_{J}(C)
.
As
L(C)
is a reductive subgroup of
\underline{H}(C)
by Proposition
10.4

(b)
, there is a Levi group
\widetilde{\underline{L}}(C)
such that

L(C)\leq\widetilde{\underline{L}}(C)\leq P_{J}(C).

Minimality of
P_{J}(C)
for
L(C)
implies minimality of
P_{J}(C)
for
\widetilde{\underline{L}}(C)
. Thus,
\widetilde{\underline{L}}(C)
is
\widetilde{L}(C)
-irreducible for a Levi group
\widetilde{L}(C)
of
P_{J}(C)
and thus
R_{u}(\underline{H})(C)\leq R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
.
The group
R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
acts on
\underline{R}
leaving
E_{\rm red}
elementwise fixed by Remark
10.7
. Since
\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\in\underline{E}
for
\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
are transcendental
over
E_{\rm red}
, we conclude that

R_{u}(P_{J})(C)\leq\underline{H}(C)
and so
R_{u}(\underline{H})(C)=R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
.

(b)
Let
\ell\in L_{\rm red}
. Then
\ell
induces a differential
F
-automorphism
\gamma
of
E_{\rm red}
by right multiplication on
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}=\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
.
It follows from Lemma
10.6
that there exists
g\in\underline{H}
such that the restriction of the induced differential
F
-automorphism
\underline{\gamma}
of
\underline{E}
is equal to
\gamma
, that is
\underline{\gamma}|_{E_{\rm red}}=\gamma
. Thus

\underline{\gamma}|_{E_{\rm red}}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\,=\,\gamma(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\,=\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,\ell\,.

We compute

\underline{\gamma}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad})\,=\,\underline{\gamma}|_{E_{\rm red}}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\,\underline{\gamma}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad})\,=\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,\ell\,\underline{\gamma}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad})\,=\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}\,g

and obtain

\ell\underline{\gamma}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad})\,=\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}\,g\,\iff\,\ell\,=\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}\,g\,\underline{\gamma}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad})^{-1}\,.

Since the defining ideal of
R_{u}(P_{J})
is defined over
C
and
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}\in R_{u}(P_{J})(\underline{E})
, we conclude that
\underline{\gamma}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad})^{-1}\in R_{u}(P_{J})(\underline{E})
.
Since
R_{u}(P_{J})\leq\underline{H}
according to

(a)
, it follows that
\ell\in\underline{H}(C)
.

(c)
We start by showing first that any Levi group
\underline{L}(C)
of
\underline{H}(C)
is isomorphic to

{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(E_{\rm red}/F)
. As in Lemma
10.6
denote by
\Aut_{\partial}(\underline{E}/F)
the group of differential
F
-automorphisms of
\underline{E}
induced by right multiplication of elements of
\underline{H}(C)
on
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
.
We obtain from Lemma
10.6
that the group homomorphism

\Aut_{\partial}(\underline{E}/F)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(E_{\rm red}/F),\ \underline{\gamma}\mapsto\underline{\gamma}\big|_{E_{\rm red}}

is surjective. We determine its kernel.
Suppose that
g_{1},g_{2}\in\underline{H}(C)
induce

\underline{\gamma}_{1}
and
\underline{\gamma}_{2}
, respectively, such that

\underline{\gamma}_{1}|_{E_{\rm red}}\,=\,\underline{\gamma}_{2}|_{E_{\rm red}}
.
Then the Bruhat decompositions of
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}g_{1}
and
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}g_{2}
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
10.5
.
The same lemma then implies that
g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}\in R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
. Moreover, for every
g\in R_{u}(P_{J})(C)\leq\underline{H}(C)
the induced differential
F
-automorphism restricts to the identity on
E_{\rm red}
.
Thus, the kernel consists of all
\underline{\gamma}

with
\underline{\gamma}(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})=\underline{\mathcal{Y}}g
for some
g\in R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
.
Hence,

\underline{L}(C)\,\cong\,\underline{H}(C)/R_{u}(P_{J})(C)\,\cong\,{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(E_{\rm red}/F)\,.

Since
L_{\rm red}(C)
and
L(C)
are reductive subgroups of
\underline{H}(C)
, there exist Levi groups
\underline{L}_{1}(C)
and
\underline{L}_{2}(C)
of
\underline{H}(C)
such that
L_{\rm red}(C)\leq\underline{L}_{1}(C)
and
L(C)\leq\underline{L}_{2}(C)
.
We have

L_{\rm red}(C)\,\cong\,{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(E_{\rm red}/F)\quad\text{and}\quad L(C)\,\cong\,H(C)/R_{u}(P_{J})(C)\,\cong\,{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(E_{\rm red}/F)

according to Proposition
10.3
and the Fundamental Theorem of Differential Galois Theory, respectively.
Since by the first part of this proof any Levi group of
\underline{H}(C)
is isomorphic to
{\rm Gal}_{\partial}(E_{\rm red}/F)
, we obtain

L_{\rm red}(C)=\underline{L}_{1}(C)
and
L(C)=\underline{L}_{2}(C)
.
The last assertion is simply the fact that all Levi groups of
\underline{H}(C)
are conjugate by elements of
R_{u}(H)(C)=R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
.
∎

The following proposition shows that every Levi group of
\underline{H}(C)
can be realized as a Levi group of
H
by the choice of a suitable maximal differential ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}\lhd\underline{R}
in the construction of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
introduced at the beginning of Section
10
.

Proposition 10.9
.

(a)

Every
u\in R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
induces a differential
E_{\rm red}
-automorphism

\varphi_{u}\colon\underline{R}\to\underline{R},\ \underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\mapsto x_{3,i}\quad\text{for all}\quad\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}\,,

where
x_{3,i}
are the respective coefficients of the Bruhat decomposition

\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,u\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{x}_{2})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x}_{3})\qquad\text{with}\,\,\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{3}\in\underline{E}^{m}\,\,\text{and}\,\,\boldsymbol{x}_{2}\in(\underline{E}^{\times})^{l}\,.

(b)

For every Levi group
\underline{L}(C)
of
\underline{H}(C)

there exists a maximal differential ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}^{(1)}\lhd\underline{R}
such that
\underline{L}(C)
is a Levi group of the differential Galois group
H^{(1)}(C)
of the respective Picard-Vessiot extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}=\Frac(\underline{R}/I_{\mathrm{max}}^{(1)})
of
F
with fundamental matrix
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}
.

(c)

Let
I_{\mathrm{max}}^{(1)}
and
I_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)}
be two maximal differential ideals in
\underline{R}
and denote by
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}=\Frac(\underline{R}/I_{\mathrm{max}}^{(1)})
and
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{(2)}=\Frac(\underline{R}/I_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)})
the respective Picard-Vessiot extension of
F
with fundamental matrices
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(2)}
.
Then there exists
u\in R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
such that

\varphi\colon\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}\to\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{(2)},\ \mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}\mapsto\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(2)}\,u

is a differential
F
-isomorphism with
\varphi(\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\rm red}^{(1)})=\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\rm red}^{(2)}
. Moreover, the map
\varphi_{u}
from
(a)
maps
I_{\mathrm{max}}^{(1)}
to
I_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)}
.

Proof.

(a)
Since by Theorem
10.8

(a)
we have
R_{u}(\underline{H})(C)=R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
, the element
u
induces a differential
F
-automorphism
\varphi_{u}
of
\underline{E}
.
Since the fixed field of
\underline{E}
under the action of
R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
is
E_{\rm red}
, it is a differential
E_{\rm red}
-automorphism of
\underline{E}
. Moreover, because
\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\in\underline{R}
for all
1\leq i\leq m
, the elements
x_{3,i}
are also in
\underline{R}
.

(b)
In the construction of the Picard-Vessiot extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}=\Frac(\underline{R}/I_{\mathrm{max}})
of
F
with differential Galois group
H(C)
at the beginning
of Section
10
we chose a maximal differential ideal
I_{\mathrm{max}}\lhd\underline{R}
.
Let
L(C)
be a Levi group of
H(C)
.
By Theorem
10.8

(c)
there exists
u\in R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
such that
\underline{L}(C)=uL(C)u^{-1}
.
According to
(a)
,
the map
\varphi_{u}
is a differential
E_{\rm red}
-automorphism of
\underline{R}
and so the image
I_{\mathrm{max}}^{(1)}
of
I_{\mathrm{max}}
under
\varphi_{u}
is a maximal differential ideal of
\underline{R}
. Then,
u
induces the differential
F
-isomorphism

\overline{\mathcal{E}}\to\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)},\ \mathcal{\overline{Y}}\mapsto\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}u\,.

The differential Galois group
H^{(1)}(C)
of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}
then satisfies
H^{(1)}(C)=uH(C)u^{-1}
.
Hence, with
\underline{L}(C)=uL(C)u^{-1}
we conclude that
\underline{L}(C)
is a Levi group of
H^{(1)}(C)
.

(c)
According to Proposition
10.2

(d)
the differential fields
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}
and
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{(2)}
are also Picard-Vessiot extensions of
E_{\rm red}
for
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
.
Thus, there exists
u\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(C)
such that the map

\varphi\colon\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}\to\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{(2)},\ \mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}\mapsto\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(2)}u

is a differential
E_{\rm red}
-isomorphism. Since by construction
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}_{\rm red}=\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(2)}_{\rm red}
, we conclude that
\varphi(\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}_{\rm red})=\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(2)}_{\rm red}
.
Then, from

\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(2)}_{\rm red}\,\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(2)}_{\rm rad}\,u\,=\,\varphi(\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}_{\rm red}\,\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}_{\rm rad})\,=\,\varphi(\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}_{\rm red})\,\varphi(\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}_{\rm rad})\,=\,\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(2)}_{\rm red}\,\varphi(\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}_{\rm rad})

we obtain that
\varphi(\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}_{\rm rad})=\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(2)}_{\rm rad}\,u
and so
u\in R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
.
The last assertion follows from the fact that the kernel of the differential
F
-homomorphism

\widetilde{\varphi}_{u}\colon\underline{R}\to\underline{R}/I_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)},\ \underline{\mathrm{int}}_{i}\mapsto x_{3,i}\quad\text{for all}\ \beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}

is
I_{\mathrm{max}}^{(1)}
, where
x_{3,i}
is as in
(a)
.
Indeed, composing
\widetilde{\varphi}_{u}
with the restriction of
\varphi^{-1}
to
\underline{R}/I_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)}
, we obtain a differential
F
-homomorphism which maps
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
to
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}^{(1)}
.
∎

11.
Computing the Reductive Part of the differential Galois Group

In this section we present an algorithm which computes the ideal
\underline{Q}
of algebraic relations between the entries of
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
and an ideal
I_{\underline{H}}
of the ring
C[\mathrm{GL}_{n}]
defining the group
\underline{H}(C)=\Stab(\underline{Q})(C)
contained in
P_{J}(C)\leq\mathrm{GL}_{n}(C)
by Proposition
10.4

(b)
.
Our algorithm will also compute the ideal
\overline{Q}_{\rm red}
of algebraic relations between the entries of
\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\rm red}
and an ideal
I_{L_{\rm red}}
in
C[\mathrm{GL}_{n}]
defining the differential Galois group
L_{\rm red}(C)=\Stab(\overline{Q}_{\rm red})(C)
, which is a Levi group of
\underline{H}(C)
contained in the standard Levi group of
P_{J}(C)
according to Theorem
10.8

(c)
.

In Section
9
we computed representatives
\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}}=(\widehat{v}_{1},\dots,\widehat{v}_{l})
in

F(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}})\,=\,F(X)\,=\,F(X_{i,j}\mid i,j=1,\dots,n_{I^{\prime\prime}})

of the residue classes in
E_{\rm red}
which are the images of
\boldsymbol{v}
under the specialization
\sigma
.
Since the derivation on
F(X)
is defined by

\partial(X)\,=\,A_{\rm comp}X\qquad(\text{cf.\ Definition~\ref{def:Q}}),

we can also compute representatives in
F(X)
of the derivatives of
\sigma(\boldsymbol{v})
in
E_{\rm red}
. Thus, we can also determine representatives in
F(X)
of the images in
E_{\rm red}
of the
m-l
differential polynomials
\boldsymbol{f}
appearing in the Bruhat decomposition of
\mathcal{Y}
. We denote these elements in
F(X)
by

\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}}\,=\,(\widehat{f}_{l+1},\dots,\widehat{f}_{m}).

We substitute in the Bruhat decomposition of

\mathcal{Y}
the generic solutions
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{f}
,
\boldsymbol{\exp}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}
by

\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}},\,\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}},\,\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}},\,\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathrm{int}}},

where we recall from the beginning
of Section
10
that the entry
\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
in
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathrm{int}}}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}
is equal to
\mathrm{intrad}_{i}
, and obtain the matrix product

(61)

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}},\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathrm{int}}})\,.

Note that the entries of this matrix product lie in the ring

F(X)[\mathrm{intrad}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}],

which for the current purpose is not considered as a differential ring.
We denote by
\mathcal{D}
the multiplicatively closed subset of
F[X,\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\mathrm{intrad}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}]
which is generated by the denominators of

\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}}
,
\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}}
,
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}}
and of
\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
with
\beta_{i}\in\Psi^{-}
and the determinants
\det(X)
and
\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})
.
We consider now the localization

\mathcal{R}\,:=\,\mathcal{D}^{-1}F[X,\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\mathrm{intrad}_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}]

and the ideal
\widetilde{Q}
of
\mathcal{R}

generated by the numerators of the entries of the matrix

\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}-\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}},\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathrm{int}}})\in\mathcal{R}^{n\times n}

and the generators of
Q
.
We can use now Gröbner basis methods to compute the intersection

\underline{Q}\,:=\,\widetilde{Q}\cap F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]\,.

Again we can use Gröbner basis methods to compute the generators of the ideal
I_{\underline{H}}
in
C[\mathrm{GL}_{n}]
which defines the stabilizer
\underline{H}(C)=\Stab(\underline{Q})(C)
in
P_{J}(C)\leq\mathrm{GL}_{n}(C)
of the ideal
\underline{Q}
. Similarly, we consider in the localization

\mathcal{R}_{\rm red}\,:=\,\mathcal{D}^{-1}F[X,\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}]

the ideal
\widetilde{Q}_{\rm red}
generated by the numerators of the entries of the matrix

\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}-\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}},\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}})\,u_{j_{1}}(\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{j_{k}}(\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{k}})\in\mathcal{R}_{\rm red}^{n\times n}

and the generators of
Q
. One uses Gröbner basis methods to compute the intersection

\overline{Q}_{\rm red}\,:=\,\widetilde{Q}_{\rm red}\cap F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]

and generators of the ideal
I_{L_{\rm red}}
defining the stabilizer
L_{\rm red}(C)=\Stab(\overline{Q}_{\rm red})(C)
of the ideal
\overline{Q}_{\rm red}
.
We summarize these steps in Algorithm
3
.

Input:

The matrix
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}},\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathrm{int}}})
and the generators of the ideal
Q\unlhd F[X]
.

Output:

•

A generating set of an ideal
I_{\underline{H}}\unlhd C[\mathrm{GL}_{n}]
defining the stabilizer
\underline{H}(C)=\Stab(\underline{Q})(C)
.
The stabilizer has a Levi decomposition
\underline{L}(C)\ltimes R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
for a Levi group
\underline{L}(C)
and
\underline{L}(C)
is conjugate by an element of
R_{u}(P_{J})(C)
to a Levi group of
H(C)
.

•

A generating set of an ideal
\overline{Q}_{\rm red}\unlhd F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]
which is a maximal differential ideal for
A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}
.

•

A generating set of an ideal
I_{L_{\rm red}}\unlhd C[\mathrm{GL}_{n}]
defining the stabilizer
L_{\rm red}(C)=\Stab(\overline{Q}_{\rm red})(C)

which is a Levi group of
\underline{H}(C)=\Stab(\underline{Q})(C)
contained in the standard Levi group of
P_{J}(C)
.

Let
\widetilde{Q}
be the ideal in
F[X,\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\mathrm{intrad}_{i},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}\mid\beta_{i}\in\Phi^{-}\setminus\Psi^{-}]

generated by the numerators of the entries of the matrix

\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}-\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}},\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathrm{int}}})\in\mathcal{R}^{n\times n}

and the generators of
Q
.

Let
\widetilde{Q}_{\rm red}
be the ideal in
F[X,\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]

generated by the numerators of the entries of the matrix

\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}-\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}},\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}})\,u_{j_{1}}(\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{j_{k}}(\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{k}})\in\mathcal{R}_{\rm red}^{n\times n}

and the generators of
Q
.

Compute with Gröbner basis methods a generating set of

\underline{Q}\,=\,\widetilde{Q}\cap F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\overline{Q}_{\rm red}\,=\,\widetilde{Q}_{\rm red}\cap F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}].

Compute with Gröbner basis methods generating sets of the defining ideals

I_{\underline{H}}\unlhd C[\mathrm{GL}_{n}]\quad\mathrm{and}\quad I_{L_{\rm red}}\unlhd C[\mathrm{GL}_{n}]

of the stabilizers of
\underline{Q}
and
\overline{Q}_{\rm red}
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(C)
.

return
(the generating sets of
I_{\underline{H}}
,
\overline{Q}_{\rm red}
and
I_{L_{\rm red}}
)

Algorithm 3

ComputeReductivePart

Proposition 11.1
.

Algorithm
3
is correct and terminates.

Proof.

Because the Gröbner basis computations terminate, the algorithm also terminates.

We first show that the computed ideal
\underline{Q}
in step 3 is the kernel of the substitution homomorphism

\underline{\varphi}\colon F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]\to F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}],\ \widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}\to\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}\,.

Then the properties of
\underline{H}(C)=\Stab(\underline{Q})(C)
stated in the output of the algorithm follow from Theorem
10.8
.

The ideal
\underline{Q}
is the kernel of the ring homomorphism

\displaystyle F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}/\widetilde{Q},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}+\widetilde{Q}\,\equiv\,(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}},\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathrm{int}}}))_{i,j}+\widetilde{Q}

and so the ring homomorphism

\displaystyle\varphi\colon F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]/\underline{Q}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}/\widetilde{Q},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}+\underline{Q}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}},\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathrm{int}}}))_{i,j}+\widetilde{Q}

is a monomorphism. Its image is the subring generated over
F
by the entries

(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}},\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathrm{int}}}))_{i,j}+\widetilde{Q}\,,

which is isomorphic as a ring to
F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]
.

Finally, we have to show that the computed ideal
\overline{Q}_{\rm red}
in step 3 is the kernel of the substitution homomorphism

\overline{\varphi}_{\rm red}\colon F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]\to F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\rm red},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}],\ \widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}\mapsto(\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\rm red})_{i,j}.

Then, the statements about
L_{\rm red}(C)=\Stab(\overline{Q}_{\rm red})(C)
will follow from Propositions
10.3
and
10.4

(a)
and Theorem
10.8

(c)
.
The ideal
\overline{Q}_{\rm red}
is the intersection of
\widetilde{Q}_{\rm red}
with
F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]
and so it is the kernel of the ring homomorphism

\displaystyle F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\rm red}/\widetilde{Q}_{\rm red},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}+\widetilde{Q}_{\rm red}\,\equiv\,(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}},\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}})\cdot

\displaystyle\quad u_{j_{1}}(\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{j_{k}}(\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{k}}))_{i,j}+\widetilde{Q}_{\rm red}.

Thus, the ring homomorphism

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\varphi\colon F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]/\overline{Q}_{\rm red}&\to&\mathcal{R}_{\rm red}/\widetilde{Q}_{\rm red},\\[5.0pt]
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}+\overline{Q}_{\rm red}&\mapsto&\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,j}+\widetilde{Q}_{\rm red}\,\equiv\,(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}},\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}})\cdot\\[5.0pt]
&&\quad u_{j_{1}}(\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{j_{k}}(\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{k}}))_{i,j}+\widetilde{Q}_{\rm red}\end{array}

is a monomorphism. The image of
\varphi
is the subring generated over
F
by the entries

(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{v}},\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\exp}})\,u_{j_{1}}(\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{j_{k}}(\widehat{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{k}}))_{i,j}+\widetilde{Q}_{\rm red}\,=\,(\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\rm red})_{i,j}

and so
\image(\varphi)
is isomorphic to
F[\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\rm red},\det(\mathcal{\overline{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}]
.
∎

12.
Computing the Unipotent Radical

We start using the results of Section
7
to show that we can compute a matrix
\overline{g}_{1}\in G(F)
such that
\overline{g}_{1}\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\in P_{J}(\underline{R})
and
\overline{g}_{1}\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\in L_{J}(E_{\rm red})
and such that
\overline{g}_{1}
gauge transforms
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
into the Lie algebra of
P_{J}
, that is

(62)

\overline{g}_{1}.A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})\,=:\,A_{P_{J}}\in\Lie(P_{J})(F)\,.

Proposition 12.1
.

In the notation of Section
7

let
\beta_{k_{1}},\dots,\beta_{k_{s}}
be the roots in
\Phi^{-}\setminus(\Phi_{1}^{\prime-}\cup\dots\cup\Phi_{d}^{\prime-})
.
We can compute

\overline{x}_{k_{1}},\dots,\overline{x}_{k_{s}}\in F
such that
A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
is gauge equivalent by

\overline{g}_{1}\,:=\,n(\overline{w})^{-1}\,u_{k_{s}}(\overline{x}_{k_{s}})\cdots u_{k_{1}}(\overline{x}_{k_{1}})\in G(F)

to a matrix
A_{P_{J}}
in
\Lie(P_{J})(F)
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\overline{g}_{1}\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle P_{J}(\underline{R})\,,

\displaystyle\overline{g}_{1}\,\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,n(\overline{w})

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle U^{+}_{\Psi}(E_{\rm red})\,,

\displaystyle\overline{g}_{1}\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle L_{J}(E_{\rm red})

with
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}=\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}
,
\underline{R}
,
\underline{\boldsymbol{v}}
and
\underline{\boldsymbol{f}}
as introduced in the beginning of Section
10
.

Proof.

According to Proposition
7.2
and Remark
7.3
we can compute
differential polynomials
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}\in C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}
such that the matrix

g_{1}\,=\,n(\overline{w})^{-1}\,u_{k_{s}}(x_{k_{s}})\cdots u_{k_{1}}(x_{k_{1}})

satisfies:

(63)

\displaystyle g_{1}.A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))\,=:\,A_{P_{J}}^{\rm gen}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\Lie(P_{J})(C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\})\,,

(64)

\displaystyle g_{1}\,\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle P_{J}(C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}])\,,

(65)

\displaystyle g_{1}\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle U^{+}_{\Psi}(C\{\boldsymbol{v}\})\,.

We are going to specialize the generic results to the corresponding results over
\underline{R}
.

Let

\pi_{\mathrm{inter}}\colon F\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}\to F\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}/S_{\mathrm{inter}}\,\cong\,F

be the surjective homomorphism of differential rings obtained from
\sigma_{\rm inter}
in Section
9
by extending scalars.
Then, we set

\overline{x}_{k_{s}}\,=\,\pi_{\mathrm{inter}}(x_{k_{s}}),\quad\dots,\quad\overline{x}_{k_{1}}\,=\,\pi_{\mathrm{inter}}(x_{k_{1}})\,.

We explain how to compute
\overline{x}_{k_{1}},\dots,\overline{x}_{k_{s}}\in F
from
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}\in C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
.
We use differential elimination to express
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}
as differential polynomials in
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
. Then one simply substitutes in these expressions

\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
by
\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{l})
be differential indeterminates over
C\{\boldsymbol{v}\}
. One uses the differential Thomas decomposition to compute the normal form of
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}
with respect to the differential ideal generated by

a_{1}-s_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}),\quad\dots,\quad a_{l}-s_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})

and an elimination ranking satisfying
\boldsymbol{v}\gg\boldsymbol{a}
. We obtain expressions for
x_{k_{1}},\dots,x_{k_{s}}
in
\boldsymbol{a}
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
.
Substituting
\boldsymbol{a}
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}
by
\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base}
, respectively, we obtain
\overline{x}_{k_{1}},\dots,\overline{x}_{k_{s}}\in F
.

Applying
\pi_{\rm inter}
to (
63
) we obtain

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\pi_{\mathrm{inter}}(A_{P_{J}}^{\rm gen})&\!=&\!\pi_{\mathrm{inter}}\big(n(\overline{w})^{-1}u_{k_{s}}(x_{k_{s}})\cdots u_{k_{1}}(x_{k_{1}}).A_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))\big)\\[1.99997pt]
&\!=&\!n(\overline{w})^{-1}u_{k_{s}}(\pi_{\mathrm{inter}}(x_{k_{s}}))\cdots u_{k_{1}}(\pi_{\mathrm{inter}}(x_{k_{1}})).A_{G}(\pi_{\mathrm{inter}}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})))\\[1.99997pt]
&\!=&\!n(\overline{w})^{-1}u_{k_{s}}(\overline{x}_{k_{s}})\cdots u_{k_{1}}(\overline{x}_{k_{1}}).A_{G}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})\,=:\,A_{P_{J}}\in\Lie(P_{J})(F).\end{array}

Let
\pi
be the surjective homomorphism of differential rings

\pi\colon F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]\to\underline{R}

obtained from
\underline{\sigma}
in (
53
) in Section
10
by restriction to
F\{\boldsymbol{v}\}[\boldsymbol{\exp},\boldsymbol{\exp}^{-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}}]
. Since the restriction of
\underline{\sigma}
to
C\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}
is
\sigma_{\rm inter}
, we conclude that the restriction of
\pi
to
F\{\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm base}\}
coincides with
\pi_{\mathrm{inter}}
and so
\pi(g_{1})=\pi_{\mathrm{inter}}(g_{1})
. Applying
\pi
to (
64
) we obtain

\pi(g_{1}\mathcal{Y})\,=\,\pi(g_{1})\,\pi(\mathcal{Y})\,=\,\pi_{\mathrm{inter}}(g_{1})\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}\,\in\,P_{J}(\underline{R})\,.

We apply
\pi
to (
65
) and obtain

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\pi(g_{1}\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w}))&\!=&\!\pi_{\mathrm{inter}}(g_{1})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\pi(\boldsymbol{v}),\pi(\boldsymbol{f}))\,n(\overline{w})\\[1.99997pt]
&\!=&\!\pi_{\mathrm{inter}}(g_{1})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\in U^{+}_{\Psi}(E_{\rm red}).\end{array}

The last assertion simply follows now from the definition of
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
, i.e. from

\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\underline{\boldsymbol{v}},\underline{\boldsymbol{f}})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\underline{\boldsymbol{\exp}})\,u_{j_{1}}(\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{j_{k}}(\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{k}})\in G(E_{\rm red})\,,

and from the fact that
u_{j_{1}}(\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{1}})\cdots u_{j_{k}}(\underline{\mathrm{int}}_{j_{k}})\in U^{-}_{\Psi}(E_{\rm red})
.
∎

Recall from Definition
9.6
that
E_{\rm red}=\Frac(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q)
is a Picard-Vessiot extension of
F
for the companion matrix
A_{\rm comp}
for

\overline{\LCLM}(\overline{\boldsymbol{s}},\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\rm base},\partial)\,y=0
.
Moreover,
H_{\rm red}(C):=\Stab(Q)(C)\leq\mathrm{GL}_{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}(C)
is the differential Galois group of
E_{\rm red}
over
F
in the representation induced by the fundamental matrix

X+Q

The Fundamental Theorem of Differential Galois Theory (cf.
[
vdPS03
, Proposition 1.34]
) implies that the fixed field
E_{\rm red}^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}}
is a finite Galois extension of
F
with Galois group
H_{\rm red}/H_{\rm red}^{\circ}
.

Definition 12.2
.

We denote the finite Galois extension
E_{\rm red}^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}}
of
F
by
F_{\mathrm{alg}}
.

Proposition 12.3
.

We can compute a primitive element

p\in\left(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q\right)^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}}

for the algebraic extension
F_{\mathrm{alg}}
of
F
.

Proof.

A proof is given in Appendix
E.1
.
∎

Recall from Section
10
the decomposition
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}=\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm rad}
and the maximal differential ideal
\overline{Q}_{\rm red}\unlhd F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]
.
Its stabilizer is the differential Galois group
L_{\rm red}(C)=\Stab(\overline{Q}_{\rm red})(C)
of the Picard-Vessiot extension
E_{\rm red}
over
F
with respect to the fundamental matrix
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
for
A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}
. Recall that
H_{\rm red}(C)\cong L_{\rm red}(C)
and that we have
E_{\rm red}^{L_{\rm red}^{\circ}}=F_{\mathrm{alg}}
.

Proposition 12.4
.

Let
\overline{g}_{1}
be as in Proposition
12.1
and for an
n\times n
matrix
\widehat{X}=(\widehat{X}_{i,j})
of indeterminates let

\overline{Q}_{\rm red}^{\prime}\unlhd F[\widehat{X},\det(\widehat{X})^{-1}]

be the ideal obtained from
\overline{Q}_{\rm red}\unlhd F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]
by applying the transformation
\widehat{X}=\overline{g}_{1}\,\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Then there exists an
F_{\mathrm{alg}}
-rational point
\overline{g}_{2}
of
\overline{Q}_{\rm red}^{\prime}
such that

\overline{g}_{2}\in L_{J}(F_{\mathrm{alg}})\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{g}_{2}\overline{g}_{1}\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}\in L_{\rm red}^{\circ}(E_{\rm red})\,.

In particular, the matrix

\overline{g}_{2}\overline{g}_{1}.A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}\,=\,\ell\delta(\overline{g}_{2}\overline{g}_{1}\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})\,=:\,A_{\rm red}\in\Lie(L_{\rm red})(F_{\mathrm{alg}})

is in reduced form.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
10.3
that
F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}]
is a Picard-Vessiot ring for
A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}
over
F
with fundamental matrix
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
and differential Galois group
L_{\rm red}(C)
.
Since
\overline{g}_{1}\in G(F)
, the ring
F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}]
is also a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\overline{g}_{1}.A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}
over
F
with fundamental matrix

\overline{g}_{1}\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
.
Moreover,
\overline{g}_{1}\in G(F)
implies that the representation of the differential Galois group with respect to
\overline{g}_{1}\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}
is again
L_{\rm red}(C)
.

We extend now the derivation of
F
to
F[\widehat{X},\det(\widehat{X})^{-1}]
by
\partial(\widehat{X})=(\overline{g}_{1}.A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre})\widehat{X}
.
Since the derivation on
F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]
is defined by
A_{\rm red}^{\rm pre}
, we conclude that

\varphi\colon F[\widehat{X},\det(\widehat{X})^{-1}]\to F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}],\ \widehat{X}\mapsto\overline{g}_{1}\,\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}

is a surjective differential
F
-homomorphism. Because

F[\widehat{\mathcal{Y}},\det(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}]/\overline{Q}_{\rm red}\to F[\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red},\det(\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red})^{-1}],\ \widehat{\mathcal{Y}}+\overline{Q}_{\rm red}\mapsto\underline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rm red}

is a differential
F
