\exp(\smallint v_{2})
for
i=2
.

Moreover, the logarithmic derivatives of the exponential solutions of the associated equations generate the same
\mathbb{Z}
-module as
g_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}),\dots,g_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})
.

Proof.

It is well known (cf.
[
vdPS03
, Ex. 1.14, 5. (b)]
) that for a linear differential equation

y^{(n)}\,=\,c_{n-1}y^{(n-1)}+\dots+c_{0}y

with coefficients in some differential field
\widetilde{F}
and for a basis
\widetilde{y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{n}
of its solution space in a Picard-Vessiot extension
\widetilde{E}
of
\widetilde{F}
, the determinant of the Wronskian matrix
\mathrm{wr}(\widetilde{y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{n})
satisfies the first order linear differential equation

y^{\prime}\,=\,c_{n-1}y\,.

Having said that, we consider for each of the above groups
G
the intermediate products

\mathcal{L}^{G}_{i}(\partial)\,:=\,(\partial-a_{1+i})\cdots(\partial-a_{1})\qquad\text{for}\ i=0,\dots,l-1

of the factorization of its normal form operator
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)
from Proposition
B.2
and Proposition
B.3
.
The solution space
\mathcal{V}_{i}^{G}
of the equation
\mathcal{L}^{G}_{i}(\partial)\,y=0
is a subspace of the solution space
V
of
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0

and we denote a basis of
\mathcal{V}_{i}^{G}
by
\widetilde{y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{i+1}
.
Moreover, the coefficient of the second highest derivative in
\mathcal{L}^{G}_{i}(\partial)
is the sum

-a_{1+i}-\dots-a_{1}.

Applying now the result mentioned at the beginning of this proof, we obtain

\det(\mathrm{wr}(\widetilde{y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{i+1}))^{\prime}\,=\,(a_{1+i}+\dots+a_{1})\,\det(\mathrm{wr}(\widetilde{y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{i+1})).

Hence,
0\neq\det(\mathrm{wr}(\widetilde{y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{i+1}))
is
an element of the solution space
V^{\det(i)}
of the
i
-th associated operator
L^{\det(i)}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)
, whose logarithmic derivative is

\det(\mathrm{wr}(\widetilde{y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{i+1}))^{\prime}\det(\mathrm{wr}(\widetilde{y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{i+1}))^{-1}\,=\,a_{1+i}+\dots+a_{1}\,.

In the following we compute the explicit values of
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
for each of the groups
\mathrm{SL}_{l+1}
,
\mathrm{Sp}_{2l}
,
\mathrm{SO}_{2l+1}
and
\mathrm{G}_{2}
separately.
The assertion then follows by applying the above result to these explicit values.
We recall from
[
Seib
, Section 5]
(note that there
g_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})
is
\overline{g}_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})
) that the diagonal entries of
n(\overline{w}).A_{\rm Liou}(\boldsymbol{v})
are equal to the negatives of the diagonal entries of
\Ad(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f}))(A_{0}^{+})
(cf. Section
3
).
Thus, Remark
4.1
implies that it is sufficient to compute the diagonal entries of

\Ad(u_{1}(v_{1})\cdots u_{l}(v_{l}))(A_{0}^{+}).

In the following we write
E
for the identity matrix
E_{1,1}+\dots+E_{n,n}.

(a)
For the construction of the normal form matrix for
\mathrm{SL}_{l+1}
we used the representation of
\Lie(\mathrm{SL}_{l+1})
introduced in
[
Seib
, Section 7]
. Using this representation we find

A_{0}^{+}\,=\,\sum_{i=1}^{l}E_{i,i+1}\quad\text{and}\quad u_{-\alpha_{i}}(v_{i})\,=\,E+E_{i+1,i}v_{i}\quad\text{for}\ 1\leq i\leq l.

Carrying out the respective matrix multiplications we obtain

A_{0}^{+}+\sum_{i=1}^{l}-v_{i}H_{i}+r\,=\,\sum_{i=1}^{l}E_{i,i+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{l}-v_{i}(E_{i,i}-E_{i+1,i+1})+r

with
r
a lower triangular nilpotent matrix in
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(C)
.
Hence, the diagonal entries of
n(\overline{w}).A_{\rm Liou}(\boldsymbol{v})
are

a_{1}\,=\,v_{1}\,,\quad a_{2}\,=\,-v_{1}+v_{2}\,,\quad\dots,\quad a_{l}\,=\,-v_{l-1}+v_{l}\,,\quad a_{l+1}\,=\,-v_{l}\,.

We conclude that for each
1\leq i\leq l
the associated equation
L^{\det(i)}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
has the exponential solution
\exp(\int v_{i})
.

(b)
The construction of the normal form matrix in case of the group
\mathrm{Sp}_{2l}
uses the representation of the Lie algebra presented in
[
Seib
, Section 8]
. Note that there we renumbered the rows and columns of matrices in
C^{2l\times 2l}
using the range
(1,2,\dots,l,-l,\dots,-2,-1)
.
In this representation we have

\displaystyle A_{0}^{+}=(\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}E_{i,i+1}-E_{-i-1,-i})+E_{l,-l}\ ,\quad u_{-\alpha_{i}}(v_{i})=E+v_{i}(-E_{i+1,i}+E_{-i,-i-1})

\displaystyle\text{for}\ 1\leq i\leq l-1\quad\text{and}\quad u_{-\alpha_{l}}(v_{l})=E+v_{l}E_{-l,l}\,.

Carrying out the matrix multiplications we find

A_{0}^{+}+\sum_{i=1}^{l}(\tilde{g}_{i}H_{i})+r=A_{0}^{+}+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\tilde{g}_{i}(E_{i,i}-E_{-i,-i})+r,

where
\tilde{g}_{1}=v_{1}
and
\tilde{g}_{i}=v_{i}-v_{i-1}
for
2\leq i\leq l
and
r\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(C)
a lower triangular nilpotent matrix. We conclude that the first
l
diagonal entries of
n(\overline{w}).A_{\rm Liou}(\boldsymbol{v})
are

a_{1}=-v_{1},\ a_{2}=-v_{2}+v_{1},\dots,a_{l}=-v_{l}+v_{l-1}

and so for
1\leq i\leq l
the
i
-th associated equation
L^{\det(i)}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)y=0
has the exponential solution
\exp(\int-v_{i})
.

(c)
For the construction of the normal form matrix for
\mathrm{SO}_{2l+1}
we used the representation of
\Lie(\mathrm{SO}_{2l+1})
introduced in
[
Seib
, Section 9]
. There, we renumbered the rows and columns of matrices in

\mathfrak{gl}_{2l+1}(C)
using the range
(1,\dots,l,0,-l,\dots,-1)
.
In this representation we find

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}A_{0}^{+}&=&\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}(E_{i,i+1}-E_{-i-1,-i})+2E_{l,0}+E_{0,-l}\ ,\\[5.0pt]
u_{-\alpha_{i}}(v_{i})&=&E+v_{i}(-E_{i+1,i}+E_{-i,-i-1})\qquad\text{for}\ 1\leq i\leq l-1\quad\text{and}\\[5.0pt]
u_{-\alpha_{l}}(v_{l})&=&E+v_{l}(-E_{0,l}-2E_{-l,0})+v_{l}^{2}E_{-l,l}\,.\end{array}

Multiplying out the conjugation of
A_{0}^{+}
by
u_{1}(v_{1})\cdots u_{l}(v_{l})
we obtain

A_{0}^{+}+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\tilde{g}_{i}H_{i}+r\,=\,A_{0}^{+}+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\tilde{g}_{i}(E_{i,i}-E_{-i,-i})+r\,,

where
\tilde{g}_{1}=v_{1}
and
\tilde{g}_{i}=v_{i}-v_{i-1}
for
2\leq i\leq l-1
and
\tilde{g}_{l}=2v_{l}-v_{l-1}
. As before,
r\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(C)
is a suitable lower triangular nilpotent matrix. Thus, the first
l
diagonal entries of
n(\overline{w}).A_{\rm Liou}(\boldsymbol{v})
are

a_{1}\,=\,-v_{1},\,\,a_{2}\,=\,-v_{2}+v_{1},\,\,\dots,\,\,a_{l-1}\,=\,-v_{l-1}+v_{l-2},\,\,a_{l}\,=\,-2v_{l}+v_{l-1}\,.

Hence, for
1\leq i\leq l-1
the
i
-th associated equation has the exponential solution
\exp(\int-v_{i})
and the
l
-th associated equation has the exponential solution
\exp(-2v_{l})
.

(d)
According to Proposition
B.3
the last
l=2
factors in the factorization of the normal form operator
L_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)
are
\partial+v_{1}-v_{2}
and
\partial-v_{1}
.
Hence, the first associated equation has the exponential solution
\exp(\int v_{1})
and the second one has the exponential solution
\exp(\int v_{2})
.

It is left to show the assertion of the supplement. Since the diagonal entries of
A_{\rm Liou}(\boldsymbol{v})
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combinations of
g_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}),\dots,g_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})
and conjugation with
n(\overline{w})
permutes those entries and potentially changes their signs, we conclude that the diagonal entries of
n(\overline{w}).A_{\rm Liou}(\boldsymbol{v})
are also
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combinations of
g_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}),\dots,g_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})
. Because the logarithmic derivatives of the exponential solutions of the associated operators are the partial sums of these diagonal entries, it follows that they are contained in the
\mathbb{Z}
-span of
g_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}),\dots,g_{l}(\boldsymbol{v})
. The reversed inclusion simply follows from the fact that
g_{i}(\boldsymbol{v})
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combinations of the
v_{i}
(in case
G=\mathrm{SO}_{2l+1}
only one
g_{i}
involves
v_{l}
and it has the form
\pm(2v_{l}-v_{l-1})
) and that the logarithmic derivatives of the exponential solutions of the associated operators have the form
\pm v_{i}
(in case
G=\mathrm{SO}_{2l+1}
one logarithmic derivative is
-2v_{l}
).
∎

Recall that in Definition
3.4
we denoted by
\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),y)=0
the Riccati equation for the

i
-th associated equation
L^{\det(i)}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
for the normal form equation
L_{G}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
.
More generally, for a definition and construction of a Riccati equation associated to a scalar differential equation, cf. e.g.
[
vdPS03
, Definition 4.6]
.
From Proposition
C.2
we immediately obtain the following corollary.

Corollary C.3
.

(a)

Case
\mathrm{SL}_{l+1}
: For
i=1,\dots,l
the Riccati equation
\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),y)=0
for the
i
-th associated equation
L^{\det(i)}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
has
v_{i}
as a solution.

(b)

Case
\mathrm{Sp}_{2l}
: For
i=1,\dots,l
the Riccati equation
\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),y)=0
for the
i
-th associated equation
L^{\det(i)}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
has
-v_{i}
as a solution.

(c)

Case
\mathrm{SO}_{2l+1}
: For
i=1,\dots,l-1
the Riccati equation
\mathrm{Ric}_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),y)=0
for the
i
-th associated equation
L^{\det(i)}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
has
-v_{i}
as a solution
and the Riccati equation
\mathrm{Ric}_{l}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),y)=0
for the
l
-th associated equation
L^{\det(l)}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
has

-2v_{l}
as a solution.

(d)

Case
\mathrm{G}_{2}
: The Riccati equations
\mathrm{Ric}_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),y)=0
and
\mathrm{Ric}_{2}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),y)=0
for the first and second associated equations, i.e. for

L^{\det(1)}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y\,=\,L_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y\,=\,0

and for
L^{\det(2)}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}),\partial)\,y=0
, have
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
as solutions respectively.

Appendix D
The Denominators in the Bruhat Decomposition

In this section we are going to analyze in more detail the coefficients in
C(G)
of the Bruhat decomposition

\overline{Y}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w})

where the entries
\overline{Y}_{i,j}
are the residue classes of
Y_{i,j}
in
C[G]=C[Y_{i,j},\det(Y)^{-1}]/I_{G}
.

Lemma D.1
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
Y=(Y_{i,j})
be an
n\times n
matrix whose entries are indeterminates
Y_{i,j}
over
C
.
For further indeterminates
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}
and
y_{i,j}
over
C
with
2\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j<i
consider the matrices

W\,:=\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&\ldots&0&c_{1}\\
\vdots&\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise 1.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{.}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 7.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 1.0mu}&c_{2}&0\\
0&\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise 1.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{.}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 7.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 1.0mu}&\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise 1.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{.}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 7.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 1.0mu}&\vdots\\
c_{n}&0&\ldots&0\end{array}\right),\qquad L\,:=\,\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}1&0&\ldots&\ldots&0\\
y_{2,1}&1&0&\ldots&0\\
y_{3,1}&y_{3,2}&1&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
y_{n,1}&\ldots&y_{n,n-2}&y_{n,n-1}&1\end{array}\right).

Moreover, for
1\leq k\leq n-1
define the matrices and vectors

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle Y^{(k)}&=&(Y_{i,j})_{{1\leq i\leq n-k}\atop{k+1\leq j\leq n}}\ \text{and}\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle v_{k}&=&(-Y_{n-k+1,k+1},-Y_{n-k+1,k+2}.\dots,-Y_{n-k+1,n}).\end{array}

(a)

The linear system of equations
(WLY)_{i,j}=0
with
1\leq i\leq n-1
and
i<j\leq n
in the indeterminates
y_{i,j}
over
C(c_{1},\dots,c_{n},Y_{i,j})
is equivalent to the conjunction of the
(n-1)
linear systems

(78)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle c_{k}\sum_{s=1}^{n-k}y_{n+1-k,s}Y_{s,k+1}&=&-c_{k}Y_{n-k+1,k+1}\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle c_{k}\sum_{s=1}^{n-k}y_{n+1-k,s}Y_{s,k+2}&=&-c_{k}Y_{n-k+1,k+2}\\[5.0pt]
\qquad\qquad\vdots&&\qquad\vdots\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle c_{k}\sum_{s=1}^{n-k}y_{n+1-k,s}Y_{s,n}&=&-c_{k}Y_{n-k+1,n}\end{array}\right\}\quad\text{with}\quad 1\leq k\leq n-1.

(b)

For
1\leq k\leq n-1
the systems of equations in
(a)
have the unique solutions

y_{n+1-k,s}\,=\,\frac{\det(Y_{s}^{(k)})}{\det(Y^{(k)})}\qquad\text{with}\ 1\leq s\leq n-k\,,

where
Y_{s}^{(k)}
is the matrix obtained by replacing the
s
-th row by
v_{k}
.

(c)

Substituting the solutions of
(b)
into
WLY
the diagonal entries
(WLY)_{i,i}
become

\displaystyle(-1)^{n-1}c_{1}\frac{\det(Y)}{\det(Y^{(1)})},\ (-1)^{n-2}c_{2}\frac{\det(Y^{(1)})}{\det(Y^{(2)})},\ \dots,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-c_{n-1}\frac{\det(Y^{(n-2)})}{\det(Y^{(n-1)})},\ c_{n}\det(Y^{(n-1)}).

Proof.

(a)
The matrix
WL
is the matrix whose rows are

c_{1}L_{n,*},\quad c_{2}L_{n-1,*},\quad\dots,\ c_{n-1}L_{2,*},\quad c_{n}L_{1,*}\,,

where
L_{i,*}
denotes the
i
-th row of
L
. Now for
1\leq k\leq n-1
the
k
-th system in (
78
) is obtained by multiplying the
k
-th row of
WL
with the columns
Y_{*,k+1},\dots,Y_{*,n}
of
Y
and then equating these products to zero.
The right hand sides of the so obtained system are those entries of the
k
-th row of
WLY
which lie above the diagonal.

(b)
Observe that the systems in (
78
) are equivalent to

L^{\prime}_{k}Y^{(k)}=v_{k}\qquad\text{for}\ 1\leq k\leq n-1\,,

where
L^{\prime}_{k}
denotes the vector of indeterminates

L^{\prime}_{k}=(y_{n+1-k,1},\dots,y_{n+1-k,n-k}).

The assertion then follows from applying Cramer’s rule.

(c)
Applying the argumentation of
(a)
to the diagonal entries
(WLY)_{i,i}
of
WLY
, we observe that they are

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle c_{1}(\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}y_{n,s}Y_{s,1}+Y_{n,1}),\ \dots,\ c_{k}(\sum_{s=1}^{n-k}y_{n+1-k,s}Y_{s,k}+Y_{n+1-k,k})\ \dots,\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle c_{n-1}(y_{2,1}Y_{1,n-1}+Y_{2,n-1})\quad\text{and}\quad c_{n}Y_{1,n}=c_{n}\det(Y^{(n-1)}).\end{array}

Let
1\leq k\leq n-1
. Substituting the solutions of
(b)
into the
k
-th diagonal element

c_{k}(\sum_{s=1}^{n-k}y_{n+1-k,s}Y_{s,k}+Y_{n+1-k,k}),

we obtain

c_{k}(\sum_{s=1}^{n-k}\frac{\det(Y_{s}^{(k)})}{\det(Y^{(k)})}Y_{s,k}+Y_{n+1-k,k})=c_{k}(\sum_{s=1}^{n-k}\frac{\det(Y_{s}^{(k)})}{\det(Y^{(k)})}Y_{s,k}+\frac{\det(Y^{(k)})}{\det(Y^{(k)})}Y_{n+1-k,k})\,.

Observe that

Y^{(k-1)}\,=\,(Y_{i,j})_{{1\leq i\leq n-k+1}\atop{k\leq j\leq n}}\,=\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c|ccccc}Y_{1,k}&&&&&\\
Y_{2,k}&&&&&\\
\vdots&&&Y^{(k)}&&\\
&&&&&\\
Y_{n-k,k}&&&&&\\
\hline\cr Y_{n-k+1,k}&&&-v_{k}&&\end{array}\right).

Developing the determinant of
Y^{(k-1)}
for the first column, we obtain

(79)

\det(Y^{(k-1)})=\sum_{s=1}^{n-k}(-1)^{s-1}Y_{s,k}\det(\widehat{Y}^{(k-1)}_{s})+(-1)^{n-k}Y_{n-k+1,k}\det(Y^{(k)}),

where
\widehat{Y}^{(k-1)}_{s}
is the matrix obtained by canceling the
s
-th row of
\begin{pmatrix}Y^{(k)}\\
-v_{k}\end{pmatrix}
.
Swapping over the last row of
\widehat{Y}^{(k-1)}_{s}
until it becomes the
s
-th row, the determinant of the so obtained matrix is

(-1)^{n-k-s}\det(\widehat{Y}^{(k-1)}_{s})=-\det(Y^{(k)}_{s})\,,

which is equivalent to

\det(\widehat{Y}^{(k-1)}_{s})=(-1)^{-n+k+s+1}\det(Y^{(k)}_{s}).

Substituting now in equation (
79
) the expression for
\det(\widehat{Y}^{(k-1)}_{s})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\det(Y^{(k-1)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{n-k}(-1)^{-n+k+2s}Y_{s,k}\det(Y^{(k)}_{s})+(-1)^{n-k}Y_{n-k+1,k}\det(Y^{(k)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{n-k}(-1)^{-n+k}Y_{s,k}\det(Y^{(k)}_{s})+(-1)^{n-k}Y_{n-k+1,k}\det(Y^{(k)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{n-k}(\sum_{s=1}^{n-k}Y_{s,k}\det(Y^{(k)}_{s})+Y_{n-k+1,k}\det(Y^{(k)}))

and so the
k
-th diagonal entry becomes

c_{k}(\sum_{s=1}^{n-k}\frac{\det(Y_{s}^{(k)})}{\det(Y^{(k)})}Y_{s,k}+\frac{\det(Y^{(k)})}{\det(Y^{(k)})}Y_{n+1-k,k})=(-1)^{n-k}c_{k}\frac{\det(Y^{(k-1)})}{\det(Y^{(k)})}.

∎

Proposition D.2
.

Let
G
be one of the groups
\mathrm{SL}_{l+1}
,
\mathrm{Sp}_{2l}
or
\mathrm{SO}_{2l+1}
and let

C[G]\,=\,C[\overline{Y}_{i,j}\mid i,j=1,\dots,n]\,=\,C[Y_{i,j}\mid i,j=1,\dots,n]/I_{G}

the coordinate ring of
G
, where
I_{G}
is the defining ideal of
G
and
n
denotes the dimension of the representation of
G
, that is
n=l+1
,
n=2l
and
n=2l+1
respectively. Then there exist
e_{1},\dots,e_{l}\in C[G]
and

\boldsymbol{z}=(\prod_{j=1}^{l}e_{j}^{a_{1,j}},\dots,\prod_{j=1}^{l}e_{j}^{a_{l,j}})\quad\text{with}\ a_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}

and
\boldsymbol{x}:=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
and
\boldsymbol{w}:=(w_{1},\dots,w_{m})
in the localization
\mathcal{M}^{-1}C[G]
of
C[G]
by the multiplicatively closed subset
\mathcal{M}
generated by
e_{1},\dots,e_{l}
such that

\overline{Y}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w})

is the Bruhat decomposition of
\overline{Y}:=(\overline{Y}_{i,j})
.

Proof.

Since we used the representations of
\mathrm{SL}_{l+1}
,
\mathrm{Sp}_{2l}
and
\mathrm{SO}_{2l+1}
presented in Sections 7, 8 and 9 of
[
Seib
]
, the maximal unipotent subgroups
U^{-}
of these groups consist of lower triangular matrices and their maximal tori
T
of diagonal matrices.
In particular,
B^{-}
consists of lower triangular matrices, too. The inverses of the representatives
n(\overline{w})
of the longest Weyl group elements for these groups are matrices of the form

\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&\ldots&0&c_{1}\\
\vdots&\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise 1.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{.}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 7.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 1.0mu}&c_{2}&0\\
0&\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise 1.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{.}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 7.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 1.0mu}&\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise 1.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{.}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 7.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 1.0mu}&\vdots\\
c_{n}&0&\ldots&0\end{array}\right)\quad\text{with}\ c_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}.

On the one hand, according to
[
Seia
, Lemma 4.2]
, there exist
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
,
\boldsymbol{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{l})
and
\boldsymbol{w}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{m})
in the field of fractions
C(G)
of the coordinate ring
C[G]
such that

\overline{Y}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w})\,.

On the other hand, Lemma
D.1
applied with
W=n(\overline{w})^{-1}
to
Y:=(Y_{i,j})
yields a unipotent lower triangular matrix
L^{-1}
and a lower triangular matrix
B
such that

Y\,=\,L^{-1}\,n(\overline{w})\,B

with the following properties. The diagonal entries of
B
are as in
Lemma
D.1

(c)
and the remaining entries of
B
and the entries of
L
are in the localization of
C[Y_{i,j}]
by the multiplicatively closed subset generated by

(80)

\det(Y^{(n-1)}),\ \det(Y^{(n-2)}),\ \dots,\ \det(Y^{(1)}),\ \det(Y)\in C[Y_{i,j}]\,.

Since the evaluation of the elements in (
80
)
at the point
n(\overline{w})
does not vanish, we conclude that these elements are not in the defining ideal
I_{G}
of
G
. Thus, the entries of
L^{-1}
and
B
map to the localization of
C[G]
by the multiplicatively closed subset generated by the residue classes

\displaystyle d_{n}\,:=\,\det(Y^{(n-1)})+I_{G},\quad d_{n-1}\,:=\,\det(Y^{(n-2)})+I_{G},\quad\ldots,

\displaystyle d_{2}\,:=\,\det(Y^{(1)})+I_{G},\quad d_{1}\,:=\,\det(Y)+I_{G}\,.

We denote the respective image matrices by
\overline{L}^{-1}
and
\overline{B}
. We obtain

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w})\,=\,\overline{Y}\,=\,\overline{L}^{-1}\,n(\overline{w})\,\overline{B}\,,

which is equivalent to

\overline{L}\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,=\,n(\overline{w})\,\overline{B}\,(\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w}))^{-1}\,n(\overline{w})^{-1}\,.

The left hand side of the last equality is a unipotent lower triangular matrix and since conjugation by
n(w)
maps a lower triangular matrix to an upper triangular one, the right hand side is an upper triangular matrix.
This implies that
\overline{L}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})
and that
\overline{B}=\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w})
.

According to
[
vdPS03
, Theorem 1.28]
(note that the torsor here is trivial) there exists a differential
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
-isomorphism of Picard-Vessiot rings

\psi\colon C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle\otimes_{C}C[G]\to C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathcal{Y}],\quad 1\otimes\overline{Y}_{i,j}\mapsto\mathcal{Y}_{i,j},

which extends to a differential
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
-isomorphism of Picard-Vessiot fields.
The exponential solutions
\exp^{\rm ass}_{1},\dots,\exp^{\rm ass}_{l}
of the associated equations (cf. Proposition
C.2
) trivially satisfy linear differential equations over
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
and so,
according to
[
vdPS03
, Corollary 1.38]
, they lie in the Picard-Vessiot ring
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathcal{Y}]
.
By Proposition
C.2

there exist
b_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

\exp^{\rm ass}_{i}\,=\,\prod_{j=1}^{l}\exp_{j}^{b_{i,j}}\,.

Moreover, since
\psi^{-1}
sends
\exp_{j}
to
z_{j}\in C(G)
, we conclude that
\psi^{-1}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{i})=:e_{i}
lies in
C(G)
.
Because
\exp^{\rm ass}_{i}
lies in the Picard-Vessiot ring
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathcal{Y}]
,
we finally obtain that
e_{i}\in C[G]
.
Since by Proposition
C.2
there exist
a_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

\exp_{i}\,=\,\prod_{j=1}^{l}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{j})^{a_{i,j}}\,,

we obtain by applying
\psi^{-1}
that

z_{i}\,=\,\prod_{j=1}^{l}e_{j}^{a_{i,j}}\,.

Denote by
\check{z}_{1},\dots,\check{z}_{n}
the diagonal entries of
t(\boldsymbol{z})
and note that these are products of
e_{1},\dots,e_{l}
with exponents in
\mathbb{Z}
and a non-zero constant.
Comparing the diagonal entries of the left and right hand side of

\overline{B}=\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w})
we obtain

\displaystyle{\rm diag}(\check{z}_{1},\check{z}_{2},\dots,\check{z}_{n-1},\check{z}_{n})={\rm diag}(\frac{d_{1}}{d_{2}},\frac{d_{2}}{d_{3}},\dots,\frac{d_{n-1}}{d_{n}},d_{n})\,,

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle d_{n}\,=\,\check{z}_{n},\frac{d_{n-1}}{d_{n}}=\check{z}_{n-1},\dots,\frac{d_{2}}{d_{3}}=\check{z}_{2},\frac{d_{1}}{d_{2}}=\check{z}_{1}.

We conclude that
d_{i}
is the product of
e_{1},\dots,e_{l}
with exponents in
\mathbb{Z}
and a non-zero constant. Combining this with Lemma
D.1
it follows that the entries of
\overline{L}=\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})
and
\overline{B}=\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w})
are in the localization of
C[G]
by the multiplicatively closed subset
\mathcal{M}
generated by
e_{1},\dots,e_{l}
.
Since the entries of
\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})^{-1}
are also contained in
\mathcal{M}^{-1}C[G]
, the same is true for
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w})
. Since producing the standard factorization of
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})
and
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w})
into root group elements only involves operations in
\mathcal{M}^{-1}C[G]
, we conclude that
\boldsymbol{x}
and
\boldsymbol{w}
are in
\mathcal{M}^{-1}C[G]

∎

Proposition D.3
.

Let
G=\mathrm{G}_{2}\subset\mathrm{SO}_{7}
. In the notation of
Proposition
D.2
there exist
e_{1},e_{2}\in C[G]
and

\boldsymbol{z}=(e_{1},e_{2}^{-1})

and
\boldsymbol{x}:=(x_{1},\dots,x_{6})
and
\boldsymbol{w}:=(w_{1},\dots,w_{6})
in the localization
\mathcal{M}^{-1}C[G]
of
C[G]
by the multiplicatively closed subset
\mathcal{M}
generated by
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
such that

\overline{Y}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w})

is the Bruhat decomposition of
\overline{Y}:=(\overline{Y}_{i,j})
.

Proof.

In the proof of Proposition
B.3
we explained how one can compute the explicit Bruhat decomposition of the fundamental matrix

\mathcal{Y}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{f})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\exp})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{int}})

for
A_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
. Carrying out this construction one finds that

\boldsymbol{\exp}=(\exp_{1},\exp_{2})=(e^{\int v_{1}},e^{\int-v_{2}}).

Now, by
[
vdPS03
, Theorem 1.28]
the map

\psi\colon C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle\otimes C[\mathrm{G}_{2}]\to C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathcal{Y}],\ \overline{Y}_{i,j}\mapsto\mathcal{Y}_{i,j}

is a differential
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
-isomorphism of Picard-Vessiot rings which extends to a differential
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle
-isomorphism

\psi\colon C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle\otimes C(\mathrm{G}_{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{E},\ \overline{Y}_{i,j}\mapsto\mathcal{Y}_{i,j}

of Picard-Vessiot fields. According to
[
Seia
, Lemma 4.2]
, there exist

(81)

\boldsymbol{x}=(\frac{x_{1,1}}{x_{1,2}},\dots,\frac{x_{6,1}}{x_{6,2}}),\quad\boldsymbol{z}=(\frac{z_{1,1}}{z_{1,2}},\frac{z_{2,1}}{z_{2,2}})\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{w}=(\frac{w_{1,1}}{w_{1,2}},\dots,\frac{w_{6,1}}{w_{6,2}})

in the field of fractions
C(G)
of the coordinate ring
C[G]
such that

\overline{Y}\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\,n(\overline{w})\,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{w})\,,

where we assume that these fractions are completely reduced.
First, we show that
\boldsymbol{z}=(e_{1},e_{2}^{-1})
with
e_{1}
,
e_{2}\in C[\mathrm{G}_{2}]
. We apply
\psi
to
\boldsymbol{z}
and obtain with
Proposition
C.2

(d)
that

\psi(\frac{z_{1,1}}{z_{1,2}})\,=\,\frac{\psi(z_{1,1})}{\psi(z_{1,2})}\,=\,\exp_{1}\,=\,\frac{\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}}{1}\ \text{and}\ \psi(\frac{z_{2,1}}{z_{2,2}})\,=\,\frac{\psi(z_{2,1})}{\psi(z_{2,2})}\,=\,\exp_{2}\,=\,\frac{1}{\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}}.

Observe that since
z_{i,1}/z_{i,2}
with
i=1,2
is completely reduced, so is
\psi(z_{i,1})/\psi(z_{i,2})
. Moreover, since
\psi(z_{i,1})
,
\psi(z_{i,2})
and
\exp^{\rm ass}_{i}
are elements of the Picard-Vessiot ring
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathcal{Y}]
(the
\exp^{\rm ass}_{i}
because of
[
vdPS03
, Corollary 1.38]
), we conclude that
z_{1,2}=z_{2,1}=1
without loss of generality.
Next we are going to show that the fractions
\boldsymbol{x}
and
\boldsymbol{w}
lie in the localization of
C[\mathrm{G}_{2}]
by the multiplicatively closed subset generated by
z_{1,1}
and
z_{2,2}
. A computation shows that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}v_{1}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}&=&-\mathcal{Y}_{7,5}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
v_{2}\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}&=&\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\,\mathcal{Y}_{6,4}+\mathcal{Y}_{2,4}\,\mathcal{Y}_{6,5}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
f_{3}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}&=&\mathcal{Y}_{6,5}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
f_{4}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{2}&=&-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\,\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\,\mathcal{Y}_{1,5}+\mathcal{Y}_{6,5}\,\mathcal{Y}_{7,5}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
f_{5}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{3}&=&-(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{2}\,\mathcal{Y}_{3,5}\,+\\[1.99997pt]
&&\mathcal{Y}_{7,5}\,(2\,f_{4}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{2}+v_{1}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\,f_{3}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})\,,\\[1.99997pt]
f_{6}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{4}\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}&=&-(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{3}\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}\,\mathcal{Y}_{4,5}-\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\,v_{2}\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}\,f_{5}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{3}\,-\\[1.99997pt]
&&2\,\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}\,f_{3}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\,f_{4}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{2}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\mathrm{int}_{1}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}&=&\mathcal{Y}_{2,4}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\mathrm{int}_{2}\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}&=&-\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\,\mathcal{Y}_{7,3}+v_{1}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\,\mathcal{Y}_{2,3}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\mathrm{int}_{3}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}&=&-\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}\,\mathcal{Y}_{2,3}-\mathcal{Y}_{2,4}\,(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\,\mathcal{Y}_{7,3}-v_{1}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\,\mathcal{Y}_{2,3})\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\mathrm{int}_{4}\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}&=&-\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\,\mathcal{Y}_{7,6}-\mathcal{Y}_{2,6}\,\mathcal{Y}_{7,5}\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\mathrm{int}_{5}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}&=&\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}\,\mathcal{Y}_{2,6}+\mathcal{Y}_{2,4}\,(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\,\mathcal{Y}_{7,6}+\mathcal{Y}_{2,6}\,\mathcal{Y}_{7,5})\,,\\[1.99997pt]
\mathrm{int}_{6}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{3}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{2})^{2}&=&(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{2}\,(\exp^{\rm ass}_{2})^{2}\,\mathcal{Y}_{2,7}\,+\\[1.99997pt]
&&(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{2}\,(\mathrm{int}_{1}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\,\mathrm{int}_{2}\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}\,-\\[1.99997pt]
&&\mathrm{int}_{3}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\exp^{\rm ass}_{2})\,\mathrm{int}_{4}\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}\,+\\[1.99997pt]
&&\mathrm{int}_{1}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\,(\mathrm{int}_{3}\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}\exp^{\rm ass}_{2})^{2}\,.\end{array}

Since
\mathcal{Y}_{i,j}
and
\exp^{\rm ass}_{1}
and
\exp^{\rm ass}_{2}
are in the Picard-Vessiot ring
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathcal{Y}]
, also the left hand sides of these equations are contained in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathcal{Y}]
. Let
a
be one of the elements
v_{1},v_{2}
,
f_{3},f_{4},f_{5},f_{6}
and
\mathrm{int}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{int}_{6}
and let
\frac{b_{1}}{b_{2}}
be its counterpart among the elements
in (
81
), that is the preimage of
a
under
\psi
.
Let
(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{c_{1}}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{2})^{c_{2}}
with
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be the respective factor from above such that
a(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{c_{1}}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{2})^{c_{2}}
is contained in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathcal{Y}]
. Applying
\psi^{-1}
we obtain

\psi^{-1}(a(\exp^{\rm ass}_{1})^{c_{1}}(\exp^{\rm ass}_{2})^{c_{2}})\,=\,\frac{b_{1}}{b_{2}}z_{1,1}^{c_{1}}z_{2,2}^{c_{2}}\in C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathrm{G}_{2}]\,.

Since
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
have no common devisor and the right hand side lies in the Picard-Vessiot ring, we conclude that
b_{2}
divides
z_{1,1}^{c_{1}}z_{2,2}^{c_{2}}
. Since
\exp^{\rm ass}_{i}
are irreducible in
C\langle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle[\mathcal{Y}]
, it follows that
z_{1,1}
and
z_{2,2}
are irreducible in
C[\mathrm{G}_{2}]
and so
b
lies in the localization of
C[\mathrm{G}_{2}]
by the multiplicatively closed subset generated by
z_{1,1}
and
z_{2,2}
.
∎

Appendix E
Computation of a Primitive Element for
F_{\mathrm{alg}}

In the last part of the appendix we explain how one can compute a primitive element for the algebraic extension
F_{\mathrm{alg}}
of
F
corresponding under the Galois correspondence to the connected component of the differential Galois group of the completely reducible part (cf. Definition
12.2
).

Proposition E.1
.

We can compute a primitive element

p\in\left(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q\right)^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}}

for the algebraic extension
F_{\mathrm{alg}}
of
F
.

Proof.

Since

E_{\rm red}^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}\,=\,\Frac(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q)^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}

is an algebraic extension of
F
and every element that is algebraic over
F
lies in the Picard-Vessiot ring
F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q
, we have that

\Frac(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q)^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}\,=\,(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q)^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}.

Indeed, if an element
a\in\Frac(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q)
is algebraic over
F
, then its orbit is finite and so the
C
-vector space spanned by the elements in the orbit is finite dimensional.
It follows from
[
vdPS03
, Corollary 1.38]
that
a
lies in
F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q
. Thus we have to compute generators of the invariant ring

(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q)^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}.

Consider the
n_{I^{\prime\prime}}^{2}
-dimensional vector space

\mathcal{V}\,=\,\overline{F}^{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}\times n_{I^{\prime\prime}}}=\mathbb{A}^{n_{I^{\prime\prime}}^{2}}(\overline{F})

over the algebraic closure
\overline{F}
of
F
and let

H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(\overline{F})\to\mathrm{GL}(\mathcal{V}),\quad g\mapsto(v\mapsto vg)

be the respective rational representation of
H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(\overline{F})
.
Let
(Q)
be the ideal in
\overline{F}[X,\det(X)^{-1}]
generated by
Q\lhd F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]
and consider the variety

\mathcal{U}\,=\,\{v\in\mathcal{V}\mid f(v)=0\ \text{for all}\,f\in(Q)\}\,.

The stability of
Q
under
H_{\rm red}(C)
implies stability of

Q
under
H_{\rm red}(\overline{F})
and so
(Q)
is stable under
H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(\overline{F})
. Thus
\mathcal{U}
is an affine variety stable under
H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(\overline{F})
. We obtain an
H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(\overline{F})
-equivariant embedding

i\colon\mathcal{U}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{V}

and so an
H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(\overline{F})
-equivariant surjective ring homomorphism

i^{*}\colon\overline{F}[\mathcal{X}]\to\overline{F}[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/(Q),\ \mathcal{X}_{i,j}\mapsto X_{i,j}+(Q),

where
\overline{F}[\mathcal{X}]
is the coordinate ring of
\mathcal{V}
with
\mathcal{X}
an
(n_{I^{\prime\prime}}\times n_{I^{\prime\prime}})
matrix whose entries
\mathcal{X}_{i,j}
are indeterminates over
\overline{F}
.
According to
[
DK15
, Corollary 2.2.9]
(recall that
H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(\overline{F})
is reductive) we have

i^{*}(\overline{F}[\mathcal{X}]^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(\overline{F})})\,=\,(\overline{F}[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/(Q))^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(\overline{F})}.

Since
\overline{F}[H_{\rm red}^{\circ}]\cong\overline{F}\otimes_{C}C[H_{\rm red}^{\circ}]
, we conclude that

\overline{F}[\mathcal{X}]^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(\overline{F})}\,=\,\overline{F}[\mathcal{X}]^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}

and that

(\overline{F}[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/(Q))^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(\overline{F})}\,=\,(\overline{F}[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/(Q))^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}.

Thus, we obtain

i^{*}(\overline{F}[\mathcal{X}]^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)})\,=\,(\overline{F}[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/(Q))^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}.

The multiplication maps

\displaystyle\mu_{1}\colon\overline{F}\otimes_{F}F[\mathcal{X}]\to\overline{F}[\mathcal{X}],\ f\otimes h_{1}\mapsto f\,h_{1}

and

\displaystyle\mu_{2}\colon\overline{F}\otimes_{F}F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q\to\overline{F}[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/(Q),\ f\otimes h_{2}\mapsto f\,h_{2}

are
H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)
-equivariant isomorphisms. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\overline{F}\otimes_{F}F[\mathcal{X}]^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}\,\cong\,(\overline{F}\otimes_{F}F[\mathcal{X}])^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}\,\underset{{\rm via}\,\mu_{1}}{\cong}\,\overline{F}[\mathcal{X}]^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}

and

\displaystyle\overline{F}\otimes_{F}(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q)^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}\,\cong\,(\overline{F}\otimes_{F}F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q)^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}

\displaystyle\underset{{\rm via}\,\mu_{2}}{\cong}\,(\overline{F}[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/(Q))^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}.

Combining this with
i^{*}
we obtain a surjective ring homomorphism

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\overline{F}\otimes_{F}F[\mathcal{X}]^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}&\!\to&\!\overline{F}\otimes_{F}(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q)^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)},\\[1.99997pt]
f\otimes h&\!\mapsto&\!f\otimes\widehat{\rho}(h)\,,\end{array}

where

\widehat{\rho}\colon F[\mathcal{X}]\to F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q,\ \mathcal{X}_{i,j}\mapsto X_{i,j}+Q.

We conclude that
\widehat{\rho}
restricts to a surjective ring homomorphism

\rho\colon F[\mathcal{X}]^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}\to(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q)^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)},\ \mathcal{X}_{i,j}\mapsto X_{i,j}+Q.

Moreover, we have that

F[\mathcal{X}]^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}\cong F\otimes_{C}C[\mathcal{X}]^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}

and so a generating set of
C[\mathcal{X}]^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}
over
C
maps to a generating set of

(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q)^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}

over
F
.
We are going to compute a generating set of
C[\mathcal{X}]^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}
using
[
DK15
, Algorithm 4.1.9]
.
To this end we need generators of the defining ideal of
H_{\rm red}^{\circ}
. We compute with Gröbner basis methods a minimal primary decomposition of the defining ideal of
H_{\rm red}
and determine by evaluating the defining polynomials of the components at the identity matrix the defining ideal of
H_{\rm red}^{\circ}
. We can compute now with
[
DK15
, Algorithm 4.1.9]
generators of
C[\mathcal{X}]^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}
and obtain generators of
(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q)^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}
by applying
\rho
to them.
Now one needs to find an
F
-linear combination

p\in F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q

of the generators of
(F[X,\det(X)^{-1}]/Q)^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}(C)}
such that its minimal polynomial over
F
has degree equal to the order of
H_{\rm red}/H_{\rm red}^{\circ}
(the number of components in the minimal primary decomposition).
Note that a generic choice of such a linear combination has the desired property.
Then
p
is a primitive element generating the algebraic extension
E_{\rm red}^{H_{\rm red}^{\circ}}=F_{\mathrm{alg}}
of
F
.
∎
